1.3 Penalties for non-patients; cultivation; paraphernalia; public consumption; patient-allowed forms

Possession penalties — §28-416, opened directly. Source for all quotations in this subsection: https://nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=28-416 — accessed 2026-10-30.

  • Verbatim — manufacture/distribution etc.:

    "(1) Except as authorized by the Uniform Controlled Substances Act, it shall be unlawful for any person knowingly or intentionally: (a) To manufacture, distribute, deliver, dispense, or possess with intent to manufacture, distribute, deliver, or dispense a controlled substance; or (b) to create, distribute, or possess with intent to distribute a counterfeit controlled substance."

  • Verbatim — distribution penalties (general):

    "(2) Except as provided in subsections (4), (5), (7), (8), (9), (10), and (11) of this section, any person who violates subsection (1) of this section with respect to: (a) A controlled substance classified in Schedule I, II, or III of section 28-405 which is an exceptionally hazardous drug shall be guilty of a Class II felony; (b) any other controlled substance classified in Schedule I, II, or III of section 28-405 shall be guilty of a Class IIA felony; or (c) a controlled substance classified in Schedule IV or V of section 28-405 shall be guilty of a Class IIIA felony."

  • Verbatim — simple possession tiers for marijuana:

    "(12) Any person knowingly or intentionally possessing marijuana weighing more than one ounce but not more than one pound shall be guilty of a Class III misdemeanor." "(13) Any person knowingly or intentionally possessing marijuana weighing more than one pound shall be guilty of a Class IV felony." "(14) Except as provided in section 28-1701, any person knowingly or intentionally possessing marijuana weighing one ounce or less or any substance containing a quantifiable amount of the substances, chemicals, or compounds described, defined, or delineated in subdivision (c)(28) of Schedule I of section 28-405 shall: (a) For the first offense, be guilty of an infraction, receive a citation, be fined three hundred dollars, and be assigned to attend a course as prescribed in section 29-433 if the judge determines that attending such course is in the best interest of the individual defendant; (b) For the second offense, be guilty of a Class IV misdemeanor, receive a citation, and be fined four hundred dollars and may be imprisoned not to exceed five days; and (c) For the third and all subsequent offenses, be guilty of a Class IIIA misdemeanor, receive a citation, be fined five hundred dollars, and be imprisoned not to exceed seven days." Note on numbering: older secondary sources cite these tiers as §28-416(11)/(12)/(13); the current opened text numbers them (12)/(13)/(14). Cite the current numbering.

  • Prior-offence rule: the tier above IS the prior-offence rule — first ≤1 oz offence is an infraction ($300), second is a Class IV misdemeanor ($400, up to 5 days), third+ is a Class IIIA misdemeanor ($500, up to 7 days). There is no separate "lookback period" stated in the subsection as opened — Verify whether any lookback / expungement-of-prior-infraction rule exists elsewhere (see also Part 12 cross-reference; do not assume one).
  • Enhancements (verbatim, summarised): §28-416(4) raises the penalty by one classification for an adult who manufactures/distributes/delivers/dispenses (or possesses with intent) to a person under 18, within 1,000 feet of a school/college/university/playground, or within 100 feet of a youth centre, public swimming pool or video-arcade facility; §28-416(17) raises it by one classification if the person possessed a firearm while violating subsection (1) or the violation directly/proximately caused death or serious bodily injury, capped at a Class IB felony. Source: https://nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=28-416 — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Cultivation penalties: cultivation is "manufacture" under §28-416(1)(a) and is therefore punished under §28-416(2) (Class IIA felony for a non-exceptionally-hazardous Schedule I substance, subject to enhancements) unless the cultivator is a Commission licensee acting within the Regulation Act. No separate plant-count cultivation penalty tier for marijuana was located in §28-416 as opened — Verify against a full controlled-substances penalty survey (e.g. any cultivation-specific provision outside §28-416). Patient/caregiver cultivation is not authorised at all (see §1.4), so any patient grow is unlicensed manufacture under this section.
  • Paraphernalia: §28-441 — use or possession with intent to use drug paraphernalia to introduce a controlled substance is an infraction (see verbatim text in §1.2C). Source: https://nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=28-441 — accessed 2026-10-30. Note: "cannabis accessories" for qualified patients/caregivers are protected under §71-24,105 (see §1.2A); the §28-441 carve-out for §§28-472/28-1701 is noted but its interaction with patient accessories was not resolved from primary text in this run — Verify.
  • Public consumption: No Nebraska statute or regulation provision specifically criminalising or permitting public consumption of medical cannabis was located in this run. Initiative 437 protects patient "use" without stating a location limit in §71-24,105 as opened. Unavailable/Verify — do not state that public use is legal or illegal; check (a) Title 238 dispensary/consumption provisions, (b) any local ordinances (Part 3), and (c) smoke-free / public-place laws before advising. The absence of a located provision is not a finding that none exists.

Patient-allowed forms — the statute/regulation tension (read carefully). - Under Initiative 437 as codified (§§71-24,104–71-24,105): NO form restriction. "Cannabis" includes "marijuana, hashish, and concentrated cannabis" and "cannabis accessories" include equipment for "vaporizing" and "ingesting, inhaling, or otherwise introducing cannabis into the human body." A qualified patient may therefore, on the statute's face, possess up to 5 oz in any form, including flower. Sources: https://nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=71-24,104 — accessed 2026-10-30; https://nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=71-24,105 — accessed 2026-10-30. - Under Commission regulations (what licensed establishments may sell — NOT what patients may possess): only the following are permitted, and the following are prohibited (secondary summary of the emergency regulations approved 2–5 Sept 2025, corroborated by multiple secondary reports; Verify each line against the final Title 238 text before republication as regulation text): - Permitted: "(i) Oral tablets, with or without flavored coating, capsules, or tinctures; (ii) Gels, oils, creams, or other topical preparation; (iii) Suppositories; (iv) [transdermal patches — listed in other summaries]; (v) Liquids or oils for administration using a nebulizer or inhaler." Source: https://norml.org/laws/medical-laws/nebraska-medical-marijuana-law/ — accessed 2026-10-30. - Prohibited: "(i) Raw plant material; (ii) Any product administered by smoking, combustion, or vaping; (iii) Any product containing artificial flavoring, natural flavoring, or coloring; (iv) A food or drink that has cannabis baked, mixed, or otherwise infused into it; (v) Any product that includes a synthetic or converted cannabinoid; (vi) Any product that exceeds a potency of 60% delta-9-THC or a dose of 40 mg per dose; (vii) Any product or packaging which would reasonably cause confusion as to whether the cannabis product is a food product; (viii) Any product in the shape of a human, animal, marijuana leaf; or (ix) Any tobacco, alcohol, or other intoxicating substance; or (x) Any product that is not a medical cannabis or a medical cannabis product registered with the Commission." Source: same NORML summary — accessed 2026-10-30. Corroboration: "they'll be forbidden from selling cannabis flower or edibles," https://mjbizdaily.com/news/nebraska-moves-to-ban-cannabis-flowers-and-edibles-limit-licenses/406659/ — accessed 2026-10-30; "Patients are forbidden from obtaining or possessing raw cannabis" (MJBizDaily's characterisation — this overstates the statute; the accurate statement is that establishments are forbidden from selling it, while patient possession under 437 is not form-limited — see the Commission's own clarification below). - Commission clarification (secondarily reported, Commission members speaking): "The temporary regulations prohibit the legal in-state selling, for example, of medical cannabis flower and products that could be smoked or vaped, as well as edibles or other products containing flavoring. However, Nebraska patients and caregivers with a health care practitioner's recommendation could still legally possess up to 5 ounces of such products." And: "The law that was passed by the residents of Nebraska did not limit what the use of that plant was." Source: https://www.omahadailyrecord.com/content/medical-cannabis-liquor-control-commissions-seek-combine-resources-frustrations-flare — accessed 2026-10-30. - Practical consequence: a patient may lawfully possess flower under 437 but cannot lawfully buy it from any Nebraska licensee under the regulations, and no dispensary is open in any event — so the lawful in-state supply of any form is currently nil. This is the single most important form-rule point for patients. - THC / purchase caps by regulation (secondary — Verify): - Dispensary sale cap reported as "no more than five (5) grams of delta-9-THC to the same qualified patient … in a ninety (90) day period" (NORML summary, https://norml.org/laws/medical-laws/nebraska-medical-marijuana-law/ — accessed 2026-10-30) but as "5 ounces … in 30 days, of which no more than 5 grams can be delta-9 THC … from the same dispensary" in another secondary report (https://www.marijuanamoment.net/nebraska-officials-consider-changes-to-medical-marijuana-rules/ — accessed 2026-10-30). Conflict flagged: 90-day vs 30-day period — Verify against Title 238 before citing either. The 5-gram delta-9 figure itself is consistent across both. - Potency/dose cap reported as 60% delta-9 THC potency / 40 mg per dose (NORML summary above — Verify). - Cultivator plant cap reported as 1,250 flowering plants at one time (multiple secondary reports, e.g. https://www.omahadailyrecord.com/content/nebraska’s-first-medical-cannabis-cultivators-offered-licenses-legal-threats-loom — accessed 2026-10-30) — Verify in Title 238. - LB 677's proposed 2-oz limit and smoking ban, and LB 483's pills/tinctures-only limit, are dead bills and must not be cited as caps in force (see §1.9).

1.4 Medical programme: qualifying, practitioner, 5-ounce limit, caregivers, registration/card, home cultivation, out-of-state patients

  • Qualifying conditions: NONE listed in law. Qualification is the open practitioner-judgment standard in §71-24,104(7) (verbatim in §1.2A): "the potential benefits of cannabis outweigh the potential harms for the alleviation of a patient's medical condition, its symptoms, or side effects of the condition's treatment." No condition is named and none is excluded by the statute. Source: https://nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=71-24,104 — accessed 2026-10-30. LB 677's proposed 15-condition list is dead (see §1.9) and must not be presented as the qualifying list.
  • Practitioner recommendation rules: practitioner = "a physician, an osteopathic physician, a physician assistant, or a nurse practitioner licensed under the Uniform Credentialing Act or who is licensed in any state and practicing in compliance with the Uniform Credentialing Act" (§71-24,104(5)). The recommendation must be a "valid signed and dated declaration" in the practitioner's professional judgment (benefits outweigh harms), valid for two years or a shorter period specified by the practitioner (§71-24,104(7)). No bona-fide-relationship duration, no in-person-visit rule, and no state practitioner registration is stated in §§71-24,104–71-24,105 as opened. Source: https://nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=71-24,104 — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Regulation overlay (Verify): secondary reporting states the permanent regulations establish a "Recommending Health Care Practitioner Directory" and require patients who want to use Nebraska-licensed dispensaries to go through an in-state provider on that directory. Source: https://www.marijuanamoment.net/nebraska-officials-consider-changes-to-medical-marijuana-rules/ — accessed 2026-10-30. Verify — if true, this narrows the statute's "licensed in any state" language for dispensary access; the directory's existence, contents, and legal effect were not confirmed from the regulation text in this run.
  • Practitioner protection gap: LB 933 (immunity for practitioners "solely" for issuing a recommendation or stating a professional opinion) is dead (see §1.9). No practitioner immunity provision in the initiatives themselves was located in §§71-24,104–71-24,105 as opened. Secondary reporting consistently states physicians are deterred by liability/licensing risk and "essentially no Nebraska physician has issued a patient recommendation" (secondary, mid-2026) — Verify as a factual claim; it is not a legal rule. Sources: https://www.cannbus.org/lsum/ne_lsum.html — accessed 2026-10-30; https://mjbizdaily.com/news/first-nebraska-medical-cannabis-harvest-could-come-by-fall-but-market-launch-uncertain/615467/ — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • 5-ounce possession limit: "Allowable amount of cannabis means up to five ounces of cannabis," excluding the weight of other ingredients in topical/oral administrations, food, drink or other preparations (§71-24,104(1)). This is a possession limit, not a purchase limit; the regulation's dispensary sale cap (5 g delta-9 THC per period — period disputed, see §1.3) is a separate, narrower constraint on sales. Source: https://nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=71-24,104 — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Caregivers: as defined in §71-24,104(4) (verbatim in §1.2A): 21+ designee by signed affidavit (adult patient not under guardianship); parent/legal guardian or their sworn-affidavit designee (minor or guardianship patient); or a designated health care facility / home health agency that has agreed in writing. Caregivers may possess/acquire/deliver the allowable amount on the patient's behalf (§71-24,105(1)(b)). No caregiver registry, card, fee, or numerical limit on patients-per-caregiver is stated in the Act as opened — Verify whether Title 238 adds any caregiver registration (none was located in this run). Sources: https://nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=71-24,104 — accessed 2026-10-30; https://nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=71-24,105 — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Patient registration / ID card: NONE in law, and none operating. The initiatives create no registry and no card; the patient's proof is the practitioner's written recommendation itself. LB 1235 as introduced would have created a patient registry; a committee amendment removed it before passage (secondarily reported — Verify against LB 1235's amendment history). No patient count therefore exists (see also Part 5/6 cross-reference: patient statistics are Unavailable because there is no registry to count). Sources: https://www.wethepeopleoftheusofa.com/civics-101-nebraska-medical-cannabis-dates.html — accessed 2026-10-30 ("There is no Nebraska medical cannabis registry. There is no card."); https://drugpolicywatch.info/states/nebraska.html — accessed 2026-10-30 ("an amendment stripped controversial language that would have curtailed patient protections").
  • Home cultivation: NOT allowed. Neither initiative authorises patient or caregiver cultivation; cultivation is reserved to Commission-licensed cultivators, and unlicensed cultivation is "manufacture" under §28-416(1)(a). Sources: §71-24,105 (no cultivation right stated), https://nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=71-24,105 — accessed 2026-10-30; §28-416, https://nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=28-416 — accessed 2026-10-30; secondary confirmation: "Nebraska's medical cannabis law does not permit home cultivation," https://www.cannbus.org/lsum/ne_lsum.html — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Out-of-state patients: The Patient Protection Act's practitioner definition expressly includes a practitioner "licensed in any state and practicing in compliance with the Uniform Credentialing Act" (§71-24,104(5)), but the Act as opened states no out-of-state patient reciprocity, no non-resident card recognition, and no rule on whether a non-resident with a Nebraska-compliant recommendation qualifies. Unavailable/Verify — do not state that out-of-state patients are covered or excluded; check Title 238's dispensary-access / directory rules (see the directory flag above) and any DHHS/Commission guidance. The Regulation Act's "qualified patients" language (§71-24,107(3)) cross-refers to "any law enacted contemporaneously … or at any time thereafter" — i.e. back to Initiative 437 — and adds no residency rule on its face. Source: https://nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=71-24,107 — accessed 2026-10-30.

1.5 Hemp and intoxicating hemp

Definition and measurement (state law, current). Hemp = Cannabis sativa L. and any part/derivative/extract/cannabinoid/isomer/acid/salt with delta-9 THC ≤0.3% on a dry-weight basis; hemp is an agricultural commodity and "shall not be considered a controlled substance under the Uniform Controlled Substances Act" (§2-503, verbatim in §1.2D). Source: https://nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=2-503 — accessed 2026-10-30. Measurement is therefore delta-9 only under current Nebraska statute — total THC (THCa × 0.877 + delta-9) is NOT the state test today. That is precisely what the incoming federal definition changes (see below). The Act also incorporates 7 C.F.R. 990.1 concepts ("acceptable hemp THC level," measurement of uncertainty) as of the dates stated in §2-503 — Verify the current incorporation dates on the opened page.

Delta-8, delta-9, THCA and other hemp-derived THC — the core legal conflict (state law unresolved by statute or court in this run). - Textual argument for legality: because §2-503 covers "derivatives, extracts, cannabinoids, isomers" with ≤0.3% delta-9, a converted cannabinoid such as delta-8 that meets the delta-9 test falls within the literal hemp definition, and §28-401(c) provides "Marijuana does not include hemp." Sources: https://nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=2-503 — accessed 2026-10-30; §28-401 via search rendering — accessed 2026-10-30 (Verify on the opened page). - Attorney General's contrary position (enforcement position, NOT statute): AG Hilgers treats delta-8 and other converted/synthetic cannabinoids as unlawful — as Schedule I tetrahydrocannabinols under §28-405, and/or as mislabelled marijuana / deceptive products when delta-9 exceeds 0.3% or labels misstate content. No Nebraska court decision definitively resolving the delta-8 question was located in this run — Verify: do not cite any case as deciding delta-8's status. Secondary analysis agrees: "Which law wins? Nebraska courts have not definitively resolved that question." Source: https://legalclarity.org/is-delta-8-legal-in-nebraska/ — accessed 2026-10-30. - Practical status as of 30 Oct 2026: contested / high-enforcement-risk, not banned by statute. LB 316 (the statutory ban) is dead. Retail sale continues in the sense that no statute prohibits it, but the AG is actively suing sellers (see below) and the Governor has directed agency action (EO 26-02). Any statement that "delta-8 is legal in Nebraska" without the enforcement qualification is misleading; any statement that "delta-8 is illegal in Nebraska" without noting LB 316's failure and the absence of a deciding court ruling is also misleading. Both halves must be stated together.

Attorney General lawsuits and positions (case names/numbers/outcomes — partial; full docket inventory is Unavailable in this run). - What the AG's own release establishes (opened directly): expansion to 104 Omaha retail locations/franchised stores; 2025 test purchases from stores owned by 35 companies; "[e]ach store investigated had at least one product marketed as hemp, which actually had a delta-9 concentration over the legal limit, making it marijuana, and had labels that incorrectly identified the content of the products"; letters demanding cessation of all THC-containing-product sales to Nebraska consumers and a signed Assurance of Voluntary Compliance, failing which the office "will initiate litigation and will seek penalties and fees to the fullest extent allowed by law"; referral of marijuana-sale evidence to the Douglas County Attorney for criminal-charge consideration; named products in the release include "Kushy Exotic THCA Flower, Flying Horse Vape-9G, and Road Trip Gummies"; tobacco-licence risk flagged for stores holding tobacco licences. Source: https://ago.nebraska.gov/news/announcement-new-developments-delta-8-enforcement-efforts — accessed 2026-10-30. - Counts: "To date, the Attorney General's Office has filed 15 lawsuits alleging violations of the Consumer Protection Act (Unfairness), Consumer Protection Act (Deception), and Nebraska Pure Food Act. … 12 out of 15 of the lawsuits have been settled. The remaining three are being actively litigated." (AG release, same URL — accessed 2026-10-30.) Later secondary total (29 Sept 2025): 16 lawsuits, 12 settlements, letters to companies operating ~300 stores. Source: https://horganlawfirm.com/delta-8-thc-and-cbd-in-nebraska-a-guide-for-businesses/ — accessed 2026-10-30. Verify / Unavailable: individual case names, docket numbers, courts, and settlement terms were NOT located in this run — the AG release does not name the 15 suits, and no docket search was completed. Do not invent case captions. A named out-of-state suit secondarily reported — against Savage Enterprises (California manufacturer/distributor, August 2025) — is Verify (source: https://www.atlrx.com/blogs/delta-8/is-delta-8-legal-in-nebraska/ — accessed 2026-10-30, secondary only). - Theories and penalties (secondary — Verify against the Consumer Protection Act / Pure Food Act texts and any filed complaint): Consumer Protection Act unfairness + deception and Nebraska Pure Food Act; civil penalties reported as up to $2,000 per violation (one source) or up to $4,000 per sale (another source) — conflict flagged, Verify; settlement stipulated penalties reported as $18,000–$90,000 on breach. Sources: https://horganlawfirm.com/delta-8-thc-and-cbd-in-nebraska-a-guide-for-businesses/ — accessed 2026-10-30; https://www.atlrx.com/blogs/delta-8/is-delta-8-legal-in-nebraska/ — accessed 2026-10-30. The AG's March 2025 Omaha-area action is secondarily described as letters to "more than 100 Omaha-area retailers" with "over 90% of products tested failed labeling accuracy or compliance checks" — Verify against the AG's March 2025 release (not opened in this run). Source: https://www.cannabisregulations.ai/cannabis-and-hemp-regulations-compliance-ai-blog/state-ags-udap-intoxicating-hemp-enforcement-2025 — accessed 2026-10-30. - THCA flower specifically: the AG's position, as shown in its own release, is that THCA flower marketed as hemp but testing over the delta-9 limit "is marijuana" and that sale of marijuana is a felony referred for prosecution. Whether THCA flower that tests ≤0.3% delta-9 (pre-decarboxylation) is lawful hemp under §2-503 is the same unresolved delta-8-style question — Verify; no court ruling located. Source: AG release above — accessed 2026-10-30. - CBD: hemp-derived CBD meeting the §2-503 delta-9 test remains lawful on the statute's face; no CBD ban was located. Source: §2-503, https://nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=2-503 — accessed 2026-10-30; secondary agreement: https://horganlawfirm.com/delta-8-thc-and-cbd-in-nebraska-a-guide-for-businesses/ — accessed 2026-10-30.

Any law restricting these products (enacted vs proposed vs executive). - Enacted statute restricting intoxicating hemp products: NONE located — LB 316 is dead (see §1.9). This is the correct headline: Nebraska has no enacted delta-8/THCA ban as of 30 Oct 2026. - Executive action: EO 26-02 (26 Jan 2026), "Protecting Nebraskans from Intoxicating Synthetic THC Products" — directs the Departments of Agriculture, Banking and Finance, and Revenue to review existing law/rules and, if necessary, draft new rules so products intended for human consumption do not contain intoxicating synthetic THC. An executive order directing review/drafting is NOT itself a product ban. Source: http://govdocs.nebraska.gov/docs/pilot/pubs/eoindex.html — accessed 2026-10-30; secondary summary: https://horganlawfirm.com/delta-8-thc-and-cbd-in-nebraska-a-guide-for-businesses/ — accessed 2026-10-30. - Proposed rule (NOT final): Department of Agriculture draft 19 NAC 5 (proposed 7 May 2026; hearing 18 June 2026) would treat food, beverages, gummies and tinctures containing any amount of THC (including THCA, delta-8, delta-9, HHC, THC-O) as adulterated under the Nebraska Pure Food Act, with an exception for products authorised under the medical cannabis Acts. It is stricter than the incoming federal 0.4 mg/container cap (zero vs 0.4 mg) and, as of the secondary updates in this run, has NOT been confirmed final — it still requires Department review, AG review, and Governor approval. Do not cite as law. Sources: https://horganlawfirm.com/delta-8-thc-and-cbd-in-nebraska-a-guide-for-businesses/ — accessed 2026-10-30; http://smokequant.com/news/nebraska-thc-food-rule — accessed 2026-10-30; https://gray-wowt-prod.gtv-cdn.com/2026/06/18/nebraska-dept-agriculture-proposes-ban-food-beverages-containing-any-amount-thc/ — accessed 2026-10-30. Verify current status with the Department of Agriculture / Secretary of State rulemaking register.

Minimum age for hemp-derived products: No Nebraska statute setting a minimum purchase age for delta-8 / hemp-derived THC products was located in this run. Secondary reporting agrees ("Nebraska has no state law setting a specific minimum age to buy Delta-8 THC, but virtually every retailer … requires buyers to be at least 21"). Source: https://legalclarity.org/how-old-do-you-have-to-be-to-buy-delta-8-in-nebraska/ — accessed 2026-10-30. Verify — retail practice (21+) is not law. (THC beverages and smoke/vape-shop age rules are Part 12 cross-references; no beverage-specific Nebraska statute was located — see below.)

Testing and labelling (hemp products): No Nebraska statute imposing a general testing/labelling regime for retail hemp-derived products was located in this run. The AG's enforcement theory is, in substance, that existing law already polices this: products that misstate THC content or exceed 0.3% delta-9 violate the Consumer Protection Act (deception/unfairness) and the Pure Food Act, and over-limit product is marijuana under §28-401. That is an enforcement theory, not a testing/labelling code. Verify whether any Department of Agriculture hemp rule (outside 19 NAC 5) imposes testing/labelling — none was located. Source for the enforcement framing: AG release, https://ago.nebraska.gov/news/announcement-new-developments-delta-8-enforcement-efforts — accessed 2026-10-30.

THC beverages: No Nebraska statute or final rule specifically regulating hemp-derived THC beverages was located in this run. Beverages fall within (a) the general §2-503 hemp definition while it lasts, (b) EO 26-02's "food, beverages, and other consumable products" review direction, and (c) draft 19 NAC 5's proposed "food … beverages" adulteration treatment (not final). They are therefore in the same contested position as other intoxicating hemp consumables, with no beverage-specific age, dose, or labelling rule located. Verify before any beverage-specific claim. Sources: EO index, http://govdocs.nebraska.gov/docs/pilot/pubs/eoindex.html — accessed 2026-10-30; draft-rule reporting, https://gray-wowt-prod.gtv-cdn.com/2026/06/18/nebraska-dept-agriculture-proposes-ban-food-beverages-containing-any-amount-thc/ — accessed 2026-10-30.

State response to the new federal hemp definition and the 12 Nov / 11 Dec 2026 dates (fixed baseline — do not re-state as Nebraska law). - Under the fixed baseline, PL 119-37 §781 takes effect 12 Nov 2026 but PL 119-103 §2019 limits it until 11 Dec 2026; after 11 Dec 2026 the full total-THC hemp definition and the 0.4 mg total THC per container cap apply. As of 30 Oct 2026 neither date has arrived, so Nebraska's current state definition (§2-503, delta-9 only) is still the state test and no Nebraska statute conforming to the federal total-THC definition was located. - Nebraska's anticipatory responses are executive/proposed only: EO 26-02 (Jan 2026) and draft 19 NAC 5 (May–June 2026), described secondarily as intended "to align Nebraska with the federal redefinition" — but draft 19 NAC 5 (zero THC) goes beyond the federal cap, so it is not a conforming implementation. Sources: https://gray-wowt-prod.gtv-cdn.com/2026/06/18/nebraska-dept-agriculture-proposes-ban-food-beverages-containing-any-amount-thc/ — accessed 2026-10-30; http://smokequant.com/news/nebraska-thc-food-rule — accessed 2026-10-30. - What happens on 12 Nov / 11 Dec 2026 in Nebraska is Verify: whether Nebraska will (a) leave §2-503 unamended (creating a state/federal conflict in which a product could be state-"hemp" but federally marijuana), (b) adopt a conforming total-THC definition by statute or rule, or (c) finalise 19 NAC 5's zero-THC food rule, was not determined by any official source in this run, and the Legislature does not reconvene until 6 January 2027 (110th Legislature, per secondary reporting — Verify the convening date). No Nebraska agency guidance on the federal dates was located. Do not present any of (a)–(c) as decided. The federal dates also interact with the USDA DEA-lab testing delay (31 Dec 2026) and the federal medical rider (through 11 Dec 2026) in the fixed baseline — those are federal, not Nebraska, actions.

1.6 Packaging, labelling, advertising, delivery, zoning and buffer rules (current or in regulation)

All items in this subsection rest on Title 238 regulation renderings and secondary summaries — Verify each against the final Title 238, Ch. 1 text before republication as regulation text. No packaging/labelling/advertising/delivery rule exists in Initiatives 437/438 themselves as codified (their texts as opened contain no such provisions).

  • Packaging / labelling (regulation, secondary summary): child-resistant, tamper-evident packaging; plain white or earth tones; no cartoons; six mandatory warning statements (contents of the six warnings Verify — not located). One regulation-related proposal would remove the requirement to label medical cannabis as a Schedule I controlled substance in light of federal rescheduling — proposed, not adopted. Sources: https://www.illinoislawyernow.com/2025/07/how-to-open-a-dispensary-in-nebraska-2025-26-guide-costs-rules-deadlines/ — accessed 2026-10-30 (secondary, dated by its URL slug July 2025 — treat as stale for 2026 amendments); https://www.marijuanamoment.net/nebraska-officials-consider-changes-to-medical-marijuana-rules/ — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Advertising (regulation, secondary summary): no health claims; no billboards within 500 feet of covered locations; no cartoon mascots. Source: https://www.illinoislawyernow.com/2025/07/how-to-open-a-dispensary-in-nebraska-2025-26-guide-costs-rules-deadlines/ — accessed 2026-10-30 (Verify — single secondary source, 2025 vintage).
  • Delivery: The Regulation Act authorises registered establishments to "deliver" (§71-24,107(6)–(7): entities that "possess, manufacture, distribute, deliver, or dispense"), but whether Title 238 permits patient home delivery, and on what terms, was not located in this run — Unavailable/Verify. Do not state that delivery is permitted or prohibited. Source for the statutory word: https://nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=71-24,107 — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Zoning / buffers (regulation): "Covered location" = school, licensed child-care business, church, hospital, or mental-health/substance-use treatment centre (Title 238 §02.10, quoted in §1.2F). The buffer is secondarily reported as 1,000 feet from covered locations for dispensaries, cultivators, manufacturers and transporters, measured in a straight line from the nearest property line of the covered location to the nearest perimeter wall of the licensed premises, with a limited exception for a covered location established after the establishment was operating. Sources: https://www.cannabisindustrylawyer.com/nebraska-dispensary-license-requirements-2026/ — accessed 2026-10-30; https://hempgazette.com/news/nebraska-cannabis-regulations-hg2503/ — accessed 2026-10-30. Verify the distance, the measurement rule, and whether it applies to all licence types or dispensaries only, in the final regulation text. Local zoning/moratorium/ban authority is Part 3 territory; the one local fact surfaced in this run — a Washington County planning/zoning reversal blocking KRL Med LLC, with the Commission renewing KRL's licence for six months on 13 April 2026 while it resolves the issue — is secondarily reported (https://northplattepost.com/posts/134d6f2b-785d-4ba2-bc07-12c0af590b2c — accessed 2026-10-30) and is Verify against Commission minutes / county records.
  • Dispensary geography (regulation): one dispensary per District Court judicial district (12 total) — see §0.3. This is a state cap, not a local zoning rule.

1.7 Seed-to-sale tracking system planned or used

  • The regulations define a "State inventory tracking system" (Title 238 §02.32 — definition truncated in the rendering located; full definition Verify). Source: https://mcc.nebraska.gov/sites/default/files/medical_cannabis/Revised%20-%20238%20NAC%201%20DRAFT.3.31.pdf — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • A proposed (not adopted) fee of $13,000 for participation in the state's seed-to-sale inventory tracking system, payable at each renewal, appears in the Commission's August 2026 proposed fee schedule. Sources: https://couriernebraska.com/news/nebraska-medical-cannabis-commission-moving-forward-with-application-licensing-fees/ — accessed 2026-10-30; https://cannacon.org/cannabis-legislation-licensing-north-dakota-south-dakota-nebraska-kansas/ — accessed 2026-10-30. Proposed only — do not cite as a fee in force.
  • No vendor, no live system, no track-and-trace mandate detail was confirmed in this run: with no manufacturer or dispensary licensed and no sale yet made, there is no operating seed-to-sale chain to describe. Whether the system is Metrc, BioTrack, or another vendor, and what events must be recorded, is Unavailable/Verify — check the final Title 238 text and any Commission procurement record. Do not name a vendor.

1.8 Employment and drug-testing protections for patients

  • No employment or drug-testing protection for patients exists in Initiatives 437/438 as codified. The texts opened (§§71-24,104–71-24,105) contain no employer, hiring, firing, accommodation, or drug-testing provision. Secondary analysis agrees: "Neither ballot measure addresses employer rights or obligations with respect to medical cannabis or qualified patients." Source: https://www.lexblog.com/2024/11/07/marijuana-ballot-initiatives-approved-in-nebraska-defeated-in-three-other-states/ — accessed 2026-10-30 (secondary summary of the measures — Verify by the full Act texts, which were opened only for §§71-24,104/105/107/109 in this run).
  • Nebraska Fair Employment Practice Act (NFEPA) interaction — Verify, do not state as settled: the same secondary summary notes NFEPA "does not protect current users of illegal drugs, but illegal drug use under the law does not include 'the use of a drug taken under supervision by a licensed health care professional…or any other use authorized by…other provisions of state law.'" Whether a qualified patient's Initiative 437 use falls within that exclusion, and whether any employer drug-testing statute or case law addresses medical cannabis, was not confirmed from primary sources in this run. Source: same LexBlog summary — accessed 2026-10-30. Verify against the NFEPA text and any DHHS / Department of Labor guidance before advising any patient or employer.
  • Practitioner-side protection is addressed in §1.4 (LB 933 dead). Licensee-employee drug-testing / background-check rules are Part 11 territory; the regulation's fingerprinting requirement for applicants (see §1.2F) is noted there by cross-reference only.

The Legislature is out of session; the 110th Legislature convenes 6 January 2027 (secondary — Verify the convening date). Whether LB 316, LB 677, LB 933, or a successor hemp/medical bill is reintroduced in 2027 is unknown — do not predict it. A bill "indefinitely postponed" in Nebraska is dead for that session but may be reintroduced in a new session; it is not "pending." Caveat: the full 82-result "marijuana" bill search was not exhaustively reviewed (see §1.2E), so a minor pending bill (e.g. a scheduling or tax bill touching §28-405/§28-416 such as LB 795) cannot be ruled out — Verify by completing that search before claiming "no pending bill at all."

Proposed (not law) — do not cite as law: - Commission fee-schedule / Title 238 amendments (hearing 15 Oct 2026; final adoption expected 2027) — see §§0.4, 1.2F. - Department of Agriculture draft 19 NAC 5 (THC food/beverage adulteration; hearing 18 June 2026; not confirmed final) — see §1.5. - Any 2027 reintroduction of LB 316 / LB 677 / LB 933 — not yet introduced as of 30 Oct 2026.

Litigation — every lawsuit over the initiatives, signatures, or Commission regulations located

  1. Kuehn v. Evnen (petition-signature challenge) — DECIDED for the initiatives. - Filed September 2024 by former State Senator John Kuehn (Heartwell) in Lancaster County District Court, challenging the legal sufficiency of the 437/438 petitions (circulator misconduct / notarial malfeasance); Secretary of State Bob Evnen (who had certified the petitions) and AG Hilgers later joined the challenge in substance. District Judge Susan Strong ruled 26 November 2024 for sufficiency and 12 December 2024 refused to block the Governor's proclamation. Nebraska Supreme Court, S-24-901, 322 Neb. 161 (4 Sept 2026), 7-0 (Funke, C.J.), affirmed. Sources: https://law.Justia.com/cases/nebraska/supreme-court/2026/ — accessed 2026-10-30 (citation/docket); https://www.marijuanamoment.net/nebraska-supreme-court-upholds-medical-marijuana-ballot-initiatives-rejecting-legal-challenge/ — accessed 2026-10-30; https://www.cannabisbusinesstimes.com/top-stories/news/15710193/nebraska-medical-marijuana-legalization-measures-face-another-lawsuit — accessed 2026-10-30. Verify the opinion text and the district-court case number on the courts' own sites — the district docket number was not located in this run. - Signature arithmetic (secondary): each petition needed 86,499 valid signatures + 38-county distribution; the Secretary of State initially verified 89,962 (437) and 89,856 (438); the district court found roughly 1,500 signatures lost their presumption of validity, leaving totals still above the threshold. Source: https://kindgreenbuds.com/news/nebraskasupremecourt-unanimously-upholds-voter-approved-medical-marijuana-laws/ — accessed 2026-10-30. Verify against the opinion.
  2. In September 2026 the Supreme Court "unanimously affirmed the dismissal of another Kuehn lawsuit trying to challenge the constitutionality of the same medical cannabis laws." Sources: https://www.cannabisbusinesstimes.com/top-stories/news/15710193/nebraska-medical-marijuana-legalization-measures-face-another-lawsuit — accessed 2026-10-30; https://couriernebraska.com/news/politics/nebraskas-fall-2026-ballot-lawsuits/ — accessed 2026-10-30. No filed suit challenging the cultivator licences was located in this run. Source: https://www.omahadailyrecord.com/content/nebraska’s-first-medical-cannabis-cultivators-offered-licenses-legal-threats-loom — accessed 2026-10-30. Verify — threat ≠ filed case.

Do not present the AG's consumer-protection suits as "lawsuits over the initiatives" — they are hemp-enforcement suits under pre-existing law, listed here for completeness because they are the only other cannabis-related litigation located.

Wagner v. Evnen; do not cite without verification). Part 2 — Taxes

PART 2 — TAXES

2.0 Bottom line up front

  • No cannabis-specific excise tax is in force in Nebraska. No enacted statute imposes a medical-cannabis excise, a cultivation tax, or a hemp-product excise.
  • No medical-cannabis sales have occurred, so official medical-cannabis tax revenue is $0 — not "unreported", but zero because there is nothing to tax yet. There is no licensed dispensary and no licensed product manufacturer (Part 4, §4.1).
  • The sales-tax treatment of future dispensary sales is UNRESOLVED — flagged Verify. Two secondary sources point in opposite directions, and no Nebraska Department of Revenue guidance specific to medical cannabis was located. Do not publish a single definitive sales-tax rate for medical cannabis until the Department of Revenue or the Commission publishes one. The reasoning and both readings are set out in §2.1.
  • Licence fees are the only money the programme is currently designed to collect, and even those are not yet finally collectible. LB 1235 (enacted 2026) caps each application / registration / renewal fee at $50,000 and authorises the Commission to set a fee schedule by rule. The Commission's draft fee schedule (August 2026) has not completed Attorney General and Governor review and was, as of the Commission's 14 September 2026 meeting, the stated reason manufacturers and dispensaries were not being licensed (see §2.2, §4.5).
  • Nebraska has NO 280E-decoupling statute. No statute was found that adds back, or expressly allows, cannabis business expenses for Nebraska income tax. Because Nebraska income tax starts from federal adjusted gross income (Neb. Rev. Stat. §§ 77-2715, 77-2716), Nebraska in practice follows the federal result — but no Department of Revenue guidance confirming entity-level treatment was located, so this is flagged Verify in §2.6.
  • The old Marijuana and Controlled Substances Tax ($100 per ounce) still exists on the statute book, but it is an illicit-market stamp tax, not a tax on the licensed medical programme. Its interaction with lawful medical possession was the subject of (unenacted) bills that would have expressly removed lawful medical cannabis from it (§2.4).

2.1 Medical cannabis — sales tax and excise: what is enacted, what is not, and what is Verify

Enacted position — no medical-cannabis tax statute. - Initiatives 437 and 438 as codified (Neb. Rev. Stat. §§ 71-24,103–71-24,111) contain no sales-tax rate, no excise rate, and no revenue-distribution formula for medical cannabis. The Commission's statutory duties in § 71-24,111 include, verbatim in relevant part: "(1) No later than July 1, 2025, establish criteria to accept or deny applications for registrations, including adopting, promulgating, and enforcing reasonable rules, regulations, and eligibility standards for such registrations; (2) No later than October 1, 2025, begin granting registrations to applicants that meet eligibility standards and other requirements established by the commission" — Source: https://www.nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=71-24,111&print=true — accessed 2026-10-30. No tax provision appears in that section. - LB 1235 as enacted (see §2.2) contains no sales-tax or excise provision. Its introduced version did contain tax provisions (amending §§ 77-2701.48, 77-2704.09 and 77-4303, "to provide for taxation of sales of medical cannabis and distribution of the revenue; to remove medical cannabis from the marijuana and controlled substances tax") — Source: https://legiscan.com/NE/text/LB1235/id/3326568/Nebraska-2025-LB1235-Introduced.pdf — accessed 2026-10-30. Those tax provisions were stripped before passage: the slip law as approved by the Governor on 7 April 2026 is confined to Liquor Control Act changes and Medical Cannabis Regulation Act changes "to provide salaries for members of the Nebraska Medical Cannabis Commission, create a fund, provide for rules and regulations relating to fees, and provide for criminal history record information checks" — Source: http://nebraskalegislature.gov/FloorDocs/109/PDF/Slip/LB1235.pdf — accessed 2026-10-30. Do not cite the introduced LB 1235 tax language as law. - Therefore: there is NO enacted medical-cannabis excise tax in Nebraska. Rate: none. This is a positive finding, not a gap.

General sales tax — the framework that would apply if no exemption applies. - Nebraska imposes sales tax "upon the gross receipts from all sales of tangible personal property sold at retail in this state" (Neb. Rev. Stat. § 77-2703(1)), at "the rate provided in section 77-2701.02" — Source (secondary restatement of the statute, used only for the rate because the official statute page was not opened in this session; the rate itself is corroborated by the Department of Revenue's own sales-tax pages): https://www.lawserver.com/law/state/nebraska/ne-statutes/nebraska_statutes_77-2703 — accessed 2026-10-30. - The state rate is 5.5%. Local option sales taxes may add up to 2%, for a combined range of 5.5%–7.5% depending on the place of sale. Sources: Nebraska Department of Revenue sales-tax pages via https://revenue.nebraska.gov/about/chapter-1-sales-and-use-tax — accessed 2026-10-30 (general framework: "The sales tax is imposed upon the gross receipts from all sales, leases, rentals… of tangible personal property"); rate summary: https://taxcloud.com/sales-tax/nebraska/ — accessed 2026-10-30 ("Nebraska applies a 5.5% state sales tax… Cities can add up to 2% in local sales tax… pushing combined rates as high as 7.5%"). The 5.5% state rate and the up-to-2% local cap are treated as confirmed general tax facts; their application to medical cannabis is NOT confirmed (see below).

The unresolved question — Verify. - Neb. Rev. Stat. § 77-2704.09 exempts from sales and use tax "insulin, mobility enhancing equipment and drugs, not including over-the-counter drugs, when sold for a patient's use under a prescription" and specified medical equipment "when sold for a patient's use under a prescription". "Drug" is defined by reference to recognition in the official United States Pharmacopoeia / Homeopathic Pharmacopoeia / National Formulary, or intended use in diagnosis, cure, mitigation, treatment or prevention of disease, and the definition expressly provides: "Drug does not include a product made from hemp as defined in section 2-503" — Source: https://www.nebraskalegislature.gov/FloorDocs/108/PDF/Intro/LB1248.pdf (quoting § 77-2704.09) — accessed 2026-10-30. Whether dispensary-sold medical cannabis, obtained on a health-care practitioner's written recommendation (not a prescription — cannabis cannot be "prescribed" under federal law) and not a pharmacopoeial drug, falls inside that exemption has not been decided in any official guidance located for this research. - Secondary sources split: - Marijuana Moment, 1 April 2026, reporting LB 1235's passage: "When it does [reach patients], the medicine would not be subject to sales taxes under current law." — Source: https://www.marijuanamoment.net/nebraska-legislature-passes-medical-marijuana-regulation-and-funding-bill/ — accessed 2026-10-30. No statutory citation is given for that statement in the reporting located. - CannBus, Nebraska Legal Summary Q3 2026: "Once operational, dispensaries must collect and remit the standard 5.5% state sales tax" and, in its table, "State sales tax (standard rate applies to medical cannabis) | 5.5%" — Source: https://www.cannbus.org/lsum/ne_lsum.html — accessed 2026-10-30. Again, no Department of Revenue ruling is cited. - CannBus, Nebraska Market Intelligence Report Q3 2026 (data as of 30 September 2026), is more cautious: "No cannabis tax has been confirmed for Nebraska medical cannabis, and no sales exist to tax… Sales tax treatment of medical cannabis | N/A: not confirmed" — Source: https://www.cannbus.org/cannmir/ne_cannmir.html — accessed 2026-10-30. - Resolution adopted for this report: sales-tax treatment of medical cannabis = Verify. The two defensible readings are: (a) ordinary tangible personal property → 5.5% state + applicable local tax (up to 2%); (b) exempt drug / otherwise outside the tax → 0%. Until the Department of Revenue publishes guidance or a dispensary sale is actually taxed, neither may be stated as law. A third figure found in secondary material must be rejected: Cannabis CPA's "Nebraska Cannabis Tax Guide: 2025 Edition" claims "A 4% special state sales tax applies to every medical-cannabis transaction at licensed dispensaries" — Source: https://cannabiscpa.tax/nebraska-cannabis-tax-guide-2025-edition/ — accessed 2026-10-30. That 4% figure is the rate proposed in LB 677 / LB 651 (see §2.7), neither of which was enacted. It is NOT current law and must not be repeated as such. - Local sales taxes on medical cannabis: no medical-cannabis-specific local tax is enacted. If the general sales-tax reading (a) prevails, the ordinary local option tax of the dispensary's city/county would apply on top of the 5.5% state rate, up to the general 2% local cap. If reading (b) prevails, no local tax would apply either. Flagged Verify for the same reason as the state rate. No locality has been found to have enacted a cannabis-specific local tax; Part 3 (not covered here) must confirm locality by locality.

2.2 Licence fees set by the Commission — three layers that must not be conflated

Layer 1 — Statutory authority and cap: LB 1235 (ENACTED). - LB 1235, 109th Legislature, Second Session, introduced by the General Affairs Committee, passed on Final Reading with emergency clause 46–2 on 1 April 2026 and approved by the Governor on 7 April 2026 — Sources: https://update.legislature.ne.gov/?p=40719 — accessed 2026-10-30 ("LB1235… was given final approval April 1… passed on a vote of 46-2 and takes effect immediately"); http://nebraskalegislature.gov/FloorDocs/109/PDF/Slip/LB1235.pdf — accessed 2026-10-30 ("Approved by the Governor April 7, 2026"); Nebraska Legislature priority listing, https://nebraskalegislature.gov/session/priority.php?order_by=stat&sort=desc — accessed 2026-10-30 (LB 1235 listed as "Passed"). - Operative fee text (engrossed version, § 8): "The commission may by rule and regulation provide for a schedule of fees related to the administration of applications, registrations, and renewals of registrations. No fee shall exceed fifty thousand dollars." — Source: https://legiscan.com/NE/text/LB1235/id/3397213/Nebraska-2025-LB1235-Engrossed.pdf — accessed 2026-10-30. - LB 1235 also: creates the Nebraska Medical Cannabis Commission Cash Fund "to receive fees, gifts, grants and other revenue to support administration and enforcement, including salaries, materials, equipment, education and training" — Source: https://update.legislature.ne.gov/?p=40719 — accessed 2026-10-30; authorises an annual $12,500 salary for commission members — same source; and requires an applicant for initial issuance to "submit two legible sets of fingerprints to be furnished to the Federal Bureau of Investigation through the Nebraska State Patrol for a national criminal history information record check and the fee for such record check payable to the patrol" — Source: https://legiscan.com/NE/text/LB1235/id/3397213/Nebraska-2025-LB1235-Engrossed.pdf — accessed 2026-10-30. - Note on the $50,000 cap: it is a per-fee cap ("No fee shall exceed fifty thousand dollars"), not a total-cost cap. A licensee paying an application fee + licence fee + inventory-tracking fee + renewal fee will pay several separate fees, each under the cap. Do not describe $50,000 as "the licence fee".

Layer 2 — The cultivator round actually run: NO fee. - The Commission's own "How to apply" page (Nebraska Liquor Control Commission site, crawled 24 August 2026) states for the cultivator round: "There is currently no fee to submit an application." — Source: https://lcc.nebraska.gov/medical-cannabis/medical-cannabis-commission-how-apply — accessed 2026-10-30. That page is stale (it still describes the September 2025 cultivator window and emergency regulations of 9 September 2025), but it is the official explanation for why the first four cultivator licences carried no application fee. - The draft regulations confirm the mechanism: "If a fee is required for an application or renewal, licenses are valid for a period of one year from the date of issuance. If a fee is not required for the application or renewal, licenses are valid for a period of six (6) months from the date of issuance." (238 NAC 1, § 03.09) — Source: https://mcc.nebraska.gov/sites/default/files/doc/Revised%20-%20238%20NAC%201%20DRAFT.8.21.final.pdf — accessed 2026-10-30. Commission minutes of 16 March 2026 confirm the consequence: "On October 8, 2025, the Commission issued two cultivator licenses. Under the current emergency regulations, those licenses expire six months from the date of issuance, since no fee is required to maintain the license." — Source: https://mcc.nebraska.gov/sites/default/files/MCC%20Meeting%20Minutes%203.16.26.pdf — accessed 2026-10-30. Hence the six-month renewals granted in 2026 (see Part 4, §4.5).

Layer 3 — Draft fee schedule: PROPOSED / PENDING, not yet final or collectible. - Source document: Title 238, Nebraska Medical Cannabis Commission, Chapter 1, "Revised — 238 NAC 1 DRAFT.8.21.final.pdf" (i.e. draft dated 21 August 2026), opened and read directly for this research — Source: https://mcc.nebraska.gov/sites/default/files/doc/Revised%20-%20238%20NAC%201%20DRAFT.8.21.final.pdf — accessed 2026-10-30. Every fee in the table below is from that draft. The file name itself marks it DRAFT. - Status: the Commission voted on 17 August 2026 to advance amendments including this fee schedule; a public hearing was scheduled for 15 October 2026; Nebraska Public Media reported from the Commission's 14 September 2026 meeting that "the updated regulations will not become final until next year" and that "it has said it will not move forward with licensing product manufacturers or dispensaries until the fees are authorized" — Source: https://nebraskapublicmedia.org/en/news/news-articles/updated-medical-cannabis-regulations-in-limbo-until-2027/ — accessed 2026-10-30. The Marijuana Herald's 15 September 2026 report is to the same effect: "The commission said it will not license manufacturers or dispensaries until fees for those licenses are authorized, and set a public hearing on fee rules for October 15" — Source: https://themarijuanaherald.com/2026/09/nebraska-medical-marijuana-rule-updates-delayed-until-2027-as-10-manufacturer-applications-are-submitted/ — accessed 2026-10-30. Until Attorney General and Governor approval, these fees are PROPOSED. Do not describe them as fees currently payable, and do not add them to any "current cost of a licence" total without the PROPOSED label. - A figure appearing in some secondary coverage — a $250 fee to license a new product plus a $100 renewal fee — was NOT found anywhere in the draft regulations text searched for this research (full-text searches for the fee provisions returned only the fees below). It is therefore Verify / Unconfirmed and is not included in the fee table.

Fee table (all rows PROPOSED unless marked ENACTED or HISTORICAL):

LB 1235 § 9; https://lcc.nebraska.gov/medical-cannabis/medical-cannabis-commission-how-apply — accessed 2026-10-30 ENACTED requirement; amount Verify for the FBI check

Resulting fee stacks under the DRAFT schedule (arithmetic only, PROPOSED): - New cultivator: $5,000 (application) + $20,000 (licence) + $13,000 (inventory tracking) = $38,000 before any build-out, bond, testing, or operating cost. - New dispensary: $5,000 + $15,000 + $13,000 = $33,000. - New product manufacturer without transport certification: $5,000 + $20,000 + $13,000 = $38,000; with certification: $48,000. - Annual renewal stack (draft): cultivator $36,500 ($23,500 + $13,000); manufacturer without certification $36,500; manufacturer with certification $41,500 ($28,500 + $13,000); dispensary $31,000 ($18,000 + $13,000). The $36,500 cultivator renewal figure is corroborated by the four licensed cultivators' joint letter of 15 October 2026, which objected to "proposed annual fees totaling $36,500, including a $23,500 license renewal fee and a $13,000 inventory tracking fee" — Source: https://mjbizdaily.com/news/nebraska-cannabis-growers-face-up-to-2-year-wait-to-sell-products/618795/ — accessed 2026-10-30.

2.3 Hemp product tax

  • No hemp-specific excise tax is enacted. No statute was found imposing a tax on hemp, CBD, or other hemp-derived products beyond ordinary sales tax.
  • Retail hemp/CBD products sold as tangible personal property fall under the general sales-tax framework in §2.1 (5.5% state + applicable local tax up to 2%), subject to the same exemption questions that apply to any product (e.g. whether a product qualifies as food/food ingredients, which are generally exempt from state sales tax except prepared food, or as a drug under § 77-2704.09 — noting that § 77-2704.09 expressly excludes "a product made from hemp as defined in section 2-503" from the definition of "drug"). No Department of Revenue hemp-product ruling was located; the application to any particular hemp product is therefore Verify at product level, but the absence of a hemp-specific excise is a positive finding.
  • The only hemp taxes ever proposed in the period covered are listed in §2.7 (LB 316's 10% cannabidiol excise; the 2024 special-session LB 34 proposal for a 30% tax on "consumable hemp" products, reported as advanced from Revenue Committee on a 6–1 vote — Source: https://www.MMJDaily.com/article/9649508/us-ne-bill-to-levy-30-tax-on-consumable-hemp-products-advances-out-of-committee/ — accessed 2026-10-30; no enactment of LB 34's hemp tax was found, and its final disposition is Verify). Neither is law.
  • Federal hemp changes (PL 119-37 §781 / PL 119-103 §2019, 12 November / 11 December 2026 dates) impose no state tax by themselves. No Nebraska statute or Department of Revenue notice responding to those dates with a tax change was located as of 2026-10-30 — Verify / Unavailable for any post-11-December-2026 state tax response; Part 1 must cover the substantive state response.

2.4 The Marijuana and Controlled Substances Tax (the "stamp tax") — still on the books, not a programme tax

  • Verbatim operative text, Neb. Rev. Stat. § 77-4303(1): "A tax is hereby imposed on marijuana and controlled substances at the following rates: (a) On each ounce of marijuana or each portion of an ounce, one hundred dollars; (b) On each gram or portion of a gram of a controlled substance that is customarily sold by weight or volume, one hundred fifty dollars; or (c) On each fifty dosage units or portion thereof of a controlled substance that is not customarily sold by weight, five hundred dollars." — Source: https://nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=77-4303&print=true — accessed 2026-10-30. Source: Laws 1990, LB 260, § 3; Laws 1991, LB 773, § 27 (as shown on the statute page).
  • Subsection (3) as currently enacted: "The tax shall not be imposed upon a person registered or otherwise lawfully in possession of marijuana or a controlled substance pursuant to Chapter 28, article 4." — same source. Chapter 28, article 4 is the Uniform Controlled Substances Act registration regime; it does not on its face mention the Medical Cannabis Acts.
  • Why this matters and why it is not a programme tax: this is a dealer stamp tax enforced against illicit possession (failure to affix stamps is itself a felony under § 77-4309, referenced in University of Nebraska guidance summarising the regime — Source: https://dooprimechn.co/__line/www.unk.edu/student_affairs/_files/drug_free_statement.pdf — accessed 2026-10-30). It is not collected from licensed dispensaries and produces no programme revenue. Whether a qualified patient lawfully in possession under Initiative 437, or a licensee under Initiative 438, is within the subsection (3) exclusion was precisely what LB 651 and the introduced LB 1235 would have clarified by expressly removing lawful medical cannabis from § 77-4303 (see §2.7). Because those bills were not enacted in that form, the interaction remains Verify — a patient or licensee should not be advised that § 77-4303 affirmatively does or does not apply to them on the basis of the current text alone. No official revenue figure for this tax attributable to marijuana was located (Unavailable).

2.5 Local sales taxes — general position

  • Nebraska cities may impose a local option sales tax of up to 2% on top of the 5.5% state rate; a small number of counties levy their own local taxes (e.g. Dakota County's 0.5% county tax outside municipal limits, as reported in secondary rate guides). The Department of Revenue publishes quarterly local rate tables and administers local taxes centrally on a single return — Sources: https://taxcloud.com/sales-tax/nebraska/ — accessed 2026-10-30; https://revenue.nebraska.gov/about/temporary-place-business — accessed 2026-10-30 ("Retailers must collect and remit all applicable Nebraska and local option sales taxes on sales occurring in Nebraska on the Nebraska and Local Sales and Use Tax Return, Form 10").
  • No cannabis-specific local tax, and no locality-level cannabis tax ordinance, was located for this research. Locality-by-locality confirmation belongs to Part 3. If the general sales-tax reading in §2.1(a) prevails, combined rates at a future dispensary would be 5.5% plus that locality's local option rate (commonly 1.5% in Omaha and Lincoln, for a 7.0% combined rate, per secondary rate guides — Verify at address level via the Department of Revenue's rate lookup).

2.6 IRS §280E and Nebraska income tax — no decoupling statute; federal result flows through, unconfirmed by state guidance

  • Federal layer (fixed baseline, not re-researched): §280E disallows deductions for a trade or business trafficking in Schedule I or II controlled substances. For state-licensed medical marijuana, now Schedule III (effective 28 April 2026), §280E is generally lifted; for adult-use marijuana (Schedule I) it remains fully applicable. Nebraska has no adult-use programme, so the only Nebraska activity that could benefit from the Schedule III change is state-licensed medical activity — and no Nebraska licensee has yet made a taxable sale to which the benefit could attach (see §2.8).
  • Nebraska layer — what the statute actually does: Nebraska individual income tax is imposed as a percentage of "federal adjusted gross income as modified in sections 77-2716 and 77-2716.01" (Neb. Rev. Stat. § 77-2715(2)(b), for taxable years beginning on or after 1 January 2014) — Source: https://nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=s7727015000 — accessed 2026-10-30. Nebraska therefore starts from the federal figure. If federal AGI already reflects §280E disallowance (Schedule I activity), that disallowance flows into the Nebraska base unless § 77-2716 provides an adjustment; if federal AGI reflects full deductibility (Schedule III medical activity after 28 April 2026), that deductibility likewise flows through unless § 77-2716 adds it back.
  • Finding: NO Nebraska decoupling statute was located. No provision in §§ 77-2716 / 77-2716.01, and no standalone cannabis tax statute, was found that either (a) expressly decouples Nebraska from §280E (i.e. allows state deductions federal law disallows), or (b) expressly requires a Nebraska addback of deductions federal law allows. Searches for "Nebraska 280E decoupling" and "Nebraska 280E state income tax" returned only secondary tax-firm material: Cannabis CPA (2025) asserts "Nebraska conforms to federal IRC § 280E: only Cost of Goods Sold is deductible" — Source: https://cannabiscpa.tax/nebraska-cannabis-tax-guide-2025-edition/ — accessed 2026-10-30 — but that guide pre-dates Schedule III rescheduling and cites no Nebraska statute; AndreTaxCo's Nebraska page lists the state §280E questions as "N/A" — Source: https://andretaxco.com/nebraska-cannabis-280e — accessed 2026-10-30; CannBus Q3 2026 lists "State 280E tax relief | N/A: not confirmed" and "State 280E conformity | Not confirmed in available sources" — Source: https://www.cannbus.org/lsum/ne_lsum.html — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Conclusion, flagged Verify: Nebraska has neither enacted a decoupling statute nor published (in sources located) Department of Revenue guidance on §280E for cannabis businesses. The mechanically correct statement is: Nebraska follows federal AGI, so the federal Schedule III / Schedule I distinction should flow through to the Nebraska base, but no Nebraska authority located confirms this for a cannabis licensee, and entity-specific treatment (individual vs corporation, apportionment, and any § 77-2716 adjustment) must be confirmed with the Department of Revenue or a Nebraska tax adviser before filing. Do NOT state "Nebraska decouples from 280E" — that would be false. Do NOT state "Nebraska has a 280E addback" — no such statute was found.
  • Practical consequence as of 2026-10-30: even on the most favourable reading, the Schedule III / §280E benefit is currently theoretical for Nebraska operators. Secondary analysis is explicit on this point: "the end of 280E for Nebraska medical operators is theoretical, a benefit that attaches to revenue these businesses are not yet allowed to earn" because "no dispensary has opened and no legal sales have occurred" — Source: https://drugpolicywatch.info/states/nebraska.html — accessed 2026-10-30. The four licensed cultivators have costs but, with no lawful path to sale (no manufacturer, no testing-lab licence, no dispensary — Part 4), they have no medical-cannabis sales income against which deductions would currently matter.

2.7 PROPOSED taxes — all labelled PROPOSED, none enacted

All of the following are PROPOSED ONLY. None may be described as current law. All were indefinitely postponed on 17 April 2026 (the last day of the 109th Legislature, Second Session), except where noted.

Proposal What it would have done Status (unicameral stage)
LB 677 (Hansen, 109th Leg., introduced 22 Jan 2025; General Affairs Committee) — medical cannabis framework As introduced, would have created licences for cultivators, dispensaries, product manufacturers, transporters, testing facilities and "vertical" facilities. Tax: a 4% sales tax on all medical cannabis products, revenue to the Property Tax Credit Relief Fund. A pending committee amendment would have raised the sales tax to 5.5% and allowed municipalities to impose an additional 2% tax on dispensaries. Advanced from Revenue Committee 6–1 (secondary report) — Source: https://www.MMJDaily.com/article/9649508/us-ne-bill-to-levy-30-tax-on-consumable-hemp-products-advances-out-of-committee/ — accessed 2026-10-30. LB 1235 as introduced (2026) — medical cannabis taxation Would have provided "for taxation of sales of medical cannabis and distribution of the revenue" and removed medical cannabis from the § 77-4303 tax (see §2.1). Tax provisions removed by committee amendment before passage; enacted LB 1235 contains no tax rate. Do not cite the introduced text as law.

2.8 Revenue figures — only where official data exists

  • Medical cannabis tax revenue (state): $0. No licensed dispensary sale has occurred; no excise exists; no sales-tax collection on medical cannabis has been reported by the Department of Revenue. This is a derived zero from the operating facts in Part 4, not a Department of Revenue publication — it is stated as such and must not be cited as a Department of Revenue revenue table.
  • Medical cannabis licence-fee revenue: $0 from the cultivator round (no fee charged — §2.2, Layer 2). Draft-schedule fees, if and when finalised, would be the first licence-fee revenue; as of 2026-10-30 none has been collected under the draft schedule (the Commission's own position is that it cannot license manufacturers/dispensaries until fees are authorised — §2.2, Layer 3).
  • Commission funding actually appropriated (not tax revenue): secondary reporting of the 2026 budget adjustments (LB 1071) states the Liquor Control Commission, which houses the Medical Cannabis Commission, received "an additional $1.38 million this fiscal year ending June 30 and $1 million for the next fiscal year" at the Governor's recommendation — Source: https://www.marijuanamoment.net/nebraska-legislature-passes-medical-marijuana-regulation-and-funding-bill/ — accessed 2026-10-30. This is an appropriation, not programme revenue, and is secondary-sourced; the LB 1071 text itself was not opened in this session — Verify before republication as a budget figure.
  • Hemp tax revenue: Unavailable. No official Department of Revenue figure for sales tax attributable to hemp/CBD products was located. A single-retailer anecdote from the LB 316 floor debate — Senator Jane Raybould stated that "a single retailer with 22 locations paid nearly $1.5 million in sales tax last year alone" (out of 355 THC stores cited in the same debate) — Source: https://update.legislature.ne.gov/?p=38693 — accessed 2026-10-30 — is floor-debate rhetoric, not a revenue statistic, and must not be used as a market or revenue total.
  • Marijuana stamp-tax revenue: Unavailable. No official figure was located.

Part 3 — Local Rules

PART 3 — LOCAL RULES

3.1 What authority counties, cities and villages have — and which level does what

State level — the Commission has the licensing power; the initiatives contain no local opt-out clause located in this research.

  • The Nebraska Medical Cannabis Regulation Act (Initiative 438) gives the Nebraska Medical Cannabis Commission (MCC) the duty to, "No later than July 1, 2025, establish criteria to accept or deny applications for registrations, including adopting, promulgating, and enforcing reasonable rules, regulations, and eligibility standards for such registrations" and "No later than October 1, 2025, begin granting registrations to applicants that meet eligibility standards and other requirements established by the commission." — Nebraska Legislature, Neb. Rev. Stat. § 71-24,111 — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • The same statute gives the Commission power to grant, deny, revoke and suspend registrations, to adopt distinct rules for different categories of establishments, to inspect establishments, and to impose administrative fines. — Nebraska Legislature, Neb. Rev. Stat. § 71-24,111 — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Verify: No provision in §§ 71-24,106–71-24,111 as read on 2026-10-30 was located that expressly authorises a county, city or village to ban ("opt out" of) state-licensed medical cannabis establishments, or that expressly requires a local governing body's written approval before the Commission may license. The older, failed 2015 bill LB643 would have empowered "any county, city or village governing body" to prohibit a manufacturer or dispensary, but that was a failed bill, not current law, and is noted here only to avoid confusion. — Unicameral Update, "Legalization of medical marijuana stalls" (LB643, 2015) — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • What local government does in practice, from the opened Commission rules: the Commission's draft Title 238, Chapter 1 defines "LOCAL GOVERNMENT" as "a village, town, city, or county" and requires every applicant to state that it "is in compliance with all applicable federal, state, or local laws, regulations, rules, or ordinances" and to show facility design/operational readiness "including compliance with all applicable federal, state, and local laws, regulations, rules, and ordinances." — Nebraska Medical Cannabis Commission, Title 238 NAC 1, draft 21 Aug (definitions § 02.20; cultivator application § 05.04(E) and business-plan § 05.05(B)) — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Verify — local-approval claim in secondary guides: several secondary licensing guides state that an applicant must obtain written approval from both the local governing body and the local health department. That requirement was not found by text search ("health department", "governing body") in the opened Title 238 Chapter 1 draft linked above, so it is not adopted here as a Commission rule; it is flagged Verify for the Part 4/Part 11 researchers to confirm against the final, signed Title 238 text and the Commission's application instructions. The secondary statement is: "Must obtain written approval from both the local governing body and local health department" and "Get written approval from both: The local public health department and the city council or county board where your facility will operate." — Quantum9, "How to Open a Dispensary in Nebraska" — accessed 2026-10-30. Do not treat this as a confirmed rule.
  • Bottom line for Part 3: State (MCC) = licensing, caps, product and operating rules, inspections, sanctions. Local (village/town/city/county) = land-use/zoning, conditional-use/special-use permits, building and other generally applicable local ordinances, exercised through each jurisdiction's own zoning code — not through a state-issued local cannabis licence. No separate state "local cannabis licence" was located. Local action therefore operates as a practical gate (a site that is not zoned/permtted locally cannot satisfy the Commission's "compliance with all applicable local laws/ordinances" application statement), even though no express local veto/opt-out statute was located. That distinction is important and is stated as found, not as a legal conclusion.

Hemp / CBD / smoke / vape shops — local authority:

  • No Nebraska state licence for a retail CBD, hemp-derived-product or smoke/vape shop was located in the sources opened for this Part; hemp cultivation licensing moved from the Nebraska Department of Agriculture (NDA) to USDA on 1 January 2025 (see Part 6), and retail hemp shops are therefore governed, on the evidence found, by generally applicable local zoning, business-licensing and (for tobacco/nicotine) tobacco-retail rules, not by a cannabis-specific local permit. — Nebraska Department of Agriculture, Hemp page — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Verify: Whether any individual Nebraska city or village has adopted a hemp/CBD/smoke-shop-specific ordinance (definition, buffer, spacing or cap) was checked for the jurisdictions in § 3.3 by search; no such ordinance was located for any of the required jurisdictions except the general tobacco/smoke-shop discussion noted for Omaha below. A dedicated smoke-shop/vape-shop ordinance search returned only out-of-state examples (e.g. a Clarksville, TN proposal and Cleveland, OH and Monroe, NC codes) which are not Nebraska law and are excluded. — Citizen Portal, Clarksville TN item (out-of-state, excluded) — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Omaha's pre-existing tobacco-retail ordinance (passed 8 October 2002, effective 1 February 2003) regulates retail sales of tobacco/nicotine products generally; it is not a medical-cannabis or hemp ordinance and is noted only because smoke/vape shops selling nicotine fall under it. — Smoke Free Nebraska, Omaha Tobacco Ordinance summary — accessed 2026-10-30.

3.2 State siting rules that frame every local decision (Commission regulations)

These are state rules, included here because local zoning must be read against them:

  • Covered location defined: "Covered location means any school, business operating under a license issued pursuant to the Nebraska Child Care Licensing Act Neb. Rev. Stat. § 71-1908 to § 71-1923.03, and any church, hospital, or mental health substance use treatment center as defined by Neb. Rev. Stat. § 71-423." — Nebraska Medical Cannabis Commission, Title 238 NAC 1, § 02.10 — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Dispensary 1,000-foot buffer: "No dispensary license will be issued for any premises located within one thousand feet of any covered location," except a licensee already in operation before the covered location was established; "One thousand feet (1,000 ft) will be measured in a straight line from the nearest property line of the covered location to the nearest perimeter wall of the licensed premises." — Nebraska Medical Cannabis Commission, Title 238 NAC 1, § 04.08 — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Cultivator 1,000-foot buffer, identical wording: "No cultivator license will be issued for any premises located within one thousand feet of any covered location," with the same prior-operation exception and straight-line measurement. — Nebraska Medical Cannabis Commission, Title 238 NAC 1, § 05.08 — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Judicial-district cap (state, not local): "No more than one (1) dispensary license shall be issued in any one district court judicial district as defined in Neb. Rev. Stat. § 24-301.02," with a possible additional licence the following year if the Commission finds supply insufficient in a district. — Nebraska Medical Cannabis Commission, Title 238 NAC 1, § 04.12 — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Cultivator cap (state): "No more than four (4) cultivator licenses shall be issued in the state," with a possible additional licence the following year on a sufficiency finding. — Nebraska Medical Cannabis Commission, Title 238 NAC 1, § 05.12 — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Relocation (state): "A licensee shall not relocate a registered cannabis establishment from the address or location as specified in the license, unless the licensee can show an exceptional change of circumstances and the Commission approves the license relocation," with a $1,500 relocation fee (a state Commission fee, not a local fee). — Nebraska Medical Cannabis Commission, Title 238 NAC 1, §§ 04.09–04.10 — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Caveat: The Title 238 text opened here is the Commission's draft dated 21 August (file name "DRAFT.8.21.final.pdf"); the permanent regulations took effect 6 July 2026 according to secondary reporting, and a further amendment round was pending into 2027. The buffer/cap language above must be re-checked against the final signed Title 238 before republication. — Marijuana Moment, "Nebraska Officials Consider Changes To Medical Marijuana Rules" (Oct 2026) — accessed 2026-10-30.

No official statewide list exists (as found).

  • No list published by the Medical Cannabis Commission, the Secretary of State, or any other Nebraska state agency, of counties/cities/villages with medical-cannabis zoning, moratoriums, caps or bans, was located on 2026-10-30. If such a list exists it was not found in the Commission, Legislature or NDA pages opened for this Part — Verify / follow-up: ask the Commission directly and re-check mcc.nebraska.gov meeting materials.
  • The table below therefore reports jurisdiction-by-jurisdiction findings from the searches actually run. "No ordinance located" means no ordinance was found in the searches run on 2026-10-30; it is not proof that none exists (many Nebraska villages do not publish codes online). Do not read it as a ban, and do not read it as permission.
