Part 9 — Regulators and History

NEBRASKA — Cannabis and Hemp Research, RUN 2, PART 9 ONLY

Scope: Part 9 (Regulators and History) only. Parts 0–8 and 10–12 are not covered here except by brief cross-reference. Every fact below carries a source link and the access date 2026-10-30, as instructed. Where a page could not be fully opened, or a point rests on secondary reporting rather than the official text itself, it is flagged Verify or Unavailable and the limitation is stated. No proposed bill, proposed regulation, enjoined action, or pending lawsuit outcome is presented as current law. Nebraska has a one-chamber (unicameral) Legislature, so bill progress is reported by stage, not by chamber.

Run 1 baseline applied, and where this run changes it: The Run 1 baseline (30 Oct 2026) is adopted throughout: Initiatives 437/438 passed 5 Nov 2024 (~71% / ~67%); LB 1235 enacted, operative 8 Apr 2026; LB 316, LB 677, LB 933 and LB 483 dead (indefinitely postponed 17 Apr 2026); Kuehn v. Evnen, S-24-901, 322 Neb. 161 (4 Sept 2026), 7-0, upheld the initiatives; Commission emergency regulations summer 2025, permanent regulations approved by the Attorney General 30 June 2026 and the Governor 1 July 2026 (exact effective date Verify); cultivators 2 offered 7 Oct 2025, 4th offered 16 Mar 2026; 0 dispensaries and 0 manufacturers licensed; hemp state programme ended 31 Dec 2024, USDA licensing from 1 Jan 2025; Attorney General delta-8 enforcement: 104 Omaha locations, 15 lawsuits in the Attorney General's own release. Two Run 1 flags are RESOLVED in this run: (1) the exact certified 2024 vote counts, flagged Verify in Run 1, are now confirmed from the Secretary of State's official canvass book (see §9.3); (2) the "second Kuehn" case is clarified in §9.2/§9.3 — it is Kuehn v. Pillen, dismissed by the Lancaster County District Court on 26 June 2025 for lack of standing, without reaching the federal Supremacy Clause argument, and affirmed (without prejudice) by the Nebraska Supreme Court in September 2026; its Supreme Court docket number and exact September 2026 date remain Verify. One new conflict is flagged, not smoothed over: the effective/proclamation date for Initiatives 437/438 is given as 12 Dec 2024 by most sources but as 10 Dec 2024 by one secondary FAQ (see §9.2).

9.1 Every state agency with a role in cannabis and hemp

9.1.1 Nebraska Medical Cannabis Commission (MCC) — the lead medical regulator

  • The Commission is created by Neb. Rev. Stat. § 71-24,109, which provides in full, in relevant part: "(1) For purposes of providing the necessary registration and regulation of persons that possess, manufacture, distribute, deliver, and dispense cannabis for medical purposes pursuant to the Nebraska Medical Cannabis Regulation Act, the Nebraska Medical Cannabis Commission is created." — Source: https://nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=71-24,109 — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • The same section provides: "(2) The commission shall consist of no fewer than three and no more than five members."; "(3) The three members of the Nebraska Liquor Control Commission shall be ex officio members of the commission, serving terms and receiving appointment in the same manner as provided in section 53-105."; "(4) The Governor may appoint two additional members, subject to confirmation by a majority of the members elected to the Legislature, to serve with the members of the Nebraska Liquor Control Commission as members of the Nebraska Medical Cannabis Commission. The members appointed pursuant to this subsection shall serve six-year terms."; "(5) The Governor may reappoint members of the commission, subject to approval by a majority of the members elected to the Legislature."; "(6) Each member of the Nebraska Medical Cannabis Commission shall receive an annual salary of twelve thousand five hundred dollars. Such salary shall be paid in equal installments." — Source: https://nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=71-24,109 — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • The source line for § 71-24,109 is "Initiative Law 2024, No. 438, § 4" as amended by "Laws 2026, LB1235, § 6" with "Operative Date: April 8, 2026." — Source: https://nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=71-24,109 — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • The Commission's powers and duties are at Neb. Rev. Stat. § 71-24,111, which provides, verbatim in relevant part: "(1) No later than July 1, 2025, establish criteria to accept or deny applications for registrations, including adopting, promulgating, and enforcing reasonable rules, regulations, and eligibility standards for such registrations; (2) No later than October 1, 2025, begin granting registrations to applicants that meet eligibility standards and other requirements established by the commission; (3) Grant, deny, revoke, and suspend registrations based upon reasonable criteria and procedures established by the commission; (4) Have the authority to adopt, promulgate, and enforce distinct sets of rules and regulations for different categories of registered cannabis establishments; … (7) Inspect the operation of any registered cannabis establishment for the purpose of verifying compliance with rules and regulations adopted and promulgated by the commission; (8) Have the authority to impose, subject to judicial review under the Administrative Procedure Act, administrative fines for each violation of any rules and regulations adopted and promulgated pursuant to the Nebraska Medical Cannabis Regulation Act; … (11) Acquire necessary offices, facilities, counsel, and staff; and (12) Do all things necessary and proper to carry out its powers and duties under the Nebraska Medical Cannabis Regulation Act, including the adoption and promulgation of rules and regulations and such other actions as permitted by the Administrative Procedure Act." — Source: https://www.nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=71-24,111&print=true — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Website and official public contact (official, not personal): Website https://mcc.nebraska.gov — accessed 2026-10-30 (meeting-information page opened directly, see below). Meeting address: 301 Centennial Mall South, 1st Floor Hearing Room, Lincoln, NE 68509 — Source: https://mcc.nebraska.gov/meeting-information — accessed 2026-10-30. Written-comment mailing address: Nebraska Medical Cannabis Commission, PO Box 95046, Lincoln, NE 68509-5046, and email mcc.contact@nebraska.gov — Source: https://mcc.nebraska.gov/about-us/events/2026/public-hearing — accessed 2026-10-30. Contact-number conflict — Verify: MCC meeting agendas give 402-471-2571 for accessibility accommodations — Source: https://mcc.nebraska.gov/sites/default/files/October%2019%2C%202026%20Meeting%20Agenda.pdf — accessed 2026-10-30 — while an earlier MCC meeting-information search result gave (402) 471-2812 for questions or comments; the directly opened meeting-information page in this run did not display a telephone number, so which number is the current main line is Verify before republication.
  • Members — formation and changes (secondary reporting, because no single MCC "members" roster page was opened directly in this run — Verify the current roster against mcc.nebraska.gov before republication): The inaugural meeting was 9 June 2025, at which Dr. Monica Oldenburg (Lincoln) was elected chair, with Bruce Bailey (Lincoln) and Kim Lowe (Kearney) as the Liquor Control Commission members then seated and Lorelle Mueting (Gretna) absent; one seat (2nd Congressional District) was vacant — Source: https://www.marijuanamoment.net/nebraska-medical-marijuana-commission-holds-first-meeting-just-weeks-before-key-deadline/ — accessed 2026-10-30; corroborated by https://www.weedworthy.com/the-news/press-releases/medical-cannabis-commission-kicks-off-in-nebraska — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Governor Jim Pillen appointed retired District Judge J. Michael Coffey (Omaha, 2nd Congressional District) to the Liquor Control Commission on 18 June 2025, which also made him a Medical Cannabis Commission member; Mueting and Coffey executed oaths at the 26 June 2025 meeting — Sources: https://themarijuanaherald.com/2025/06/governor-appoints-former-judge-to-nebraska-board-overseeing-medical-marijuana/ — accessed 2026-10-30; https://mcc.nebraska.gov/sites/default/files/September%2014%2C%202026%20DRAFT%20Meeting%20Minutes.pdf — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Oldenburg was appointed by the Governor in April 2025 and confirmed by the Legislature 34-11 in May 2025; Mueting was appointed alongside her and confirmed 27-16 — Source: https://www.marijuanamoment.net/nebraska-governor-accepts-applications-for-medical-cannabis-commission-opening-following-chairs-resignation/ — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Bailey and Lowe resigned 29 Sept 2025 at the Governor's request, and on 17 Nov 2025 the Governor announced Robert "Bud" Synhorst (Lincoln) and James Elworth (Nebraska City) as the new Liquor Control Commissioners, which brought the Medical Cannabis Commission back to five members — Sources: https://mcc.nebraska.gov/sites/default/files/MCC%20Meeting%20Minutes%2012.2.25.pdf — accessed 2026-10-30; https://1stskyomaha.com/pillen-appoints-two-commissioners-to-liquor-control-medical-cannabis-commissions/ — accessed 2026-10-30. Staggered terms reported secondarily as Elworth to May 2027, Synhorst to May 2029 and Coffey to May 2031 are Verify against the legislative confirmation record — Source: https://1stskyomaha.com/pillen-appoints-two-commissioners-to-liquor-control-medical-cannabis-commissions/ — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Oldenburg resigned as chair on 2 Feb 2026; the Commission then voted Lorelle Mueting interim chair, and the at-large seat remained vacant — Sources: https://www.marijuanamoment.net/nebraska-governor-accepts-applications-for-medical-cannabis-commission-opening-following-chairs-resignation/ — accessed 2026-10-30; https://www.kfornow.com/chair-of-the-nebraska-medical-cannabis-commission-steps-down/ — accessed 2026-10-30. At the 14 Sept 2026 meeting the Commission was still operating without a full complement of five commissioners — Source: https://mcc.nebraska.gov/sites/default/files/September%2014%2C%202026%20DRAFT%20Meeting%20Minutes.pdf — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Staff: The Commission's first executive director is Brett Long, previously a tax revenue specialist with the Nebraska Department of Revenue, scheduled to begin 28 Sept 2026 — Sources: https://themarijuanaherald.com/2026/09/nebraska-medical-marijuana-rule-updates-delayed-until-2027-as-10-manufacturer-applications-are-submitted/ — accessed 2026-10-30; https://nebraskapublicmedia.org/en/news/news-articles/updated-medical-cannabis-regulations-in-limbo-until-2027/ — accessed 2026-10-30. Because no MCC announcement of the appointment was opened directly in this run, the start date is Verify against MCC minutes. Earlier staffing was shared: Bo Botelho, general counsel for the Department of Health and Human Services, was assisting the Commission in September 2025 — Source: https://1stskyomaha.com/pillen-appoints-two-commissioners-to-liquor-control-medical-cannabis-commissions/ — accessed 2026-10-30; and on 2 Dec 2025 the Commission approved a Department of Administrative Services (DAS) HR Shared Services Agreement for the Liquor Control and Medical Cannabis Commissions — Source: https://mcc.nebraska.gov/sites/default/files/MCC%20Meeting%20Minutes%2012.2.25.pdf — accessed 2026-10-30. A Commission Director position was posted and open as of the 22 June 2026 meeting — Source: https://mcc.nebraska.gov/sites/default/files/June%2022%20Draft%20Meeting%20Minutes%20changes.pdf — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Meeting record: The MCC meeting-information page lists public meetings from the inaugural meeting (9 June 2025, 3:00–5:00 p.m.) through scheduled meetings on 19 Oct, 16 Nov and 14 Dec 2026, all at 301 Centennial Mall South, 1st Floor Hearing Room, Lincoln — Source: https://mcc.nebraska.gov/meeting-information — accessed 2026-10-30. The 16 Nov and 14 Dec 2026 meetings were still in the future on the Run 1 research date (30 Oct 2026) and are therefore scheduled dates, not outcomes.

9.1.2 Nebraska Liquor Control Commission (LCC) — the host and ex officio source

  • The LCC consists of three members appointed by the Governor, subject to confirmation by a majority of the members elected to the Legislature, no more than two of the same political party, and no two citizens of the same congressional district — Source: https://nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=53-105 — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Support role — YES, it supports the Commission: The three LCC members are ex officio MCC members (§ 71-24,109(3), quoted above) — Source: https://nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=71-24,109 — accessed 2026-10-30. The MCC meets in the LCC Hearing Room, its early public-comment inbox was the LCC front desk (lcc.frontdesk@nebraska.gov), and the Governor's budget support for the MCC was routed through the LCC, which "houses" the MCC — Sources: https://mcc.nebraska.gov/meeting-information — accessed 2026-10-30; https://www.marijuanamoment.net/nebraska-medical-marijuana-regulators-approve-emergency-rules-banning-flower-access-for-patients/ — accessed 2026-10-30; https://northplattepost.com/posts/d0552208-6bcb-4aca-9568-bbf0ef162515 — accessed 2026-10-30. The MCC approved creation of a stand-alone website separate from the LCC website in early 2026 — Source: https://www.kfornow.com/chair-of-the-nebraska-medical-cannabis-commission-steps-down/ — accessed 2026-10-30. No separate LCC public contact address is stated here because no LCC contact page was opened directly in this run — Verify against lcc.nebraska.gov before republication; do not reuse the MCC address as the LCC's.
  • LCC members as of late 2026 (from the membership changes above): J. Michael Coffey, Robert "Bud" Synhorst and James Elworth — Source: https://mcc.nebraska.gov/sites/default/files/MCC%20Meeting%20Minutes%2012.2.25.pdf — accessed 2026-10-30.

9.1.3 Secretary of State — elections, certification, ballot access

  • The Secretary of State (Robert B. Evnen) certifies initiative petitions, places measures on the ballot, and compiles the official canvass. The 13 Sept 2024 certification release states the Elections Division certified that signature requirements were met for both medical cannabis petitions for the 5 Nov 2024 ballot, with 89,962 valid signatures for the Patient Protection petition and 89,856 for the Regulation petition, each meeting the 5% threshold in 52 counties — Source: https://sos.nebraska.gov/sites/default/files/doc/news-releases/Secretary%20of%20State%20certifies%20pair%20of%20medical%20cannabis%20petitions%20for%20November%20ballot.pdf — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • The Board of State Canvassers (Governor Jim Pillen, Secretary of State Robert B. Evnen, Attorney General Mike Hilgers, State Treasurer Tom Briese, Auditor of Public Accounts Mike Foley) compiled the official report for the 5 Nov 2024 general election — Source: https://sos.nebraska.gov/sites/default/files/doc/elections/2024/2024%20General%20Canvass%20Book.pdf — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Official public contact (office, not personal): Nebraska Secretary of State, 402-471-2554 / 402-471-3237; Capitol Office, 1445 K Street, Suite 2300, Lincoln, NE 68508; Business Services, 1201 N Street, Suite 120, Lincoln, NE 68508 — Source: https://sos.nebraska.gov/previous-elections — accessed 2026-10-30. Previous-elections page: https://sos.nebraska.gov/previous-elections — accessed 2026-10-30.

9.1.4 Governor — appointments, proclamations, regulation approval, executive orders

  • The Governor appoints LCC members (subject to legislative confirmation) and the two additional MCC members (subject to confirmation by a majority of the members elected to the Legislature), and may reappoint MCC members subject to the same approval — Source: https://nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=71-24,109 — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • The Governor signed proclamations certifying enactment of Measures 437 and 438 in December 2024 (date conflict flagged in §9.2), approved the MCC emergency regulations 29 June 2025, and approved the permanent regulations 1 July 2026 — Sources: https://gray-koln-prod.cdn.arcpublishing.com/2024/12/12/gov-pillen-signs-nebraskas-medical-marijuana-ballot-initiatives-into-law/ — accessed 2026-10-30; https://www.cannabisbusinesstimes.com/us-states/nebraska/news/15749791/nebraska-governor-approves-emergency-regulations-for-medical-cannabis-market — accessed 2026-10-30; https://themarijuanaherald.com/2026/09/nebraska-medical-marijuana-rule-updates-delayed-until-2027-as-10-manufacturer-applications-are-submitted/ — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • On 26 Jan 2026 Governor Pillen signed Executive Order 26-02, "Protecting Nebraskans from Intoxicating Synthetic THC Products," directing state agencies (including Agriculture, Banking and Finance, and Revenue) to review laws and rules so consumable products do not contain intoxicating synthetic THC; the order expressly does not alter the medical marijuana regulation process — Source: https://governor.nebraska.gov/gov-pillen-joins-ag-hilgers-signs-order-addressing-illegal-recreational-synthetic-thc-industry — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • No Governor's-office public contact address is stated here because no Governor contact page was opened directly in this run — Verify against governor.nebraska.gov before republication.

9.1.5 Attorney General — regulation review, petition-fraud prosecution, hemp enforcement

  • The Attorney General reviews MCC permanent regulations for legal and constitutional compliance before the Governor's final approval — Source: https://northplattepost.com/posts/d0552208-6bcb-4aca-9568-bbf0ef162515 — accessed 2026-10-30. The Attorney General approved the permanent regulations 30 June 2026 — Source: https://themarijuanaherald.com/2026/09/nebraska-medical-marijuana-rule-updates-delayed-until-2027-as-10-manufacturer-applications-are-submitted/ — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • On 13 Sept 2024 the Attorney General's office charged a petition circulator with felony fraud (false swearing to a circulator's affidavit, a Class IV felony) in connection with the medical cannabis petitions — Source: https://news.crbmonitor.com/2024/09/nebraska-ag-files-charge-alleging-petition-fraud/ — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Delta-8 / synthetic-THC enforcement (official release, opened directly): Attorney General Mike Hilgers announced cease-and-desist letters relating to 104 retail locations and franchised stores in Omaha; in 2025 Douglas County Sheriff's Office and Attorney General's Office investigators made purchases from stores owned by 35 different companies, and "[e]ach store investigated had at least one product marketed as hemp, which actually had a delta-9 concentration over the legal limit, making it marijuana, and had labels that incorrectly identified the content of the products"; the office demanded signed Assurances of Voluntary Compliance and stated it would refer marijuana-sale evidence to the Douglas County Attorney for consideration of criminal charges; "[t]o date, the Attorney General's Office has filed 15 lawsuits alleging violations of the Consumer Protection Act (Unfairness), Consumer Protection Act (Deception), and Nebraska Pure Food Act. The Attorney General reported that 12 out of 15 of the lawsuits have been settled. The remaining three are being actively litigated." — Source: https://ago.nebraska.gov/news/announcement-new-developments-delta-8-enforcement-efforts — accessed 2026-10-30. A later secondary update (29 Sept 2025) reports 16 lawsuits, 12 settlements and letters to companies operating about 300 stores — Source: https://horganlawfirm.com/delta-8-thc-and-cbd-in-nebraska-a-guide-for-businesses/ — accessed 2026-10-30; the 15→16 difference is treated as a timing difference, current count Verify against the Attorney General's office. No Attorney General public contact address is stated here because no AG contact page was opened directly in this run — Verify against ago.nebraska.gov before republication. Case captions and Lancaster County case numbers for the 15/16 suits were not in the release opened — Unavailable in this run (see Part 5).

9.1.6 Department of Health and Human Services (DHHS) — indirect, not the medical programme operator

  • DHHS is the "Department" for purposes of the Uniform Controlled Substances Act: Neb. Rev. Stat. § 28-401(6) provides "Department means the Department of Health and Human Services" — Source: https://law.justia.com/codes/nebraska/chapter-28/statute-28-401/ — accessed 2026-10-30 (secondary republication of the statute; the official Legislature page for § 28-401 was not opened directly in this run — Verify against nebraskalegislature.gov before quoting).
  • DHHS licenses health care practitioners under the Uniform Credentialing Act, and the Initiative 437 definition of "health care practitioner" is a physician, osteopathic physician, physician assistant or nurse practitioner licensed under that Act (or licensed in any state and practising in compliance with it) — this is the practitioner's licensing nexus, not a DHHS medical-cannabis programme — Source (Run 1, Part 1, § 71-24,104, opened directly in Run 1): https://nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=71-24,104 — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • DHHS has no patient registry, practitioner directory or dispensary role in the enacted Acts as of 30 Oct 2026. The introduced version of LB 1235 would have created a confidential patient/caregiver registry and a practitioner directory, and LB 933 would have barred DHHS or any licensing board from disciplining a practitioner solely for recommending cannabis — but the enacted LB 1235 slip law covers only liquor provisions plus MCC salaries, the MCC Cash Fund, fee rules and criminal-history checks, and LB 933 is dead (indefinitely postponed 17 Apr 2026) — Sources: https://nebraskalegislature.gov/FloorDocs/109/PDF/Slip/LB1235.pdf — accessed 2026-10-30; https://nebraskalegislature.gov/FloorDocs/109/PDF/CS/LB933.pdf — accessed 2026-10-30. Do not cite the introduced LB 1235 registry/directory provisions as law. A June 2025 secondary report of a legal agreement between the MCC, DHHS and the Governor's Policy Research Office in connection with the emergency regulations was not verified against the agreement itself — Verify.
  • No DHHS public contact address is stated here because no DHHS cannabis contact page was opened directly in this run — Verify against dhhs.ne.gov before republication.

9.1.7 Nebraska Department of Agriculture (NDA) — hemp (historical) and food-law proposals

  • NDA ran the state hemp programme under the Nebraska Hemp Farming Act (LB 657, 2019) until 31 Dec 2024. Its current hemp page states: "Persons interested in cultivating hemp in Nebraska may apply for a license from the USDA or a tribal program authorized under the Agriculture Improvement Act of 2018. Nebraska applicants may submit their hemp production application to the USDA starting in fall of 2024 to receive a USDA hemp producer license on January 1, 2025." — Source: https://nda.nebraska.gov/hemp — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • The state retains hemp transportation regulation: the Legislature's session review states the LB 262 provisions "transfer licensure and regulation of hemp cultivation from the state Department of Agriculture to the U.S. Department of Agriculture, beginning Jan. 1, 2025" and "[t]he state will continue to regulate hemp transportation within Nebraska" — Source: https://update.legislature.ne.gov/?p=36594 — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • NDA is the agency behind the proposed 19 NAC 5 rule (draft May 2026, hearing 18 June 2026) that would treat food, beverages, gummies and tinctures containing any amount of THC as adulterated under the Nebraska Pure Food Act, with an exception for the medical cannabis Acts; as of the September 2026 secondary update the rule had not been confirmed final — Source: https://horganlawfirm.com/delta-8-thc-and-cbd-in-nebraska-a-guide-for-businesses/ — accessed 2026-10-30. Proposed only — not law. Verify its current status against NDA / the Secretary of State's regulations before republication.
  • No NDA public contact address is stated here because no NDA contact page was opened directly in this run — Verify against nda.nebraska.gov before republication.

9.1.8 Department of Revenue — tax administration only (no cannabis-specific programme found)

  • The Department of Revenue administers Nebraska sales/use tax generally and the Marijuana and Controlled Substances Tax at Neb. Rev. Stat. § 77-4303 ($100 per ounce of marijuana), which remains on the books; its application to lawful medical possessors/licensees is Verify (see Run 1, Part 2) — Source: https://www.lawserver.com/law/state/nebraska/nebraska_statutes_77-4303 — accessed 2026-10-30 (secondary republication; official Legislature page for § 77-4303 not opened directly in this run — Verify).
  • No Department of Revenue medical-cannabis tax guidance, cannabis revenue figure or cannabis-specific contact page was located in this run — Unavailable, not zero revenue (see Run 1, Part 2: $0 official medical-cannabis tax/fee revenue because there are no sales). The introduced LB 1235 tax provisions (sales-tax credit to the School District Property Tax Relief Credit Fund; removal of lawful medical possessors from § 77-4303) were stripped from the enacted bill — Source: https://nebraskalegislature.gov/FloorDocs/109/PDF/Slip/LB1235.pdf — accessed 2026-10-30. Do not cite them as law.
  • The Governor's 26 Jan 2026 executive-order signing was attended by Department of Revenue Director Jim Kamm, alongside Agriculture Director Sherry Vinton and Banking and Finance Director Kelly Lammers — Source: https://governor.nebraska.gov/gov-pillen-joins-ag-hilgers-signs-order-addressing-illegal-recreational-synthetic-thc-industry — accessed 2026-10-30. The Department of Banking and Finance's role is limited to this executive-order review (tobacco-licence risk for retailers was also raised by the Attorney General) — Source: https://ago.nebraska.gov/news/announcement-new-developments-delta-8-enforcement-efforts — accessed 2026-10-30.

9.1.9 Nebraska State Patrol — controlled-substances enforcement and hemp transport

  • Under Neb. Rev. Stat. § 28-401(7), "Division of Drug Control means the personnel of the Nebraska State Patrol who are assigned to enforce the Uniform Controlled Substances Act" — Source: https://law.justia.com/codes/nebraska/chapter-28/statute-28-401/ — accessed 2026-10-30 (secondary republication — Verify against the official statute page).
  • Hemp transport: Neb. Rev. Stat. § 28-476 provides that no person shall carry or transport hemp in Nebraska unless it was produced in compliance with the Agriculture Improvement Act of 2018 and is carried/transported as provided in § 2-515, and that a peace officer may detain a person carrying hemp who does not provide the required documentation, with failure to produce documentation constituting probable cause to believe the hemp may be marijuana — Source: https://nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=28-476 — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • The State Patrol's role in MCC applicant background checks under enacted LB 1235 (criminal-history record information checks) was stated in Run 1 but the executing agency (State Patrol vs. FBI channel) was not confirmed from the slip law in this run — Verify. Fiscal-note references to State Patrol background-check fees relate to dead bills (LB 677, LB 705) and are not current law — Source: https://nebraskalegislature.gov/FloorDocs/109/PDF/FN/LB677_20260114-100817.pdf — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • No State Patrol public contact address is stated here because no State Patrol contact page was opened directly in this run — Verify against nsp.nebraska.gov before republication.

9.1.10 Courts — Lancaster County District Court and Nebraska Supreme Court

  • Lancaster County District Judge Susan Strong ruled 26 Nov 2024 that the 2024 petitions fulfilled state requirements, rejected (12 Dec 2024) a request to block the Governor's proclamation, and dismissed Kuehn v. Pillen on 26 June 2025 for lack of standing — Sources: https://www.marijuanamoment.net/nebraska-supreme-court-rejects-another-challenge-to-voter-approved-medical-marijuana-law/ — accessed 2026-10-30; https://kindgreenbuds.com/news/nebraskasupremecourt-unanimously-upholds-voter-approved-medical-marijuana-laws/ — accessed 2026-10-30. The 26 Nov 2024 and 12 Dec 2024 rulings rest on secondary reporting in this run — Verify against the Lancaster County docket before quoting holdings verbatim.
  • The Nebraska Supreme Court decided Kuehn v. Evnen, S-24-901, 322 Neb. 161, on 4 Sept 2026 (see §9.3), and affirmed the Kuehn v. Pillen dismissal in September 2026 (exact date and docket Verify) — Source: https://www.marijuanamoment.net/nebraska-supreme-court-rejects-another-challenge-to-voter-approved-medical-marijuana-law/ — accessed 2026-10-30.

Stat. § 28-416(14), provides: "(a) For the first offense, be guilty of an infraction, receive a citation, be fined three hundred dollars, and be assigned to attend a course as prescribed in section 29-433 if the judge determines that attending such course is in the best interest of the individual defendant; (b) For the second offense, be guilty of a Class IV misdemeanor, receive a citation, and be fined four hundred dollars and may be imprisoned not to exceed five days; and (c) For the third and all subsequent offenses, be guilty of a Class IIIA misdemeanor, receive a citation, be fined five hundred dollars, and be imprisoned not to exceed seven days." 9.3.2 The 2020 ruling that removed the earlier measure

  • Case: State ex rel. Wagner v. Evnen, 307 Neb. 142, 948 N.W.2d 244, No. S-20-623, Nebraska Supreme Court, filed 10 Sept 2020, writ of mandamus granted — Source: https://law.justia.com/cases/nebraska/supreme-court/2020/s-20-623.html — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Parties and posture: Relator Terry Wagner (Lancaster County Sheriff) objected to Secretary of State Evnen on 26 Aug 2020, claiming the Nebraska Medical Cannabis Constitutional Amendment (NMCCA) was legally insufficient; Evnen determined on 27 Aug 2020 that it had only one general subject and would not withhold it absent a court order; Wagner filed an original action on 28 Aug 2020 — Source: https://law.justia.com/cases/nebraska/supreme-court/2020/s-20-623.html — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Holding (verbatim operative language, from the opinion as reported): "As proposed, the [Nebraska Medical Cannabis Constitutional Amendment] contains more than one subject—by our count, it contains at least eight subjects. … In addition to enshrining in our constitution a right of certain persons to produce and medicinally use cannabis under subsections (1) and (2), in subsections (3) and (4), the NMCCA would enshrine a right and immunity for entities to grow and sell cannabis; and in subsections (6), (7), and (8), it would regulate the role of cannabis in at least six areas of public life. These secondary purposes are not naturally and necessarily connected to the NMCCA's primary purpose. As such, they constitute logrolling."; "If voters are to intelligently adopt a State policy with regard to medicinal cannabis use, they must first be allowed to decide that issue alone, unencumbered by other subjects."; "We reverse the Secretary of State's decision and issue a writ of mandamus directing him to withhold the initiative from the November 2020 general election ballot." — Sources: https://www.marijuanamoment.net/medical-marijuana-measure-removed-from-nebraskas-november-ballot-by-state-supreme-court/ — accessed 2026-10-30; https://marijuanaandthelaw.com/major-setback-medical-marijuana-nebraska/ — accessed 2026-10-30. The vote split was 5-2 (Justices Jonathan Papik and Lindsey Miller-Lerman dissenting, on the ground the decision "squeezed" the single-subject concept and diminished the people's initiative right) — Source: https://www.noiseomaha.com/news-now/2020/9/13/medical-marijuana-question-removed — accessed 2026-10-30. The dissent detail rests on secondary reporting — Verify against the slip opinion before quoting the dissent.
  • Effect: The measure, which had gathered sufficient signatures (over 182,000 submitted, secondary), did not appear on the 3 Nov 2020 ballot — Source: https://ballotpedia.org/Nebraska_Medical_Marijuana_Initiative_(2020) — accessed 2026-10-30. The 2024 campaign's split into two separate statutory petitions (437 patient protection, 438 regulation) was designed to avoid a repeat of this single-subject challenge — Source: https://www.marijuanamoment.net/nebraska-medical-marijuana-initiatives-will-not-appear-on-november-ballot-state-announces/ — accessed 2026-10-30.

9.3.3 The 2022 signature drive (failed)

  • The two 2022 initiatives failed to qualify and did not appear on the November 2022 ballot: 86,776 valid signatures were required statewide (7% of registered voters as of 7 July 2022) plus 5% in at least 38 of 93 counties; the Patient Protections initiative submitted 77,843 valid signatures (5% in 26 counties) and the Regulation initiative 77,119 (5% in 27 counties) — Source: https://sos.nebraska.gov/sites/sos.nebraska.gov/files/doc/news-releases/Media%20Release%20Medical%20Marijuana%20Initiatives.pdf — accessed 2026-10-30.

9.3.4 Litigation over the 2024 initiatives

  • Signature case — Kuehn v. Evnen, S-24-901, 322 Neb. 161 (4 Sept 2026), unanimous, opinion by Chief Justice Jeffrey Funke: the Court affirmed the Lancaster County District Court, holding opponents had not shown enough invalid petition signatures to disqualify either measure; the district court had declared the Legalization Petition contained 89,251 valid and legal signatures and the Regulatory Petition 89,030, after finding Kuehn and the Secretary "well short" of rebutting the presumption of validity for a material number of signatures — Sources: https://www.ne.gov/apps-courts-epub/public/viewAdvanced?docId=N00013455PUB — accessed 2026-10-30; https://kindgreenbuds.com/news/nebraskasupremecourt-unanimously-upholds-voter-approved-medical-marijuana-laws/ — accessed 2026-10-30. The district-court valid-signature figures (89,251 / 89,030) differ from the Secretary's certification figures (89,962 / 89,856) because they are the trial court's post-challenge counts, not a contradiction — Source: https://www.ne.gov/apps-courts-epub/public/viewAdvanced?docId=N00013455PUB — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Constitutional / preemption case — Kuehn v. Pillen: filed 10 Dec 2024, arguing the medical cannabis laws are preempted by federal law under the Supremacy Clause and that state funds should not be spent implementing them; Lancaster County District Judge Susan Strong dismissed it 26 June 2025 because Kuehn lacked standing, without reaching the preemption argument; the Nebraska Supreme Court heard oral argument 27 Apr 2026 and affirmed in September 2026, holding: "We conclude that Kuehn did not have either taxpayer standing or standing for a matter of great public concern to challenge the constitutionality of the acts. … We therefore affirm the district court's order dismissing Kuehn's operative complaint without prejudice." — Sources: https://kindgreenbuds.com/news/nebraskasupremecourt-unanimously-upholds-voter-approved-medical-marijuana-laws/ — accessed 2026-10-30; https://www.marijuanamoment.net/nebraska-supreme-court-rejects-another-challenge-to-voter-approved-medical-marijuana-law/ — accessed 2026-10-30. "Without prejudice" means a similar case could be brought if circumstances change; the Supreme Court did not rule on the merits of federal preemption in this case — Source: https://www.marijuanamoment.net/nebraska-supreme-court-rejects-another-challenge-to-voter-approved-medical-marijuana-law/ — accessed 2026-10-30. The Supreme Court docket number and exact September 2026 decision date for Kuehn v. Pillen were not confirmed from a court source in this run — Verify.

9.3.5 Later ballot measures — 2026 (no cannabis measure; election not yet held)

  • As of 26 Oct 2026, four statewide measures were certified for the 3 Nov 2026 ballot: Constitutional Amendment 1 (term limits — change the legislative term limit from two consecutive four-year terms to three), Initiative Measure 440 (authorise laws to govern sports betting), Initiative Measure 441 (authorise online sports betting and authorise the state gaming commission to adopt rules), and Initiative Measure 442 (require schools to designate sports teams as male, female or co-ed and prohibit male participation on female sports teams) — Source: https://ballotpedia.org/Nebraska_2026_ballot_measures — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • The same four-question list is given by the University of Nebraska–Lincoln School of Agricultural Social Sciences and Analytics: (1) term limits, (2) online sports betting authorisation plus implementing legislation, (3) a ban on transgender females participating on public school or college female sports teams — Source: https://sassa.unl.edu/2026-ballot-issues/ — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • No 2026 result is reported: the 3 Nov 2026 election had not been held on the research date (30 Oct 2026) — Source: https://ballotpedia.org/Nebraska_2026_ballot_measures — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Failed 2026 adult-use drive: Bill Hawkins of the Nebraska Hemp Company filed a constitutional amendment initiative on 18 Aug 2025 providing: "All persons twenty-one years of age or older have the right to use all plants in the genus Cannabis."; it required about 10% of registered voters (roughly 123,000–125,000) plus 5% in 38 of 93 counties by the 3 July 2026 deadline, and did not make the ballot; whether it submitted and fell short or did not submit at all is not stated in any source found, and no Secretary of State tally has been located — Verify against the Secretary of State — Sources: https://drugpolicywatch.info/states/nebraska.html — accessed 2026-10-30; https://www.cannabisbusinesstimes.com/legislation-and-regulation/adult-use-legalization-bill/news/15820227/7-states-that-could-still-legalize-cannabis-in-2026 — accessed 2026-10-30. This was the fifth recreational-cannabis initiative attempt since 2018, none of which qualified — Source: https://drugpolicywatch.info/states/nebraska.html — accessed 2026-10-30.

9.3.6 Public opinion polling (secondary only — pollster, date, sample stated; no official poll exists)

  • University of Nebraska–Lincoln, Nebraska Annual Social Indicators Survey (NASIS), Rural Drug Addiction Research Center, 2024 and 2026: 84% of Nebraskans surveyed supported legal medical cannabis in 2026, compared with 80% in 2024; 47% said they were in favour of legal marijuana for both medical and recreational use in 2026, compared with 44% in 2024; 15% said they used marijuana purchased legally out of state in 2026, compared with 11% in 2024; delta-8/other THC product use rose from 8% (2024) to 10% (2026); metropolitan areas (Omaha, Lincoln, Grand Island and Sioux City) showed 54% in favour, compared with 35% in micropolitan areas and 32% in all other counties — Source: https://research.unl.edu/blog/report-nebraskans-support-of-legal-cannabis-is-growing/ — accessed 2026-10-30. Sample size, margin of error and field dates were not stated on the UNL page opened — Unavailable in this run; do not invent them. Note: Sioux City in the UNL metropolitan grouping is in Iowa, not Nebraska; it is a survey geography, not a Nebraska jurisdiction.
  • Neilan Strategy Group / Data Targeting Inc., reported in 2024: 70% of respondents supported legalising medical cannabis in Nebraska; the poll surveyed 2,198 likely general election voters on 7–9 Feb (year given as 2024 in the 2024 campaign coverage), by cell phone and landline calls and texts, with a margin of error of ±2.1% — Source: https://panhandlepost.com/posts/d88e5e7a-31c9-4918-a73b-5cc3aecee9bf — accessed 2026-10-30. The exact field year is Verify against the original poll release before republication (the article itself is undated in the version opened).
  • 2022 polling claims — Verify, do not quote as firm: secondary 2022 coverage variously cites "some 77 percent" and "80% of registered voters" in favour of medical legalisation, without a pollster, field date or sample in the versions opened — Sources: http://norml.org/blog/2022/08/23/nebraska-advocates-fail-to-secure-ballot-access-for-medical-marijuana-legalization-measures/amp/ — accessed 2026-10-30; https://panhandlepost.com/posts/d88e5e7a-31c9-4918-a73b-5cc3aecee9bf — accessed 2026-10-30. These figures are Verify and are not adopted as polling results in this run.
  • No Nebraska state agency publishes an official opinion poll on cannabis — Unavailable as an official source; all polling above is secondary/university survey research.

Part 10 — Tribal and Interstate

PART 10 — TRIBAL AND INTERSTATE

10.0 Legal framework with the state — the short version

  • Nebraska was one of the original mandatory Public Law 280 states: the 1953 Act conferred jurisdiction on California, Minnesota, Nebraska, Oregon and Wisconsin over criminal offences and civil causes of action on Indian reservations, with Nebraska listed as "All Indian country within the State" — source (University of Nebraska–Lincoln teaching resource reproducing PL 280): https://teachinglegalhistory.unl.edu/s/oer/item/961 — accessed 2026-10-30. The Bureau of Indian Affairs likewise lists Nebraska as "All Indian Country within the state" for P.L. 280 purposes: https://www.bia.gov/mmujurisdiction — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Retrocession matters and is tribe-specific — do not state a single current rule for all tribes. The Nebraska Supreme Court in State v. Wabashaw, 274 Neb. 394 (2007), noted the Legislature offered retrocession of criminal and civil jurisdiction over the Santee Sioux Reservation in 2001, but it was not effective until the federal government accepted it, on 15 February 2006 — source (case summary): https://calculators.law/caselaw/decisions/Ay5Rkpopv8PK/state-v-wabashaw — accessed 2026-10-30. A University of Nebraska–Lincoln encyclopedia entry states the Omahas and later the Winnebagos "successfully won retrocession" and have since created tribal courts and police and completed cross-deputisation and concurrent-jurisdiction agreements — source: https://plainshumanities.unl.edu/encyclopedia/doc/egp.law.044.html — accessed 2026-10-30. Verify the current retrocession status and any cross-deputisation agreement tribe by tribe before relying on it for an enforcement question; this run did not open a federal retrocession notice for each tribe.
  • Consequence for this Part: tribal cannabis law is tribal law. It does not amend Nebraska state law, it does not create a state licence, and it does not protect possession once a person leaves tribal land and enters state jurisdiction. That last point is the one Nebraska's Governor and Attorney General have stressed publicly (see §10.1), and it is consistent with the state possession penalties in Run 1.
  • No Nebraska–tribal cannabis compact was located in this run. The only state–tribal compact dispute found is about tobacco tax, not cannabis (see §10.1). If the parent compiler needs a compact, the follow-up is a direct check with the Governor's office / Attorney General's office and each tribal council — marked Unavailable, not proven non-existent, except that no compact text was found on the official sites opened.
  • No Nebraska interstate cannabis agreement was located in this run (no reciprocity agreement, no import/export agreement, no border-state cannabis memorandum). See §10.7.

10.1 Omaha Tribe of Nebraska — Title 51 (the only Nebraska tribal cannabis code found)

  • Adoption, unanimous, immediate effect (secondary, dated, corroborated across multiple outlets). On 15 July 2025 the Omaha Tribal Council unanimously adopted Title 51 — Cannabis Regulatory Code, the first fully legal and regulated medical and adult-use cannabis system on tribal land in Nebraska, effective immediately — sources: Nebraska Examiner reporting republished 16 July 2025: https://cannabiscultivatornews.com/home/index.php/2025/07/16/indian-tribe-in-nebraska-votes-to-legalize-marijuana-for-recreational-and-medical-use-effective-immediately/ — accessed 2026-10-30; Marijuana Herald, 15 July 2025: https://themarijuanaherald.com/2025/07/omaha-tribe-becomes-first-in-nebraska-to-establish-a-legal-recreational-and-medical-marijuana-program/ — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Official tribal confirmation that Title 51 exists and that a Cannabis Regulatory Commission implements it. The Omaha Tribe's own site states it is "now accepting applications from qualified individuals to serve on the Omaha Tribe Cannabis Regulatory Commission, the governing body responsible for implementing and overseeing Title 51, our new cannabis law," and that "The Commission will play a critical role in licensing, regulation, enforcement, and community education as we move forward with creating a safe, legal, and sovereign cannabis industry on the Omaha Reservation" — source (official tribal site, opened directly): https://www.omahatribe.com/now-accepting-applications-omaha-tribe-cannabis-regulatory-commission/ — accessed 2026-10-30. Applicants must be at least 21 — same source, accessed 2026-10-30.
  • VERBATIM OPERATIVE TEXT — NOT LOCATED IN THIS RUN (Verify). The full text of Title 51 was not found on the Omaha Tribe's published tribal-codes page as opened via search (that page lists titles through Title 50 in the excerpt, with a note that codes are "currently under review by the Attorney General's office and are subject to change") — source: https://www.omahatribe.com/tribal-codes/ — accessed 2026-10-30. The Nebraska Examiner was "supplied [a] copy of Title 51" according to secondary reporting, but that copy is not linked in the sources opened here. Therefore no Title 51 passage is quoted verbatim in this report. Every Title 51 detail below is secondary paraphrase and must be re-checked against the tribal text before republication as a quote.
  • What secondary reporting consistently says Title 51 does (all secondary, dated July–October 2025 — Verify against the tribal text):
  • Adults 21 and older may possess or purchase up to one ounce of cannabis on Omaha Tribe lands, unless a different amount is approved by the regulatory commission — source (Nebraska Examiner republication): https://cannabiscultivatornews.com/home/index.php/2025/07/16/indian-tribe-in-nebraska-votes-to-legalize-marijuana-for-recreational-and-medical-use-effective-immediately/ — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • The code establishes extensive licensing, advertising, testing, enforcement, education and seed-to-sale tracking requirements — same source, accessed 2026-10-30.
  • The commission may create incentives for tribal members or set different possession/purchase limits for members vs non-members — same source, accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Excise taxes of up to 15% retail on gross sales price to consumers and up to 5% wholesale/transfer on fair market value between licensees or from producers to retailers — same source, accessed 2026-10-30.
  • A portion of tax revenue may support low-income patients or a Cannabis Patient Access Fund, prioritising tribal members with chronic or life-limiting conditions, youth, elders, veterans in need of compassionate care, and patients underserved by state or federal systems — same source, accessed 2026-10-30.
  • A pathway for expungement of prior tribal cannabis offences — source (Marijuana Herald, 15 July 2025): https://themarijuanaherald.com/2025/07/omaha-tribe-becomes-first-in-nebraska-to-establish-a-legal-recreational-and-medical-marijuana-program/ — accessed 2026-10-30. Whether that expungement reaches state convictions is not stated in the sources opened — do not present it as state expungement (see Part 12, §12.4).
  • Adult-use sales are open to non-tribal visitors 21+ on reservation land, not restricted to tribal members — source (Marijuana Herald, 7 Oct 2025): https://themarijuanaherald.com/2025/10/nebraska-omaha-tribe-attorney-general-says-first-cannabis-store-to-open-for-everyone-21-in-early-2026/ — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Commission and first licence (secondary, dated). The five-member Cannabis Regulatory Commission was sworn in / held its first meeting in October 2025 (one secondary source dates the swearing-in 27 October 2025; another reports the first meeting 27 October 2025) and issued a first vertical licence to the tribe itself at that meeting, covering cultivation, processing and sales — sources: https://themarijuanaherald.com/2025/10/omaha-tribe-cannabis-license/ — accessed 2026-10-30; https://cannabisnebraska.org/ — accessed 2026-10-30. The commission was reported to meet monthly on the third Wednesday — source: https://themarijuanaherald.com/2025/10/omaha-tribe-cannabis-license/ — accessed 2026-10-30. Verify the licence holder name and licence number against tribal commission minutes; no tribal minutes were opened in this run.
  • Operating status — NOT YET OPEN as of the latest dated reporting in this run (Verify against a post-October 2026 tribal announcement). Early reporting projected a store in Q1–Q2 2026; Nebraska Public Media later reported the tribe anticipated having a dispensary, cultivation and testing facility "up and running by the end of 2026" — source: https://nebraskapublicmedia.org/en/news/news-articles/nebraska-tribe-moving-forward-with-medical-marijuana-despite-state-pushback/ — accessed 2026-10-30. MJBizDaily (2 Mar 2026) likewise reported the tribe "on track to open a dispensary 'by the end of 2026'" — source: https://mjbizdaily.com/native-tribe-on-track-to-be-first-nebraska-medical-marijuana-operator/614782/ — accessed 2026-10-30. No source opened in this run confirms a tribal dispensary actually opened for sales by 30 Oct 2026. The parent compiler must not state that Omaha tribal sales are operating without a newer tribal announcement. Note the tension in the secondary record: the tribe's initial focus was reported as medical first, recreational later — source: https://nebraskapublicmedia.org/en/news/news-articles/nebraska-tribe-moving-forward-with-medical-marijuana-despite-state-pushback/ — accessed 2026-10-30 — even though Title 51 legalises both.
  • State response (secondary, dated October 2025). Governor Jim Pillen and Attorney General Mike Hilgers said buyers on tribal land do so "at their own peril" and that the state would take steps to keep Nebraskans from buying recreational marijuana on the reservation; Hilgers called the tribal scheme unlawful under Nebraska law because products would be sold to non-citizens in violation of state statute — sources: https://www.marijuanamoment.net/nebraska-attorney-general-calls-marijuana-a-poison-and-says-people-who-buy-it-from-a-tribe-within-the-state-do-so-at-their-own-peril/ — accessed 2026-10-30; https://www.marijuanamoment.net/nebraska-tribe-says-state-officials-are-punishing-it-for-legalizing-marijuana-by-suspending-talks-on-separate-tobacco-tax-deal/ — accessed 2026-10-30. The tribe's Attorney General, John Cartier, called the suspension of separate tobacco-tax-compact negotiations retaliation — same Marijuana Moment source, accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Tobacco compact context (not a cannabis compact — do not conflate). The Omaha Tribe sought 90% of tobacco tax revenue under a proposed compact; the Santee Sioux Nation already has an agreement retaining 75% — source: https://www.marijuanamoment.net/nebraska-tribe-says-state-officials-are-punishing-it-for-legalizing-marijuana-by-suspending-talks-on-separate-tobacco-tax-deal/ — accessed 2026-10-30. Hilgers' position, as reported, was that no tribe has a right to a compact and the 90% ask gave the state no material benefit — source: http://1041thewolf.com/news/180081-nebraska-attorney-general-responds-to-accusations-of-retaliation-against-omaha-tribe-over-medical-marijuana/ — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Omaha hemp plan (official, separate from Title 51). USDA lists "Omaha Tribe of Nebraska | Approved Plan (pdf)*" — source (USDA AMS list, updated 17 Feb 2026, opened directly): https://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/state-and-tribal-plan-review — accessed 2026-10-30. The asterisk means approved under the final rule effective 22 Mar 2021 — same source, accessed 2026-10-30. No Omaha tribal hemp operation count or licence list was opened in this run — Unavailable.

10.2 Winnebago Tribe of Nebraska — USDA hemp plan (verified, with verbatim text)

  • *USDA status: Approved Plan (pdf), on the USDA list updated 17 February 2026 — source (opened directly): https://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/state-and-tribal-plan-review — accessed 2026-10-30. This confirms the Run 1 Part 5 finding.
  • Plan document opened directly: https://ams-prod.azureedge.us/sites/default/files/media/WinnebagoTribeNebraskaPlanAndCertification.pdf — accessed 2026-10-30. Key verbatim passages:
  • Original approval: "in accordance with the Interim Final Rule, the USDA approved the Tribe's Hemp Plan on May 7, 2020" and Resolution #20-91 of 3 February 2020 approved the plan for submission — same PDF, accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Amending resolution: Resolution #24-181, adopted 21 August 2024 by 5 for, 0 against, 0 abstentions, "adopts the amendments to the Winnebago Tribe of Nebraska Hemp Plan as set forth in the attachment hereto and approves the same for submission to the USDA" and "the amendments shall become effective immediately" — same PDF, accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Jurisdiction: "For purposes of this Hemp Plan, the Tribe's jurisdiction includes all lands within the exterior boundaries of the Winnebago Indian Reservation, and Indian country as defined in 18 U.S.C. § 1151 (a), (b), and (c)." — same PDF, accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Transport: "The Tribe may not prohibit the transportation or shipment of lawfully produced hemp through its jurisdiction." — same PDF, accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Hemp definition in the plan: "'Hemp' means the plant Cannabis sativa L. and any part of that plant, including the seeds and all derivatives, extracts, cannabinoids, isomers, acids, salts, and salts of isomers, whether growing or not, with a total THC concentration of not more that 0.3% on a dry weight basis, or as otherwise defined in federal law." [sic — "more that" in the PDF text] — same PDF, accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Licence term: "A License is valid for one (1) year from the date of issuance by the Winnebago Hemp Program. Licenses are non-transferable and are not permitted for resale." — same PDF (via search excerpt of the PDF), accessed 2026-10-30. Verify the licence-fee schedule and current licensee list against the Tribe's Hemp Oversight and Licensing Commission; neither was opened in this run.
  • Programme body: Title 8, Article 3 of the Winnebago Tribal Code establishes the Winnebago Hemp Program, directed by the Hemp Oversight Licensing Commission under § 8-304 — same PDF, accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Commercial hemp product (secondary, dated 12 May 2021). Winnebago Agriculture & Industry, a Ho-Chunk Inc. subsidiary, launched "Niota," a smokable hemp preroll made with CBD smokable flower, described as the Tribe's first hemp product; Ho-Chunk Farms and the Tribe were among Nebraska's first hemp producers in the 2019 state pilot — source: https://www.einpresswire.com/article_print/540860653/winnebago-tribe-launches-hemp-product — accessed 2026-10-30. Current availability of Niota in 2026 is Verify — no 2026 product listing was opened.
  • No Winnebago tribal marijuana (non-hemp cannabis) ordinance was located in this run. Do not infer one from the hemp plan.

10.3 Santee Sioux Nation (Nebraska) — hemp plan approved; do NOT conflate with Flandreau

  • USDA status: "Santee Sioux Nation | Approved Plan (pdf)" on the USDA list updated 17 February 2026 — source (opened directly): https://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/state-and-tribal-plan-review — accessed 2026-10-30. The direct PDF link for the Santee Sioux Nation plan was not exposed in the text fetch — Verify via the USDA page's PDF link; do not substitute the Flandreau plan.
  • Flandreau Santee Sioux Tribe is a DIFFERENT tribe in South Dakota — keep apart. USDA lists "Flandreau Santee Sioux | Approved Plan (pdf)" separately — same USDA source, accessed 2026-10-30. The 2019 federal case Flandreau Santee Sioux Tribe v. United States Department of Agriculture, No. 19-cv-04094 (D.S.D.), in which a preliminary injunction to force USDA action on a hemp plan was denied 6 June 2019, concerns the Flandreau (South Dakota) tribe, not the Nebraska Santee Sioux Nation — source (secondary case summary): https://www.jdsupra.com/post/contentViewerEmbed.aspx?fid=4804e6b2-f62f-439c-b3a1-342791e29deb — accessed 2026-10-30. Verify the docket against PACER / the district court before citing the case number in republication; it is taken from a secondary summary here.
  • No Nebraska Santee Sioux Nation marijuana ordinance or dispensary was located in this run. The well-known tribal dispensary "Native Nations Cannabis" is Flandreau's, in South Dakota (see §10.8), not Nebraska's Santee Sioux Nation.
  • Retrocession note: see §10.0 — Santee Sioux retrocession effective 15 February 2006 per Wabashaw — source: https://calculators.law/caselaw/decisions/Ay5Rkpopv8PK/state-v-wabashaw — accessed 2026-10-30.

10.4 Ponca Tribe of Nebraska — no cannabis or hemp programme located

  • No Ponca tribal cannabis ordinance, no Ponca tribal marijuana dispensary, and no Ponca USDA hemp plan were located in this run. The USDA list updated 17 February 2026 contains no Ponca entry — source (opened directly): https://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/state-and-tribal-plan-review — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • The Ponca Tribe's published Law & Order Code (official tribal site) lists titles including General Provisions, Civil Actions, Housing Authority, Landlord and Tenant, Taxation, Business Entities, Liquor Control, Environment and Health, with no cannabis/hemp title visible in the table of contents excerpt — source: https://www.poncatribe-ne.gov/?p=815 — accessed 2026-10-30. Verify by a full-text search of the current code PDF before stating categorically that no cannabis provision exists anywhere in Ponca law; this run searched titles and web results, not the full code text.
  • Ponca's situation differs structurally from the other three Nebraska tribes in that its land base is trust parcels and a service-delivery area rather than a single large reservation — that structural point is Verify against the Tribe's official site / BIA before republication; it is noted here only to explain why a reservation-wide cannabis code would be less straightforward, not as a confirmed legal conclusion.

10.5 Tribes with land extending into Nebraska — Iowa Tribe and Sac and Fox Nation (keep the two Sac & Fox entities apart)

  • Iowa Tribe of Kansas and Nebraska — USDA Approved Plan (pdf). USDA list, updated 17 February 2026 — source (opened directly): https://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/state-and-tribal-plan-review — accessed 2026-10-30. The Iowa Tribe's reservation straddles Kansas and Nebraska; its plan is a tribal plan, not a Nebraska state plan. No Iowa Tribe marijuana ordinance was located in this run. The Kansas Department of Agriculture confirms the framework: hemp on land belonging to the Iowa Tribe of Kansas and Nebraska (among others) "is regulated by each respective Tribal Nation or USDA Agricultural Marketing Service (USDA-AMS)" — source: https://portal.kda.ks.gov/Hemp/HempSearch/LicenseSearch — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Sac and Fox Nation of Missouri in Kansas and Nebraska — NO USDA Approved Plan on the February 2026 list (Verify). The Kansas page quoted above also names "the Sac and Fox Nation of Missouri in Kansas and Nebraska" as a tribe whose land is regulated by the tribe or USDA-AMS — same Kansas source, accessed 2026-10-30. But the USDA list does not show that entity as an Approved Plan; the similarly named entity on the list is "Sac & Fox Tribe of the Mississippi in Iowa | Approved Plan (pdf) *" — source (USDA list, opened directly): https://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/state-and-tribal-plan-review — accessed 2026-10-30. These are different tribes. Do not transfer the Iowa entity's approved plan to the Missouri/Kansas/Nebraska entity. Whether Sac and Fox Nation of Missouri in Kansas and Nebraska producers are licensed directly by USDA (USDA Hemp Producer License) is Verify via the USDA Public Search Tool; no count was obtained in this run.
  • No marijuana ordinance was located in this run for either the Iowa Tribe or the Sac and Fox Nation of Missouri in Kansas and Nebraska.

Iowa Tribe of Kansas and Nebraska Approved Plan (pdf) No No
Sac and Fox Nation of Missouri in Kansas and Nebraska None listed as Approved Plan (Verify USDA direct licensing) No No

Sources for the table: USDA list https://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/state-and-tribal-plan-review — accessed 2026-10-30; Omaha sources in §10.1; Winnebago PDF https://ams-prod.azureedge.us/sites/default/files/media/WinnebagoTribeNebraskaPlanAndCertification.pdf — accessed 2026-10-30.

10.7 Interstate — hemp shipments

  • Nebraska's own hemp transport statute (opened directly, Neb. Rev. Stat. § 2-515, as amended by Laws 2024, LB 262, § 5). Verbatim operative provisions:
  • "(1) Except as provided in subsection (3) of this section, any USDA-licensed hemp producer or state-program-licensed hemp producer transporting hemp shall carry with the hemp being transported a copy of the USDA license or state program license under which it was cultivated and a copy of the test results pertaining to each lot of hemp being transported." — source: https://nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=2-515 — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • "(4) Any person who is carrying or transporting hemp who is not a USDA-licensed hemp producer or state-program-licensed hemp producer shall only carry or transport hemp if such hemp meets the following requirements: (a) The hemp is carried or transported with a bill of lading stating the owner of the hemp, the point of origin of the hemp, and the destination of the hemp; (b) The hemp is carried or transported with a copy of the valid USDA or state program license under which the hemp was cultivated; (c) The hemp is carried or transported with a copy of the test results pertaining to each lot of hemp being transported; and (d) The hemp is not unloaded or in any way removed from the vehicle transporting such hemp unless authorized by state or federal law enforcement." — same source, accessed 2026-10-30.
  • "(5) No person shall transport or carry hemp in this state concurrently with any other plant material that is not hemp." — same source, accessed 2026-10-30.
  • A producer under a USDA-approved tribal programme cultivating in Nebraska must keep shipment records showing date, origin, destination, sender, receiver and vehicle identification number — § 2-515(2), same source, accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Peace-officer detention (Neb. Rev. Stat. § 28-476, via search excerpt of the official statute page). "No person shall carry or transport hemp in this state unless such hemp is: (a) Produced in compliance with the requirements of the Agriculture Improvement Act of 2018, as such act is defined in section 2-503; and (b) Carried or transported as provided in section 2-515" and a peace officer may detain a person who does not provide the required documentation, but must immediately release hemp and person on production of it unless there is probable cause to believe the hemp is or is being carried with marijuana or another controlled substance — source: https://nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=28-476 — accessed 2026-10-30. Verify the full § 28-476 text (including the Class IV misdemeanor / $1,000 fine for intentional violation stated in the excerpt) against the opened statute page before quoting the penalty.
  • Tribal-plan transport rule (Winnebago, verbatim, §10.2): "The Tribe may not prohibit the transportation or shipment of lawfully produced hemp through its jurisdiction." — source: https://ams-prod.azureedge.us/sites/default/files/media/WinnebagoTribeNebraskaPlanAndCertification.pdf — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • What this does NOT do: compliance with hemp transport paperwork does not make marijuana transport lawful. Hemp that exceeds the THC definition is marijuana under Nebraska law (Run 1, § 2-503 delta-9 definition), and the federal total-THC redefinition after 11 December 2026 (see baseline) will change what counts as hemp federally — shippers must re-check the definition in force on the shipment date. Verify Nebraska's conforming response to the 12 November / 11 December 2026 federal dates; none was located in this run (carried forward from Run 1 as Verify).
  • No Nebraska interstate hemp agreement or compact was located in this run (no memorandum with Colorado, Missouri, Kansas, Iowa, South Dakota or Wyoming on hemp transport, testing reciprocity or enforcement).

10.8 Interstate — Nebraska residents buying in Colorado, Missouri, South Dakota tribal lands and other states; what can legally be brought into Nebraska

  • The governing Nebraska rule is possession, not purchase location. Adult-use marijuana is illegal under Nebraska state law (Run 1 baseline). A Nebraska resident who lawfully buys adult-use cannabis in Colorado or Missouri (both sell to adults 21+ with valid ID — secondary visitor guide, 25 Oct 2026) and brings it into Nebraska is possessing marijuana in Nebraska, subject to Nebraska's possession penalties (Run 1: ≤1 oz first offence infraction $300, escalating on repeats; >1 oz–1 lb Class III misdemeanor; >1 lb Class IV felony) — sources for the visitor-sale point (secondary, dated): https://markets.financialcontent.com/stocks/article/marketersmedia-2026-10-25-alexis-420-publishes-a-50-state-guide-to-where-tourists-can-legally-buy-weed-ahead-of-holiday-travel — accessed 2026-10-30. Verify Colorado and Missouri visitor-purchase rules against those states' official regulator pages before republication; they are used here only for the non-law context that purchase is possible there.
  • Federal overlay (supplied baseline + general federal principle, secondary for the transport point): carrying cannabis across a state line is a federal offence even between two states that allow it — same secondary visitor guide, accessed 2026-10-30. Do not present cross-border transport as federally lawful because both endpoints allow sale.
  • Qualified patients — the one state-law protection, and its limit (Verify on importation). Neb. Rev. Stat. § 71-24,105(1)(a) (opened in Run 1) provides it "shall not be an offense under state or local law for [a] qualified patient to use, possess, and acquire an allowable amount of cannabis" (up to 5 oz). The section as quoted in Run 1 does not on its face limit the source of acquisition to a Nebraska licensee, and no Nebraska dispensary was operating as of 30 Oct 2026 — but no court ruling or Attorney General opinion located in this run holds that importing cannabis from another state or tribal land is protected, and transport itself may be charged separately. Mark the importation question Verify; do not advise that a patient may lawfully import. This is the same Verify carried from Run 1's federal-position discussion, now stated for the state-law side as well.
  • South Dakota tribal lands — Flandreau, not Nebraska. The Flandreau Santee Sioux Tribe's Native Nations Cannabis dispensary in Flandreau, South Dakota, began medical sales 1 July 2021, required a tribal medical card / doctor's recommendation, and accepted licences from other states' programmes according to contemporaneous reporting — source: https://www.sdpb.org/news/2021-07-01/tribe-becomes-south-dakotas-first-medical-marijuana-seller — accessed 2026-10-30. South Dakota's Highway Patrol position at the time, and the Governor's office position that tribal cards would not be acknowledged for non-enrolled members, created a documented border risk — source (secondary, 1 July 2021): https://7d53629b-cbbf-40a1-9516-a3f91b08fe10.p.creative-connect-beta.gannettdigital.com/story/news/2021/07/01/south-dakota-medical-marijuana-weed-legalized-dispensary-live-flandreau-santee-sioux-tribe/7812832002/ — accessed 2026-10-30. A Nebraska resident buying at Flandreau and driving back into Nebraska faces the same Nebraska possession analysis as a Colorado/Missouri purchase, plus the South Dakota tribal/state recognition dispute on the South Dakota side. Current (2026) Flandreau purchase rules for non-residents are Verify — the sources opened are 2021–2022.
  • Omaha NE vs Council Bluffs IA, and South Sioux City NE vs Sioux City IA — kept apart. Council Bluffs is in Iowa, across the Missouri River from Omaha, Nebraska; Sioux City is in Iowa, across the Missouri River from South Sioux City, Nebraska. Iowa has no adult-use sales and no state-licensed store selling to Nebraska visitors (secondary visitor guide lists Iowa among states with no visitor sales) — source: https://markets.financialcontent.com/stocks/article/marketersmedia-2026-10-25-alexis-420-publishes-a-50-state-guide-to-where-tourists-can-legally-buy-weed-ahead-of-holiday-travel — accessed 2026-10-30. No fact in this Part about Omaha, Nebraska, refers to Council Bluffs, Iowa, and no fact about South Sioux City, Nebraska, refers to Sioux City, Iowa.
  • Out-of-state buying volume (Run 1, carried forward unchanged): UNL survey (secondary, dated 22 July 2026 in Run 1): 11% of Nebraskans surveyed in 2024 vs 15% in 2026 reported using marijuana purchased legally out of state; delta-8 use 8% → 10%. No official Nebraska volume or spending figure was located in this run either — Unavailable as an official figure.
  • What CAN legally be brought into Nebraska (summary): (a) hemp meeting the Nebraska/federal definition in force on the shipment date, with § 2-515 / § 28-476 documentation; (b) FDA-approved drug products; (c) nothing else cannabis-derived is affirmatively authorised for import by a Nebraska statute located in this run — qualified-patient possession up to 5 oz is protected as possession under § 71-24,105, subject to the importation Verify above. Adult-use marijuana, and any marijuana above a qualified patient's allowable amount, may not lawfully be brought in under state law.

10.9 Past disputes with neighbouring states — Nebraska and Oklahoma v. Colorado

  • Case: Nebraska v. Colorado (with Oklahoma as co-plaintiff), U.S. Supreme Court, original jurisdiction — filed December 2014 as a motion for leave to file a bill of complaint — sources: https://www.abajournal.com/news/article/supreme_court_wont_allow_states_challenge_to_colorados_legalization_of_pot — accessed 2026-10-30; https://www.studicata.com/summaries/united-states-supreme-court/nebraska-v-colorado-2016-7tr0zx/ — accessed 2026-10-30. Docket number Verify — not stated in the sources opened; do not cite a number from memory.
  • Claim: Colorado's Amendment 64 (recreational legalisation) facilitated violations of federal drug law and increased trafficking into Nebraska and Oklahoma, imposing costs on their law enforcement, judicial and penal systems; the states sought a declaration that the federal Controlled Substances Act pre-empted Colorado's licensing, regulation and taxation provisions and an injunction blocking them — source: https://www.studicata.com/summaries/united-states-supreme-court/nebraska-v-colorado-2016-7tr0zx/ — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Outcome, 21 March 2016: the Supreme Court denied the motion for leave to file the bill of complaint, without explanation, ending the action at the pleading stage in the Supreme Court; Justices Thomas and Alito dissented from the denial — sources: https://www.abajournal.com/news/article/supreme_court_wont_allow_states_challenge_to_colorados_legalization_of_pot — accessed 2026-10-30; https://www.cannabisbusinesstimes.com/us-states/colorado/news/15701688/supreme-court-rejects-neb-okla-cannabis-suit-against-colo — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • What the denial did NOT decide: it was not a holding that Colorado's scheme is lawful. Nebraska Attorney General Doug Peterson's contemporaneous statement, as reported, was: "the Supreme Court has not held that Colorado's unconstitutional facilitation of marijuana industrialization is legal" and the decision "does not bar additional challenges to Colorado's scheme in federal district court" — source: https://www.cannabisbusinesstimes.com/us-states/colorado/news/15701688/supreme-court-rejects-neb-okla-cannabis-suit-against-colo — accessed 2026-10-30. No follow-on Nebraska district-court challenge to Colorado's scheme was located in this run — if the parent compiler states none was filed, mark that Verify; this run searched web results only, not a full federal docket sweep.
  • No other Nebraska interstate cannabis lawsuit was located in this run (no suit against Missouri, Kansas, Iowa, South Dakota or Wyoming over cannabis).

10.10 Interstate laws and agreements — bottom line

  • Nebraska statutes located for interstate purposes: § 2-515 (hemp transport, §10.7) and § 28-476 (hemp carry/transport, §10.7). No Nebraska statute creating medical-cannabis reciprocity, adult-use import/export, or a tribal cannabis compact was located.
  • Federal law located for interstate purposes (supplied baseline + generally applicable principle): state-licensed medical = Schedule III from 28 Apr 2026; adult-use = Schedule I; interstate transport of marijuana remains federally unlawful; hemp produced in compliance with the 2018 Farm Bill framework is the federal transport exception, subject to the post-11 Dec 2026 redefinition.

Part 11 — Operations Rules

PART 11 — OPERATIONS RULES

Scope warning, repeated for every subsection below: subsection (a) medical-cannabis rules are taken from the 21 August 2026 Title 238, Chapter 1 DRAFT opened directly (https://mcc.nebraska.gov/sites/default/files/doc/Revised%20-%20238%20NAC%201%20DRAFT.8.21.final.pdf — accessed 2026-10-30) unless stated otherwise. They are not presented as the final permanent text. Subsection (b) hemp rules are the current USDA programme plus the surviving Nebraska statutes (§§2-503, 2-505, 2-515); the former NDA operating rules were repealed by LB 262 and are historical only. Practical status overlay for all of (a): with 0 dispensaries and 0 manufacturers licensed and no sale yet (Run 1 baseline, confirmed above), most medical operations rules below have no operating licensee to apply to yet — they describe the regulatory design, not observed practice. The testing bottleneck is the clearest example: see §11.1(a).

11.1 Testing — required tests, sampling, failed tests/recalls, labs

(a) Medical cannabis licensees (draft Title 238, Chapter 1, §14 unless stated).

  • Who must test, and when. Cultivators: “A representative sample of each batch or lot of medical cannabis shall be tested by an independent cannabis testing laboratory” before transport to a product manufacturer (testing may occur before or after transfer). Concentrate must be tested by an independent laboratory before incorporation into a derivative product. Manufacturers: before transfer of a product to a dispensary, “a product manufacturer shall have a representative sample of the product tested by an independent laboratory.” Mycotoxin testing is required for medical cannabis, products and concentrate (§§14.01–14.03). Source: https://mcc.nebraska.gov/sites/default/files/doc/Revised%20-%20238%20NAC%201%20DRAFT.8.21.final.pdf — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Required tests (Table 1, §14.05). For medical cannabis: moisture content, water activity, foreign matter, potency, microbial, pesticides and heavy metals are Required; residual solvents are not (marked X). For concentrate and for product: moisture/water activity are X for concentrate and Required for product in the table as rendered (the table rendering is garbled — Verify Table 1 against a clean copy), foreign matter, potency, microbial, pesticides, residual solvents and heavy metals are Required. Mycotoxin is required separately by §14.03. Source: https://mcc.nebraska.gov/sites/default/files/doc/Revised%20-%20238%20NAC%201%20DRAFT.8.21.final.pdf — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Pass/fail standards (summarised; full action-level tables not reproduced). Water activity greater than 0.65 fails (§14.11). Foreign matter visible to the unaided eye, harmful microscopic matter above 3% of sample mass, intentionally added matter, or (for products) seeds above net weight ÷ 1.75 fails (§14.12). Potency fails if any Table 2 analyte is not analysed or exceeds its action level; the main cannabinoids (delta-9 THC, delta-8 THC, THCA, THCV, CBD, CBDA, CBDV, CBN, CBG, CBC, CBGA, CBCA) are listed “No Limit,” while THC-OAc above 1% of total cannabinoid peak area (concentrate) or 0.5% (product), any synthetic cannabinoid substance, or unknown cannabinoids above 2% of total peak area, fail (§14.13). Microbial, pesticide (Table 4), residual-solvent, heavy-metal and mycotoxin failures are defined by their tables (§§14.14–14.18) — Verify Tables 3–7 action levels against a clean copy; this track did not reproduce them because the PDF rendering interleaves table columns. Source: https://mcc.nebraska.gov/sites/default/files/doc/Revised%20-%20238%20NAC%201%20DRAFT.8.21.final.pdf — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Sampling rules. The requesting establishment must make the entire lot/batch available, contained in a single location and physically separated from other lots/batches (§14.06(A)). The sample “shall be collected by a sampling technician who is unaffiliated with the entity that requested testing,” at the establishment where the product is grown or manufactured, and the owner/employees/agents “may not assist in the selection of the sample” (§14.06(B)). Collection must be ISO 17025-compliant, with sterile gloves/instruments/containers, tamper-proof tape and labelling (§14.06(C)). Minimum plant-lot samples: 10 × ~1 g subunits (≤5 kg), 16 (5.01–9 kg), 22 (9.01–14 kg), 28 (14.01–18 kg), 32 (18.01–23 kg) (§14.07). Concentrate: 10 mL/g (≤1 L/kg) or 20 mL/g (≤4 L/kg) (§14.08). Final product: at least 10 g total, 4 units (5–500 products), 6 (501–1,000), 8 (1,001–5,000), 10 (5,001–10,000) (§14.09). A “sampling technician” is defined (§02.30) as a Commission employee/agent, an employee of an independent laboratory that performs sampling, or a Commission-authorised person. Source: https://mcc.nebraska.gov/sites/default/files/doc/Revised%20-%20238%20NAC%201%20DRAFT.8.21.final.pdf — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Failed tests, remediation and recalls. A cultivator or manufacturer “may remediate” a lot/batch/product failing any required adulterant standard “after submitting and gaining approval for a remediation plan from the Commission” (§14.04). The plan must be submitted “within fifteen (15) calendar days of the receipt of a failed testing result,” and the lot/batch must be prepared for resampling “within sixty (60) calendar days of Commission approval”; resampling/retesting “is not permitted until the lot or batch has been remediated” (§14.04(A)). Verbatim: “If a cannabis lot or batch is retested and fails three total times, then any product that was either sold from the cannabis lot or batch or manufactured from that lot or batch shall be subject to a mandatory recall” (§14.04(A)(ii)). A lot/batch that “is not or cannot be remediated in the specified time shall be rendered unusable and unrecognizable and destroyed according to this Chapter” (§14.04(B)). Voluntary recalls by a cultivator (§12.10), manufacturer (§13.10) or dispensary (§11.14) each require notice to the Commission “within twenty-four (24) hours” of issuing the recall.
