Test results and certificates of analysis must be kept, a copy kept on the licensed premises, and copies furnished to the Commission on request (§14.05). Source: https://mcc.nebraska.gov/sites/default/files/doc/Revised%20-%20238%20NAC%201%20DRAFT.8.21.final.pdf — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Labs — and the missing licence route (verified in the draft). Testing must be by an “independent cannabis testing laboratory” (§14.01) and, for product registration, by “a testing facility accredited under [ISO/IEC] standard 17025:2017” whose accreditation scope “shall include all cannabis product testing required by this chapter” (§13.07(C)). A certificate of analysis (§02.08) must list analyte quantities, the laboratory's name, address, authorised signatory's name and dated signature, representative contact information, and ISO 17025 / IEC accreditations. No testing-laboratory licence type, application chapter or fee appears in the draft chapters opened (§§03–07 cover dispensary, cultivator and manufacturer applications and renewals only; transport is a manufacturer certification, §02.35). That confirms the Run 1 flag: testing is required but has no licence route in the draft — Verify against the final permanent text and against any Commission laboratory list or accreditation requirement published separately; no Commission list of approved labs was located in this track (Unavailable, not “no labs exist”). Cultivators and manufacturers may also permit a testing lab to collect samples “for testing and research for developmental purposes” (§§12.08, 13.05) — developmental sampling is not a consumer-sampling programme.
  • Practical bottleneck (secondary, October 2026). Secondary reporting of an October 2026 Commission meeting states cultivators were warned they face “at least one more year, and possibly two, before receiving guidelines on how to test and sell their product,” and that “A grower can't move product to a manufacturer unless there's testing.” Source (secondary, dated by its content to October 2026): https://www.mmjdaily.com/article/9886214/nebraska-medical-cannabis-commission-warns-cultivators-of-up-to-two-more-years-wait/ — accessed 2026-10-30. That is consistent with the Run 1 baseline (no sale, no manufacturer) and explains why the §14 regime, however detailed on paper, is not yet an operating testing system.

(b) Hemp businesses.

  • Pre-harvest sampling, total-THC testing, DEA-lab delay, remediation/disposal and negligent-violation tiers are in §7.4 above and are not repeated here. In short: USDA sampling agent within 30 days before harvest, harvest within 30 days of sampling, DEA-registered lab testing subject to the fixed-baseline delay to 31 December 2026, remediation with retest or destruction for non-compliant lots, 1.0% negligent threshold, three-in-five-years revocation.
  • No Nebraska hemp testing-lab licence exists today. Former “approved testing facility” handling was an exception in historical §2-506(1) (see §7.2), not a standalone NDA lab licence described in the excerpts opened; the current Act contains no lab licence. Hemp compliance labs are DEA-registered laboratories under the USDA programme. Verify the current USDA DEA-lab directory for any Nebraska laboratory — none is named in this track.

11.2 Licence rules — transfers, ownership changes, renewals, ownership caps, relocation, revocation, insurance/bonding

(a) Medical cannabis licensees (draft Title 238 unless stated).

  • Transfers: prohibited. “Licenses are non-transferrable” — dispensary §04.11, cultivator §05.11 (as numbered in the draft excerpts; the same rule is repeated for manufacturers). There is no transfer application in the draft. Source: https://mcc.nebraska.gov/sites/default/files/doc/Revised%20-%20238%20NAC%201%20DRAFT.8.21.final.pdf — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Ownership changes: no standalone procedure located. A search of the draft for “change in ownership” returned no provision. What the draft does require is: renewal applications must list current officers, directors and owners including minority owners (§07.05); a licensee must report any change in manager (§10.02); and a “significant change” to the business plan must be notified within three (3) business days (§10.03) — but “significant change shall not include any change in financial projections, leadership team qualifications, or any changes in experience.” Whether an ownership change must be reported as a business-plan change, or requires a new application, is therefore Verify against the final text and Commission practice. Do not infer a change-of-ownership approval route from the former hemp rule (automatic lapse, §7.2) — that was a different programme, now repealed.
  • Renewals. Licence duration (§03.09): “If a fee is required for an application or renewal, licenses are valid for a period of one year from the date of issuance. If a fee is not required for the application or renewal, licenses are valid for a period of six (6) months from the date of issuance” — which is why the fee-free 2025 cultivator round produced six-month licences (Run 1). Draft renewal fees (§07.02): cultivator $23,500; manufacturer $23,500 without a transportation certification, $28,500 with one; dispensary $18,000, each “upon approval of renewal by the Commission.” Renewal requires fingerprints (draft §07.03, see §11.8), residency statements, tax-compliance statements and a business plan (§§07.05–07.06), and may be denied for good cause (§07.10). Product registrations are separate: $250 per product name (§13.07(B)), renewal $100, applied for 30–90 days before the one-year registration expiry (§13.07(E)–(F)). Sources: https://mcc.nebraska.gov/sites/default/files/doc/Revised%20-%20238%20NAC%201%20DRAFT.8.21.final.pdf — accessed 2026-10-30. All draft fees are PROPOSED/DRAFT, not final — see the Run 1 fee warning and the draft-status warning above.
  • Ownership caps and vertical integration. Vertical licensing is not permitted: “An applicant may not possess more than one license type authorized by this chapter” (dispensary §04.01, cultivator §05.01, manufacturer §06.01). Caps (draft §§04.12, 05.11 and Run 1 §4): 4 cultivators, 4 manufacturers, 12 dispensaries (one per judicial district, Neb. Rev. Stat. §24-301.02), each +1 per year only if the Commission finds supply insufficient in that category/district. No more than one establishment licence per address/location (§07.12). Residency/majority ownership: individual applicants must be U.S. citizens resident in Nebraska for at least four years; entities must be majority-owned by such citizens and have officers/directors resident for four years; “majority owned means more than fifty percent (50%) of the financial interests (other than a security interest, lien, or encumbrance) or more than fifty percent (50%) of the voting interests” (draft §§04.03, 05.03, 07.04). Draft text therefore says “more than 50%,” resolving Run 1's 50% vs 51% question for draft text only — final text Verify. Prohibited persons (draft §§04.07, 07.08): any person convicted of any felony or controlled-substance offence within the preceding 10 years (and entities whose officers/directors/owners are so convicted), entities whose officers/directors/owners had a licence suspended/revoked under these regulations, minors, government bodies, health care practitioners who issued recommendations/orders in the preceding five years, and persons with a cannabis-related citation/fine/sanction/injunction/judgment. Source: https://mcc.nebraska.gov/sites/default/files/doc/Revised%20-%20238%20NAC%201%20DRAFT.8.21.final.pdf — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Relocation. “A licensee shall not relocate a registered cannabis establishment from the address or location as specified in the license, unless the licensee can show an exceptional change of circumstances and the Commission approves the license relocation” (§§04.09, 05.09, 06.09). The relocation application must state the exceptional change and include “a relocation fee of one thousand five hundred dollars ($1500)” (§§04.10, 05.10, 06.10, draft). Source: https://mcc.nebraska.gov/sites/default/files/doc/Revised%20-%20238%20NAC%201%20DRAFT.8.21.final.pdf — accessed 2026-10-30. (Run 1, Part 3 records the Midwest Cultivator Group Omaha→Gretna relocation as an individual case; this rule is the mechanism it would use — the approval itself is not re-verified here.)
  • Revocation, sanctions, surrender, appeals. Sanctions (§08.01) may include suspension, revocation, fines, licence limitations, probation, seizure/destruction of cannabis/products/accessories, or any combination, for violations of the chapter, the Regulation Act, or good cause. Good cause (§07.11) includes violation, material false statement, breach of licence conditions, non-compliance with state/federal/local law, unpaid taxes/fees, prior sanction/revocation here or in another state/territory, improper surrender, or incomplete application. A licence “may be revoked if the license holder is convicted of any felony or any controlled substance related offense during the term of the license” (§08.03); non-compliance with local requirements may also be sanctioned (§08.04). Inactivity “without good cause” may lead to revocation/cancellation/non-renewal (§08.02 — wording difference flagged above). Surrender requires at least 30 days' advance written notice and proper collection/disposal of all cannabis/products before surrender; failure to give proper notice “may result in immediate revocation”; voluntary non-renewal counts as surrender, but a properly surrendered licence is not a sanction/revocation for future determinations (§07.09). Appeals: a licensee may request a fair hearing under the Nebraska Administrative Procedure Act within ten (10) business days, under Title 53 NAC model rules (§09.02). False statements in an application or renewal may lead to denial or revocation (§§04.06, 07.07). The licensee must notify the Commission within three (3) business days of citations/fines/judgments/lawsuits/investigations, adverse events, hours changes/temporary closures, suspected theft/attempted theft, manager changes, or a licensee/manager/employee/agent/financial-or-voting-interest holder being charged with a disqualifying offence (§10.15). Source: https://mcc.nebraska.gov/sites/default/files/doc/Revised%20-%20238%20NAC%201%20DRAFT.8.21.final.pdf — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Insurance and bonding minimums. No general liability insurance minimum was located in the draft (a search for “insurance” returns only the Department of Insurance authorisation for surety companies). What the draft does require, within 30 calendar days of licence issuance (§§10.11–10.13), is: (A) a reclamation bond of no less than $200,000 (the draft text renders the figure as “two hundred dollars ($200,000.00)” — the $200,000 figure is the operative amount, the words are a drafting error, Verify against the final text), naming the State of Nebraska as secured party, issued by a Nebraska-qualified surety, covering reclamation/restoration including equipment removal, waste destruction, environmental-hazard remediation, public-access prohibition, improperly coded buildings, seized-property disposition and other reclamation costs; and (B) a performance bond of no less than $100,000 (draft text: “one hundred dollars ($100,000.00)” — same words/figures mismatch, Verify), naming the State as secured party, covering lawful operation/compliance. Each bond must remain in effect at least one year from cancellation/expiration; proof of renewal/new bond is due at least 30 days before expiry, and a cancelled bond must be replaced before its termination date; a claim may be filed up to one year after revocation/surrender or bond cancellation/expiration, whichever is later. Separately, §10.14 preserves a landlord/landowner's right to require “an additional bond or other form of security” for leasing to an establishment. Source: https://mcc.nebraska.gov/sites/default/files/doc/Revised%20-%20238%20NAC%201%20DRAFT.8.21.final.pdf — accessed 2026-10-30.

(b) Hemp businesses.

  • USDA producer licences are non-transferable (§990.22, §7.1); renewal is before expiry with the same information; denial is appealable under Subpart D. There is no Nebraska hemp ownership-change, relocation or bonding rule in the surviving Act — the former automatic-lapse-on-ownership/location-change rule (§2-506, historical) was repealed by LB 262. A USDA producer changing locations must update its licence information through USDA/HeMP — the exact USDA location-change procedure was not opened in this track, Verify against the HeMP user guide. No hemp insurance or bonding minimum was located in the current Nebraska Act or in 7 CFR Part 990 excerpts opened — Unavailable, not “none required” under general law.

11.3 Cultivation rules — indoor/outdoor, plant limits, patient/caregiver cultivation; hemp plot registration

(a) Medical cannabis.

  • Location and facility type: “All cultivation of medical cannabis shall take place at the premises identified in the cultivator license. Cultivation may occur in outdoor, indoor, greenhouse facilities, or in any combination of these cultivation facilities” (§12.03). Cultivation locations “must be secured to prevent access to any cannabis plants or products by animals, or individuals who are not authorized agents of the cultivator and not otherwise authorized access by law” (§12.04). The application must state whether cultivation will be indoor, outdoor, greenhouse or a combination (draft §05.04(C)).
  • Signage (§12.05): cultivators “shall erect and maintain signage at the site of the cultivation locations that must be visible from the road at the primary entrance,” at the property perimeter, no less than 18 inches by 24 inches, font no less than 2 inches, black standardised font on white, compliant with county/local ordinances, showing business name, physical address, business phone number and cultivator licence number.
  • Plant limit — confirmed in draft text: “A cultivator may utilize any combination of indoor, outdoor, or greenhouse facilities, but may possess no more than one thousand two hundred fifty (1,250) flowering plants at one time” (§014.06). “Flowering plant” means a plant “from the time it exhibits the first signs of sexual maturity through harvest” (§02.16); clones and immature plants are separately defined (§§02.09, 02.18) and are not counted in the 1,250 figure as written — Verify whether any immature/mother-plant cap exists in the final text; none was located in the draft excerpts opened. Source: https://mcc.nebraska.gov/sites/default/files/doc/Revised%20-%20238%20NAC%201%20DRAFT.8.21.final.pdf — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Sale channels and seed stock: a cultivator “may only cultivate or process cannabis for sale to a Nebraska licensed product manufacturer or other Nebraska licensed cultivator” (§12.02) and “cannot transfer cannabis plants or cannabis products to qualified patients, caregivers, or any other unlicensed entity” (§02.11). Seed/immature plants/genetic material may only be obtained “from another Nebraska licensed cultivator or a cultivator authorized to operate in another state” (§12.09); manufacturers may only obtain plants/products from a Nebraska licensed cultivator or manufacturer (§13.06).
  • Patient/caregiver cultivation: none. Initiative 437 authorises use, possession and acquisition of up to 5 ounces; it does not authorise home cultivation, and no Title 238 provision located in this track creates patient/caregiver cultivation (Run 1 baseline, confirmed). A patient or caregiver is not a licensee and may not use the cultivation rules above.
  • Records: cultivators must retain, for at least seven years, sales invoices (including any discount applied), daily inventory, purchase/transport records, seed-planting dates/quantities, each plant's vegetative/flowering/harvest/transport/processing/destruction/sale status, strain and Total THC, all organic pesticides/herbicides/fertilisers applied with ingredients, all testing samples/results, and a waste disposal log (date, time, location, weight, destruction method, mixing medium, destroying employee IDs) (§12.14). Manufacturers and dispensaries have parallel seven-year record duties (§§13.17–13.18, 11.17).

(b) Hemp.

  • Plot registration and pre-harvest sampling are in §7.4 and are not repeated here: licence-application location information, FSA acreage reporting (deadline Verify), sampling-agent collection within 30 days before harvest, harvest within 30 days of sampling. There is no hemp plant-count cap in the USDA programme or the surviving Nebraska Act — the cap is the licensed acreage/location, not a plant number. Indoor/greenhouse production is covered by the licence's location/lot information (including greenhouse lots in §990.21); no separate indoor hemp licence type exists.

11.4 Security requirements

(a) Medical cannabis (draft §§10.08–10.10, 10.17, 10.20, 13.13–13.16).

  • General duty (§10.08): “All registered cannabis establishments shall implement appropriate security measures to deter and prevent the unauthorized entrance into areas containing medical cannabis and the theft and diversion of medical cannabis and medical cannabis products. All establishments shall develop a comprehensive security plan. All videos, access logs, and any other monitoring data shall be available to the Nebraska State Patrol, any other law enforcement agency, or the Commission upon request.” Licensees are responsible for security on the licensed premises; manufacturers with transportation certificates are responsible for cannabis/products/accessories/waste in their possession during transit (§10.08(A)). Theft/diversion controls must be effective (§10.08(B)). Patient data must be kept in a secured database abiding by state and federal law and disclosed only to the Commission/agents/employees, a Commission-licensed party, or a registered health care practitioner (§10.08(D)). Intra-facility movement on the same plot requires a written security/tracking plan (§10.08(E)).
  • Equipment (§10.09): intrusion-detection devices (including radio/cellular signal systems); exterior lighting covering buildings, perimeter and parking (except outdoor cultivation); continuous electronic video monitoring at at least 15 frames per second, identifying people/activities in all lighting levels, with Commission inspection on request; motion detection alone is not permitted where cannabis product is or will be present; remote access via https or a Commission-approved format; cameras covering every entry/exit including windows, loading docks, parking, every area where cannabis is or will be present, every point of sale, every vault/safe, every seed-to-sale access point including offsite warehouses and transport vehicles, the entire perimeter including at least 20 feet around an outdoor grow area, and cannabis/products from at least two angles wherever grown, cultivated, manufactured, sampled, stored, weighed, packaged, processed, sold, distributed, rendered unusable/unrecognizable, disposed or loaded for transport. Cameras must have date/time generators, be tamper-resistant and cabled (not Wi-Fi-only); recordings must be stored at least 120 days in a secure location/service capable of producing copies for the Commission at the licensee's expense, with storage-mechanism changes notified within 10 days; at least 60 minutes' battery backup; surveillance equipment housed in a designated locked secured room/enclosure with restricted access; immediate automatic law-enforcement notification on unauthorised breach; security film on glass doors/storefronts.
  • Premises and people: employees must wear identification inside the establishment (§10.18); no person under 18 may be on/in licensed premises (§10.05); no consumption/use on/in licensed premises (§10.06); no licensee may employ/retain a prohibited person (§10.07); when closed or unstaffed, the establishment must be securely locked with commercial-grade non-residential door locks, all cannabis locked in secured storage, and the alarm active (§10.20). Preoperational inspection by the Commission/agents is required before operation, including State Fire Marshal inspection/occupancy evidence, property ownership or owner agreement, a final diagram showing storage and alarm/camera/surveillance locations, and evidence of compliance with the regulations and the application business plan (§10.17).
  • Transport security (§§13.13–13.16): a manifest is required for all transported cannabis/products/accessories/waste (date, origin/receiver licence numbers, addresses and contacts, quantity by weight/unit and product type, accompanying persons, departure/delivery date-time, driver signature, receiver agent's name/date/signature, transporter manufacturer licence number), with copies to originator and receiver. Vehicles must have a locked storage compartment/container, no markings identifying medical-cannabis transport, a copy of the transportation certification and manufacturer licence, and meet Nebraska road requirements. Drivers must have a means of communication with originator/receiver and 911, conspicuously display a manufacturer employee badge, hold a valid Nebraska driver's licence for the vehicle class, wear no clothing indicating medical-cannabis possession, and not be a prohibited person. Seed-to-sale tracking (§10.10, §18) is covered in §11.1's tracking cross-reference below: before a state system is implemented, a Commission-approved seed-to-sale system must be used; after implementation, no untracked cannabis may be sold/transferred/purchased/accepted, and a private system must fully integrate or the establishment must report directly into the state system.

(b) Hemp businesses.

  • No Nebraska hemp-specific security-equipment rule (cameras, alarms, lighting, storage) was located in the surviving Act or in the USDA excerpts opened. Security for hemp is by licence-location control, sampling/testing, transport documentation (§2-515, §7.5) and USDA audit/site-visit powers (§990.28). A hemp producer must still comply with general law (theft, trespass, pesticide storage, food safety where applicable) — Verify any site-security condition in the individual USDA licence or tribal plan, which were not opened here.

11.5 Waste disposal — including non-compliant hemp destruction

(a) Medical cannabis (draft §§02.23, 02.37, 10.08(C), 11.15–11.16, 12.11–12.13, 13.11–13.12).

  • Definitions: “medical cannabis waste” is cannabis “unused, unwanted, damaged, defective, expired, or contaminated” (§02.23). “Unusable and unrecognizable” means all waste components are “indistinguishable from and incapable of being ingested, inhaled, injected, swallowed, or otherwise used for medical use,” by thermal treatment/melting, shredding/grinding/tearing, or incorporation with other municipal waste at a mixture of “at least 50% non-cannabis waste by volume” (§02.37).
  • Storage and handling: all waste must be stored “in a secure waste receptacle that is locked with commercial-grade locks prior to any disposal or transfer,” in a safe secure location with limited access (§10.08(C)). Cultivators and manufacturers must store, secure, manage and record waste/plant-material waste under all applicable federal/state/local laws and, before transport of plant-material waste, render it unusable and unrecognizable (§§12.12, 13.12). Manufacturers must dispose of liquid/chemical product waste under applicable laws (§13.12).
  • Collection route: a manufacturer with a transportation certification “shall collect medical cannabis waste from Nebraska licensed dispensaries and Nebraska licensed cultivators” (§13.11); a dispensary “shall permit” such a manufacturer to retrieve its waste and must keep a written record of all product returned for disposal (§11.15), storing/securing/managing/recording it pending return (§11.16). Returned product collected by a manufacturer may be used for research/development or retained samples, but “shall not [be introduced] into lots of products intended for sale or transport,” and waste not so used/retained must be disposed of with a written disposal record (§13.11).
  • Exceptions (§12.13): root balls, soil/growing media and stalks “are not considered medical cannabis waste and do not require treatment to render the materials unusable and unrecognizable, provided that the cannabis does not contain any cannabis flower or leaves with any visible trichomes.”
  • Logs (seven-year retention): cultivator logs must show date, time, location, weight, destruction method, mixing medium and destroying employee IDs (§12.14(I)); manufacturer logs the same (§13.17(F)); dispensary logs show date, time, manufacturer name, product name and quantity received for disposal (§11.17(D)). Failed-test destruction is in §11.1(a) (§14.04(B)).

(b) Hemp — non-compliant destruction.

  • See §7.4: USDA producers must dispose of or remediate non-compliant lots (with post-remediation retest); former NDA destruction (only the non-compliant lot, off-site hemp, or unlawfully handled hemp; licensee bears all cost and no compensation) is historical. No Nebraska hemp waste-disposal method list (e.g. composting vs burial) was located in the surviving Act — Verify the current USDA disposal guidance. Compliant hemp byproduct/waste handling (e.g. stalks, seed) is not separately regulated as “waste” in the surviving Act; general agricultural-waste and food/feed law may apply — Verify, do not assume.

11.6 Environmental rules — water, pesticide limits, energy

(a) Medical cannabis.

  • Pesticides/herbicides/fertilisers — the only detailed environmental rule located: “A cultivator may not use pesticides, herbicides, or fertilizers other than those certified organic by the Organic Materials Review Institute or another similar standards organization. No other chemicals may be used for the purposes of planting, growing, or cultivating medical cannabis” (§12.07). All such products applied, with ingredients, must be recorded (§12.14(G)). Pesticide residues are then tested against Table 4 action levels (§14.15, Table 4 — levels Verify against a clean copy). “Pesticide” is broadly defined (§02.27) to include plant regulators, defoliants, desiccants and spray adjuvants; “adulterant” includes pesticides, heavy metals, solvents, microbial life, toxins, foreign matter and artificially derived cannabinoids (§02.01).
  • Water: no water-use, irrigation, discharge or wastewater rule was located in the draft excerpts opened — “water” appears only as water-activity testing (§02.38, §14.11). No rule located does not mean no general water law applies; it means Title 238 as opened contains no water-quantity/quality operating rule for cultivators.
  • Energy: no energy, lighting, carbon or efficiency rule was located in the draft excerpts opened. No rule located.
  • Adjacent environmental-adjacent duties: food-safety compliance and FDA approval for all non-cannabis ingredients in manufactured products (§13.09); State Fire Marshal inspection/occupancy as part of preoperational inspection (§10.17); reclamation-bond coverage expressly includes “remediation of environmental hazards” (§10.11, §11.2).

(b) Hemp businesses.

  • No Nebraska-specific hemp water, pesticide or energy operating rule was located in the surviving Hemp Farming Act (§§2-501, 2-503, 2-505, 2-515) or in the USDA excerpts opened. Pesticide use on hemp is therefore governed by general pesticide law (federal label law and Nebraska pesticide law) — Verify which products are labelled for hemp in Nebraska before advising use; this track did not locate an NDA hemp pesticide list, and the former NDA programme's pesticide position was not in the excerpts opened. Water and energy: no hemp-specific rule located.

11.7 Sampling, giveaways, discounts and promotions

(a) Medical cannabis.

  • Giveaways/promotions — prohibited for dispensaries: “A dispensary shall not give away or otherwise offer or transfer cannabis as part of a promotional event” (§11.01). No consumer “free sample” programme exists in the draft — the only “samples” in the draft are testing/developmental samples to a laboratory (§§12.08, 13.05, §14) and retained/R&D samples from returned product (§13.11). Do not conflate testing samples with patient samples.
  • Practitioner promotions — prohibited: an enrolled recommending practitioner shall not “Offer a discount or thing of value to a qualified patient who uses or agrees to use a particular dispensary or caregiver” (§16.04(C)), shall not accept/solicit/offer pecuniary remuneration to/from a licensed person (§16.04(A)), shall not advertise for any registered establishment (§16.04(E)), and shall not hold an economic interest in a licensed entity (§16.04(F)).
  • Dispensary price discounts — no general ban located, but discounts must be recorded: sales invoices/receipts for cultivators, manufacturers, transporters and dispensaries must show “The cost to the purchaser, together with any discount applied to the price as shown on the invoice” (§§11.17(B)(iv), 12.14(A)(iv), 13.17(A)(iv), 13.18(A)(iv)). That recording duty is not, on its face, a discount ban or a discount permission — Verify whether any advertising/promotion rule outside Chapter 1 (or in the final permanent text) restricts dispensary discount advertising. No dispensary advertising rule was located in the draft excerpts opened beyond the practitioner rules above.
  • Other promotion-adjacent limits in the draft: no delivery/transport to a patient/caregiver — a dispensary “may only sell medical cannabis products at its licensed premises and may not deliver or otherwise transport to a qualified patient, caregiver, or any other unlicensed entity” (§11.04); no sale without an electronic written recommendation (within 24 months) and written order direct from the recommending practitioner, with photo-ID verification (§11.04); no refills — a dispensary “may not sell medical cannabis to the same qualified patient or caregiver for the same written order more than once” (§11.10); quantity limits — no sale resulting in possession above 5 ounces or above the written-order amount, no more than a 30-day supply, no more than 5 grams Total THC per patient in a 90-day period, and no sale within 30 days after filling a valid written order (§013.07; the 5 g/90-day figure matches Run 1's 90-day version and resolves Run 1's 30-vs-90-day question for draft text — final text Verify).

(b) Hemp businesses.

  • No hemp-specific sampling, giveaway, discount or promotion rule was located in the surviving Nebraska Hemp Farming Act or in the USDA excerpts opened. Hemp product promotion is therefore governed by general law (consumer-protection, food/drug advertising, and the Attorney General's hemp-enforcement position summarised in Run 1, Part 1) — Verify before advising any hemp promotion, and do not import the medical dispensary giveaway ban (§11.01) into hemp retail.

11.8 Drug testing and background checks for licence holders and staff

(a) Medical cannabis.

  • Background checks — fingerprints, for applicants and managers. An applicant for initial issuance “shall also submit required fingerprints, to be furnished for every person included in the application, to the Nebraska State Patrol for a National criminal history record information check and a fee for such record check payable to the patrol,” authorising release to the Commission (dispensary §04.02, cultivator §05.02, manufacturer §06.02). The draft renewal chapter repeats fingerprinting (§07.03) — Verify whether renewal fingerprinting survives in the final permanent text, as it is unusual and the Run 1 track did not separately confirm it. Each licensee must report its manager's name and “submit the manager's background check to the Commission” (§10.01) and report manager changes (§10.02). The 10-year felony/controlled-substance bar and other prohibited-person categories are in §11.2(a). Transport drivers must not be prohibited persons (§13.16(E)).
  • Drug testing of staff — no rule located. A full-text search of the draft for “drug test” returned no hits. No employee drug-testing requirement was located in Title 238 Chapter 1 as opened. That is not a finding that employers may not test under general employment law, nor that a future Commission rule will not add testing — it is a gap in the draft text. Verify against the final permanent text and any Commission employment guidance.
  • Practitioner background/education (for completeness, not a licensee rule): recommending practitioners must enrol in the Recommending Health Care Practitioner Directory, complete 10 hours of accredited medical-cannabis education within one calendar year of enrolment and a 2-hour annual refresher (ACCME-accredited), and re-enrol annually 30–90 days before expiry (draft §§16.01–16.02). This is a directory, not a licence, and does not create a patient registry.

(b) Hemp businesses.

  • Current USDA: FBI criminal history report (within 60 days, secondary — Verify, §7.1) for the applicant and every key participant; 10-year felony controlled-substance bar (§990.22). Former NDA: background check for felony controlled-substance charges in the prior 10 years, at applicant expense, for all listed individuals (§2-510, historical). No hemp staff drug-testing rule was located in the surviving Act or USDA excerpts opened.

11.9 Research and university cannabis/hemp work (UNL, UNMC)

(a) Medical cannabis research — no research licence in the draft.

  • The draft mentions a “medical cannabis research facility” only inside the State Inventory Tracking System definition: the system accounts for cannabis “until the medical cannabis or medical cannabis product is sold to a patient at a licensed dispensary, disposed of in accordance with these rules, or used in a research project by a medical cannabis research facility” (§02.32). No research-facility licence type, application, fee, cap or approval process was located anywhere in the draft chapters opened. Developmental testing/research sampling by cultivators/manufacturers (§§12.08, 13.05) and R&D use of returned product (§13.11) are the only research-adjacent permissions located. Verify whether a research-facility route exists in the final permanent text, in statute (§71-24,107 et seq.), or in a separate Commission rule — Unavailable in this track.
  • UNL: the University of Nebraska–Lincoln work located in this track is survey/policy research, not cultivation or clinical research: UNL's Rural Drug Addiction Research Center tracked Nebraskans' opinions and use through the Nebraska Annual Social Indicators Survey in 2024 and 2026 (84% supported legal medical cannabis in 2026 vs 80% in 2024; 47% favoured both medical and recreational legalisation in 2026). Source (official university, 22 July 2026): https://research.unl.edu/blog/report-nebraskans-support-of-legal-cannabis-is-growing/ — accessed 2026-10-30. No UNL medical-cannabis cultivation, manufacturing or clinical-trial programme was located in official sources in this track — Unavailable, not disproven.
  • UNMC: secondary reporting (November 2025) describes a UNMC pharmacy professor teaching a medical-cannabis dosing/history/chemistry/toxicity/interactions course and notes few Nebraska experts in medical-marijuana use. Source (secondary news): https://gray-koln-prod.web.arc-cdn.net/2025/11/06/will-people-unmc-pharmacologist-medical-cannabis-safety-applications/ — accessed 2026-10-30. Claims in low-authority secondary summaries about a UNMC/NIH cannabis brain-development grant or a DHHS “Medical Cannabis Research Program” were not relied on here and remain Verify against UNMC/NIH primary sources. No Nebraska state-funded medical-cannabis research programme was located in official sources in this track.

(b) Hemp research.

  • No separate university hemp-research licence exists in the surviving Act. Historically, LB 657-era summaries described University of Nebraska / NDA hemp research authority, but those provisions are not in the surviving §§2-501/2-503/2-505/2-515 opened here and the former research-related sections were among those repealed by LB 262 — Verify the historical research authority against the pre-2024 Act text before citing it. Today, a university wishing to grow hemp in Nebraska would, on the texts opened, need a USDA producer licence (or a tribal-program licence on tribal land) like any other producer; no university exemption was located. UNL hemp agronomy/extension work, if any, was not located in official sources in this track — Unavailable, not disproven. Tribal hemp research, if any, belongs in Part 10.

11.10 Cross-cutting operations note: seed-to-sale tracking (medical only in this part)

  • Medical: draft §18.01 — “The Commission shall procure and maintain a centralized computer software tracking system that tracks cannabis from seed or immature plant stage through growth, harvest, and processing, to testing, transport, and sale, and allows real-time, twenty-four (24) hour access by the Commission,” with each establishment responsible for recording all cannabis sold/transported and for tracking-system compliance costs, directly or by integrating its own enterprise software (§18.01). Draft §10.10 (above, §11.4) governs the interim Commission-approved seed-to-sale system. No vendor, product name or live-system status was located in this track — Verify against Commission procurement/minutes before naming a system (Run 1, Part 1, §1.7, carries the same flag). Hemp has no Nebraska seed-to-sale system: USDA tracking is by licence, lot, FSA acreage report and lab reporting through HeMP (§7.4).

Part 12 — Consumer and Social Rules

PART 12 — CONSUMER AND SOCIAL RULES

12.1 Paraphernalia and smoke-shop rules

  • Definition (opened directly, Neb. Rev. Stat. § 28-439). Verbatim: "drug paraphernalia shall mean all equipment, products, and materials of any kind which are used, intended for use, or designed for use, in manufacturing, injecting, ingesting, inhaling, or otherwise introducing into the human body a controlled substance in violation of sections 28-101, 28-431, and 28-439 to 28-444 or the Uniform Controlled Substances Act." The enumerated examples include "(4) Objects used, intended for use, or designed for use in ingesting, inhaling, or otherwise introducing marijuana, cocaine, hashish, or hashish oil into the human body," including "Metal, wooden, acrylic, glass, stone, plastic, or ceramic pipes," "Water pipes," "Roach clips," "Bongs," "Chillums" and "Ice pipes or chillers" — source: https://nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=28-439 — accessed 2026-10-30. Source line: Laws 1980, LB 991, § 1 — same source, accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Use / possession (opened directly, § 28-441). Verbatim: "(1) It shall be unlawful for any person to use, or to possess with intent to use, drug paraphernalia to manufacture, inject, ingest, inhale, or otherwise introduce into the human body a controlled substance in violation of sections 28-101, 28-431, and 28-439 to 28-444. (2) Any person who violates this section shall be guilty of an infraction. (3) A person shall not be in violation of this section if section 28-472 or 28-1701 applies." — source: https://nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=28-441 — accessed 2026-10-30. Last amended per the source line: Laws 2022, LB 519, § 5 — same source, accessed 2026-10-30. Note the interaction with Run 1: Initiative 437 protects a qualified patient's "cannabis accessories" (defined in § 71-24,104(3) as equipment/products/materials used in storing, vaporising or containing cannabis, or ingesting/inhaling/introducing it) — so a qualified patient's accessories for protected use are not the same legal question as a non-patient's paraphernalia. Verify how §§ 28-472 / 28-1701 (the § 28-441(3) exceptions) apply to medical patients; their full texts were not opened in this run.
  • Delivery / manufacture for delivery (opened directly, § 28-442). Verbatim: "(1) It shall be unlawful for any person to deliver, possess with intent to deliver, or manufacture with intent to deliver, drug paraphernalia, knowing, or under circumstances in which one reasonably should know, that it will be used to manufacture, inject, ingest, or inhale or otherwise be used to introduce into the human body a controlled substance in violation of sections 28-101, 28-431, and 28-439 to 28-444. … (3) Any person who violates this section shall be guilty of a Class II misdemeanor." — source: https://nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=28-442 — accessed 2026-10-30. The pharmacist syringe exception in § 28-442(2) is for prevention of infectious disease — same source, accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Delivery to a minor (opened directly, § 28-443). Verbatim: "Any person eighteen years of age or older who violates section 28-442 by delivering drug paraphernalia to a person under eighteen years of age who is at least three years his or her junior shall be guilty of a Class I misdemeanor." — source: https://nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=28-443 — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Smoke shops / CBD shops — NO cannabis-specific shop licence found. There is no Nebraska "smoke shop licence" or "CBD shop licence" in the statutes located in this run. What applies instead is the general framework: (a) paraphernalia delivery rules above (§ 28-442) — a shop that knowingly sells items for drug use risks a Class II misdemeanor, which is why shops commonly sell pipes etc. as tobacco accessories; that marketing distinction is a practical enforcement point, not a statutory safe harbour located in this run — flag as Verify/practice, not law; (b) tobacco retailer licensing through city/village clerks or county clerks for tobacco products (§§ 28-1421 et seq., via secondary summaries — Verify against the current statute text); (c) local zoning (Run 1 Part 3); (d) Attorney General Consumer Protection Act / Pure Food Act enforcement against hemp retailers selling over-limit delta-9 products (Run 1). A shop selling only compliant hemp is not, on the statutes opened, required to hold a cannabis licence — but no statute expressly says "hemp retail needs no licence" either; the absence is stated as NO RULE FOUND for a hemp-retail licence, not as a positive exemption.
  • Advertising / sale of paraphernalia (§ 28-444, not opened in this run): Verify — the advertising provision was not read directly; do not quote it here.

12.2 Minimum age for hemp-derived products — NO STATE MINIMUM AGE FOUND

  • Finding: NO RULE FOUND in Nebraska statute for a minimum purchase age specifically for hemp-derived products (including delta-8). Multiple secondary sources agree there is no state age floor for delta-8/hemp-derived cannabinoids, while retailers commonly card at 21 as a business practice — sources (secondary, dated): https://legalclarity.org/how-old-do-you-have-to-be-to-buy-delta-8-in-nebraska/ — accessed 2026-10-30; https://www.mdpi.com/3043-0429/1/2/10 (peer-reviewed pilot study, 26 Oct 2026, stating "Nebraska law did not establish a statewide minimum age for the purchase of hemp-derived CBD products" and that 18/21 retailer restrictions "reflect individual retailer policies rather than state law") — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Do not import the tobacco age. Neb. Rev. Stat. § 28-1419 (tobacco products, including vapour products / alternative nicotine products, to a minor under 18 — Class III misdemeanor per secondary summaries) covers tobacco/nicotine, not THC — and federal Tobacco 21 sets 21 for tobacco/vapour products federally. Neither, on the sources opened, sets a Nebraska age for hemp-derived THC products. The 21+ figure in many retailer guides is a practice standard, not a Nebraska hemp statute — do not cite it as law.
  • Contrast — ages that ARE in law: qualified patient 18+ on their own recommendation, under-18 only with practitioner recommendation + parent/guardian written permission (§ 71-24,104, Run 1); caregiver 21+ for an adult patient (§ 71-24,104, Run 1); Omaha tribal law 21+ for adult-use possession/purchase on tribal land (secondary, §10.1); Omaha tribal commission applicants 21+ (official tribal site, §10.1). Proposed age-21 hemp rules in dead bills (LB 316, indefinitely postponed 17 Apr 2026) are not law and are not counted here.

12.3 THC driving limits and impairment testing — NO per se THC limit; impairment-based

  • No per se THC limit in Nebraska. Secondary comparison (15 Sept 2026) lists Nebraska as "Impairment-based | No marijuana-specific numeric THC limit" — source: https://themarijuanaherald.com/2026/09/marijuana-dui-laws-by-state/ — accessed 2026-10-30. This matches Run 1's baseline and is adopted here, with the statute below as the primary anchor.
  • Statute (opened directly, Neb. Rev. Stat. § 60-6,196, operative text via annotations). The offence is operating or being in actual physical control of a motor vehicle "(a) While under the influence of alcoholic liquor or of any drug" — the 0.08 alcohol thresholds in (b) and (c) are alcohol-only — source: https://nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=60-6,196 — accessed 2026-10-30 (Justia reproduction of the statute text was also consulted: https://law.justia.com/codes/nebraska/chapter-60/statute-60-6-196/ — accessed 2026-10-30).
  • Impairment standard (case law annotations on the official statute page). "under the influence of alcoholic liquor or of any drug" requires "ingestion of alcohol or drugs in an amount sufficient to impair to any appreciable degree the driver's ability to operate a motor vehicle in a prudent and cautious manner" — State v. Falcon, 260 Neb. 119, 615 N.W.2d 436 (2000), as annotated on the statute page — source: https://nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=60-6,196 — accessed 2026-10-30. A drug test result "is one piece of evidence that the defendant's ability to operate a motor vehicle is impaired; it is not conclusive under subsection (1)(a)" — Falcon, same source, accessed 2026-10-30. Impairment "can be shown by observations of witnesses, particularly police officers who are trained to make these observations" and by poor field-sobriety performance — Falcon / State v. Daly, 278 Neb. 903, 775 N.W.2d 47 (2009), same source, accessed 2026-10-30. "This section only requires proof that the defendant was under the influence of any drug and does not require the drug to be identified by the arresting officer" — Daly, same source, accessed 2026-10-30.
  • No medical-patient driving defence located. Initiative 437 protects use/possession/acquisition; it does not on its face authorise driving under the influence, and no Nebraska statute located in this run creates a prescription-style defence for a qualified patient charged under § 60-6,196. Verify whether any DHHS / State Patrol guidance treats a practitioner's recommendation as a "medical order" for driving purposes; none was located. Patients must be told plainly: a recommendation is not a licence to drive impaired.
  • Implied consent / chemical testing (§§ 60-6,197 et seq.): Verify current penalty and refusal consequences against the opened statute text — secondary summaries (NORML, undated/old) state consent to blood/breath/urine testing for alcohol concentration or presence of drugs and a 6-month suspension for refusal, but those summaries were not re-verified against the current official text in this run and are not adopted as current law here. Drug-recognition-expert (DRE) evidence: State v. Falcon (2000) accepted a DRE opinion that the defendant was under the influence of marijuana to an impairing extent — source (secondary case summary): http://norml.org/laws/drugged-driving/nebraska-drugged-driving/ — accessed 2026-10-30. Whether Nebraska currently operates a DRE programme and how many DREs it has is Unavailable in this run.
  • No cannabis open-container statute in force was located. The proposal to expand open-container law to cannabis products appeared in LB 677 / LB 52 drafts (both not enacted) — not law, do not cite as current. Smoking/vaping while driving a private vehicle is not separately codified as a traffic offence in the sources opened; the charge route is DUI under § 60-6,196 if impaired, plus possession/paraphernalia where applicable.

12.4 Expungement and record clearing for marijuana offences — petition-based, NOT automatic; no cannabis-specific automatic expungement

  • No automatic marijuana expungement statute in Nebraska. There is no Nebraska law that automatically clears marijuana convictions on the passage of Initiatives 437/438, and no "Clean Slate" cannabis act in force (LB 52's Cannabis Conviction Clean Slate Act was a proposal, not enacted — not law).
  • What exists instead — three distinct routes, do not conflate them: 1. Removal from the public record for non-convictions (Neb. Rev. Stat. § 29-3523, opened directly). Verbatim operative scheme: after the stated periods, "a criminal justice agency shall respond to a public inquiry in the same manner as if there were no criminal history record information" — § 29-3523(1); for an arrest/citation/referral with no charges filed, the record "shall not be part of the public record after one year"; after completed diversion, after two years; after dismissal (including acquittal, deferred judgment, drug-court completion), "immediately upon notification" / entry of dismissal — § 29-3523(3) — source: https://nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=29-3523 — accessed 2026-10-30. A person "cannot be questioned with respect to any offense for which the record is sealed" and "may respond as if the offense never occurred" — § 29-3523(8), same source, accessed 2026-10-30. Erroneous-arrest expungement requires a district-court petition and clear-and-convincing proof of agency error — § 29-3523(9), same source, accessed 2026-10-30. This section does not erase convictions. 2. Set-aside of a conviction (Neb. Rev. Stat. § 29-2264, via search excerpt of the official statute page). A person placed on probation who satisfactorily completes it, or sentenced to a fine only or community service, may petition the sentencing court to set aside the conviction after fulfilment/payment/completion; a person sentenced otherwise (but not to more than one year imprisonment) may petition after completion of sentence — § 29-2264(2)–(3) — source: https://nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=29-2264 — accessed 2026-10-30. A set-aside does not erase the record, does not require reinstatement of lost office/employment, and does not preclude use of the conviction for impeachment, subsequent sentencing, or subsequent set-aside eligibility — § 29-2264(6), same source, accessed 2026-10-30. Secondary summaries agree set-aside convictions still appear, marked "set aside" — source: https://libertylawgroup.com/blog/why-do-i-need-a-lawyer-for-my-marijuana-charge-in-nebraska/ — accessed 2026-10-30. Verify the full § 29-2264 text, including the denial grounds in (3)(b) and the firearm-advice notice in (3)(c), before quoting further. 3. Pardon + sealing. A pardoned person may move the sentencing court to seal the record — § 29-3523(5), source above, accessed 2026-10-30. Pardons are via the Board of Pardons (secondary) — Verify against the Board's official page for procedure.
  • Marijuana-specific note (secondary, with a caution): one secondary guide states the set-aside remedy "applies to" possession of ≤1 oz and related paraphernalia, and not to distribution/manufacture/>1 oz — source: https://legalclarity.org/29-4125-how-to-expunge-a-marijuana-conviction-in-nebraska/ — accessed 2026-10-30. That eligibility gloss is not in the § 29-2264 excerpt opened and is therefore flagged Verify — § 29-2264 on its face is a general conviction set-aside statute, not a marijuana-specific one. Do not present "§ 29-4125" as a marijuana expungement statute: the URL slug is a secondary site's label, not a Nebraska statute number.
  • Tribal expungement (Omaha Title 51) is tribal-law only (§10.1) and does not clear Nebraska state convictions on the sources opened.
  • Juvenile sealing (§ 43-2,108.01, secondary only): Verify — not opened in this run.

12.5 Consumption rules

  • Public / indoor smoking — Clean Indoor Air Act applies to plant-product smoke, not just tobacco. Neb. Rev. Stat. § 71-5727 (opened directly) defines: "Smoke or smoking means inhaling, exhaling, burning, or carrying any lighted or heated cigar, cigarette, pipe, hookah, or any other lighted or heated tobacco or plant product intended for inhalation, whether natural or synthetic, in any manner or in any form. The term includes the use of an electronic smoking device which creates an aerosol or vapor, in any manner or in any form." — source: https://nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=71-5727 — accessed 2026-10-30. Last amended Laws 2020, LB 840, § 6 — same source, accessed 2026-10-30. Smoking in a place of employment or public place in violation of the Act is a Class V misdemeanor first offence, Class IV misdemeanor second/subsequent — § 71-5733 (via search excerpt of the official statute page) — source: https://www.nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=71-5733 — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • What that means for patients: the Act's definition covers cannabis smoke/vapour as a "plant product intended for inhalation," so indoor public places and places of employment are not lawful consumption sites merely because the smoker is a qualified patient — Initiative 437 protects use, but it does not override the Clean Indoor Air Act on the texts opened. Verify whether any Commission regulation or DHHS guidance creates a patient exception; none was located. The LB 677 proposal to expressly apply the Act to cannabis smoke/vapour was unnecessary on this reading and, in any event, LB 677 is dead — do not cite it as the source of the rule.
  • Private consumption: no Nebraska statute located in this run bans a qualified patient's private, at-home use of up to 5 oz (subject to landlord rules, §12.7, and DUI rules, §12.3). Public consumption of marijuana by a non-patient is possession/use of an unlawful substance and is chargeable as possession/paraphernalia, not as a standalone "public consumption" offence located in this run — NO STANDALONE PUBLIC-CONSUMPTION STATUTE FOUND; do not invent one.
  • No licensed consumption lounge / on-site consumption venue exists under state law (no dispensary licensed at all, Run 1). Omaha tribal land consumption is governed by tribal law on tribal land (§10.1) — Verify Title 51's on-site consumption rules against the tribal text.

12.6 Visitor and out-of-state patient rules

  • No visitor purchase programme in Nebraska. No state dispensary is licensed, so no visitor — patient or adult — can lawfully buy medical cannabis in Nebraska as of 30 Oct 2026 (Run 1 baseline, matched).
  • No reciprocity / recognition provision in Initiatives 437/438 located. The Acts as quoted in Run 1 contain no clause recognising another state's patient card, no temporary visitor card, and no non-resident purchase route. NO RECIPROCITY RULE FOUND — do not import Missouri's possession protection for out-of-state cardholders or South Dakota's non-resident card into Nebraska law.
  • Residency — open question, flagged Verify (do not overstate). The § 71-24,104 definition of "qualified patient" as quoted in Run 1 ("An individual eighteen years of age or older with a written recommendation from a health care practitioner") contains no express Nebraska-residency requirement, and "health care practitioner" includes a practitioner "licensed in any state and practicing in compliance with the Uniform Credentialing Act" (Run 1 quote). That text, read literally, does not exclude a non-resident with a qualifying recommendation from being a "qualified patient" for possession purposes — but no Commission rule, court ruling or AG opinion located in this run confirms that reading, and it creates no right to purchase (no seller exists) and no protection for transport into Nebraska (§10.8). Verify before advising any visitor.
  • Visitors who are adults 21+ from other states gain nothing extra: adult-use is illegal in Nebraska regardless of home-state law, and a home-state adult-use purchase does not convert to Nebraska medical protection without a qualifying recommendation.
  • Practical advice line for the compiler (not law): a visitor's safest statement of Nebraska law as of 30 Oct 2026 is: do not expect to buy cannabis legally in Nebraska, do not bring cannabis into Nebraska expecting a visitor exemption, and a medical recommendation (home-state or otherwise) has no tested Nebraska reciprocity.

12.7 Landlord and tenant rules — NO cannabis-specific tenant protection; general eviction routes apply

  • NO RULE FOUND protecting a medical-cannabis patient tenant from eviction or lease non-renewal solely for lawful medical use. Initiatives 437/438 contain no landlord/tenant provision located in Run 1 or this run (contrast the dead LB 474 (2021) committee language that would have barred schools/landlords from refusing enrolment/lease solely for programme participation — a proposal, not law).
  • General routes a landlord CAN use (opened directly, § 76-1431):
  • Material noncompliance / lease breach: 30-day termination notice if not remedied in 14 days — § 76-1431(1) — source: https://nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=76-1431 — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Non-payment: termination if rent not paid within 7 calendar days after written notice — § 76-1431(2), same source, accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Violent criminal activity / illegal sale of a controlled substance / health-and-safety-threatening activity on the premises: 5 days' written notice, no right to cure, suit for possession — § 76-1431(4), same source, accessed 2026-10-30. Critically, that subsection's "possession of a controlled substance" ground expressly excludes possession where "such controlled substance was obtained directly from or pursuant to a medical order issued by a practitioner legally authorized to prescribe while acting in the course of his or her professional practice" — § 76-1431(4)(c), same source, accessed 2026-10-30. Verify whether a § 71-24,104 "written recommendation" counts as such a "medical order" — a recommendation is not a prescription under Nebraska law on the texts opened, and no case located in this run decides the point. Do not present the exclusion as settled protection for medical patients.
  • Public-housing / housing-authority route (opened via search excerpt, § 71-15,139): a housing agency may terminate after 3 days' written notice without an administrative hearing for drug-related or violent criminal activity on the premises, including possession of a controlled substance by a resident or a person on the premises with the resident's consent if the resident knew or should have known — unless obtained directly from/pursuant to a medical order from an authorised practitioner — source: https://nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=71-15,139 — accessed 2026-10-30. Verify the full § 71-15,139 text and whether federally assisted housing applies federal drug rules in addition.
  • Smoking in a rental: a landlord may prohibit smoking (including cannabis smoke) by lease; the Clean Indoor Air Act (§12.5) covers indoor public/employment places, not private dwellings as such — lease terms, not the Act, are the usual route for a private-rental smoking ban. Verify against a specific lease / local ordinance for any given tenancy.
  • Cultivation in a rental: home cultivation is not authorised for patients at all (Run 1), so a tenant has no medical-cultivation right to assert against a landlord.

12.8 Gun ownership conflicts for patients — federal conflict, unresolved by Schedule III on the texts supplied

  • Federal prohibition (statute, via secondary summaries of the official text). 18 U.S.C. § 922(g)(3) prohibits a person who is an "unlawful user of or addicted to any controlled substance" from shipping, transporting, receiving or possessing firearms or ammunition — sources (secondary summaries of the statute): https://legalclarity.org/can-you-carry-a-gun-with-a-medical-marijuana-card/ — accessed 2026-10-30; https://fedlaws.org/marijuana-use-and-firearm-possession-under-federal-law/ — accessed 2026-10-30. ATF Form 4473 asks whether the buyer is an unlawful user of or addicted to marijuana or any other controlled substance, with a warning that marijuana use remains unlawful federally regardless of state legalisation — same sources, accessed 2026-10-30. ATF's 2011 open letter to federal firearms licensees stated there are "no exceptions in federal law for marijuana purportedly used for medicinal purposes, even if such use is sanctioned by State law" — same sources, accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Nebraska-specific application — conflict, not resolution:
  • Nebraska has no state statute located in this run that either strips a medical patient's state firearm rights or protects them. State firearm law is therefore not the conflict; the conflict is federal § 922(g)(3) vs state-law medical possession.
  • Schedule III wrinkle — Verify, do not resolve. The supplied federal baseline moves state-licensed medical marijuana to Schedule III from 28 April 2026. Section 922(g)(3) turns on "unlawful user," not on schedule alone, and the secondary record notes the Department of Justice has argued the ban should survive rescheduling, while federal appeals courts have limited it on Second Amendment grounds in some cases and the Supreme Court heard argument in early 2026 (U.S. v. Hemani as named in secondary coverage) — source: https://legalclarity.org/is-marijuana-legal-in-omaha-nebraska/ — accessed 2026-10-30. Whether a Nebraska qualified patient using state-licensed Schedule III medical product (once such product exists — none is sold yet) is an "unlawful user" is Verify. The 2026 ATF interim final rule redefining "unlawful user" as regular use over an extended period continuing into the present (secondary summary, Jan 2026) is also Verify against the Federal Register before quoting — source: https://fedlaws.org/marijuana-use-and-firearm-possession-under-federal-law/ — accessed 2026-10-30.
  • Practical consequence that IS settled on the sources opened: a Nebraska patient who uses marijuana (medical or otherwise) and answers Form 4473 untruthfully risks a separate federal false-statement offence, and a dealer who knows the buyer holds a medical card has, under the 2011 ATF letter, reasonable cause to believe the buyer is a prohibited person — same secondary sources, accessed 2026-10-30. Nebraska patients must not be told state medical law "protects" their federal firearm rights.
  • Nebraska set-aside firearm notice: § 29-2264(3)(c) requires a court granting a set-aside to notify the offender to consult an attorney about the effect, if any, on firearm possession under state or federal law (via § 29-2264 excerpt, §12.4) — source: https://nebraskalegislature.gov/laws/statutes.php?statute=29-2264 — accessed 2026-10-30.

12.9 Pet and veterinary products — NO Nebraska veterinary cannabis rule found; federal position is unapproved

  • NO RULE FOUND in Nebraska statute or Nebraska veterinary board rule located in this run authorising, prohibiting by name, or setting standards for CBD / cannabis products for animals. Do not import California's veterinary-discussion law or any other state's veterinary cannabis law into Nebraska.
  • Federal position (secondary, consistent across sources): FDA has not approved any CBD product for use in animals; companies may not market pet CBD products with unsubstantiated therapeutic claims, and FDA has issued warning letters on that basis — sources (secondary): https://livegoodforlife.com/product/cbd-oil-for-pets/ — accessed 2026-10-30; https://dogsanddreams.se/2025/05/18/legal-aspects-of-cbd-for-dogs-precisely-what-you-should-know/ — accessed 2026-10-30. Veterinarians cannot prescribe CBD as an FDA-approved animal drug because none exists — same sources, accessed 2026-10-30. Verify against FDA's current animal-CBD page before republication; FDA pages were not opened directly in this run.
  • Nebraska overlay (inference flagged, not law): a hemp-derived pet product that meets Nebraska's hemp definition (≤0.3% delta-9, Run 1) is hemp, not marijuana, under state law — but a product marketed as a drug / with disease claims, or containing over-limit THC, falls under the Pure Food Act / drug framework and AG enforcement posture in Run 1. A pet product containing marijuana-derived THC given to an animal is not protected by Initiative 437, which protects qualified patients (humans) — that limitation is a reading of § 71-24,104/105, Verify against any DHHS/NDA guidance (none located).
  • Safety note (non-law context, secondary): THC is toxic to pets; any pet CBD product should be verified by certificate of analysis for near-zero THC — source: https://livegoodforlife.com/product/cbd-oil-for-pets/ — accessed 2026-10-30.

12.10 Medical cannabis in schools — NO school-administration statute found

  • NO RULE FOUND: no Nebraska statute, regulation or DHHS guidance located in this run authorises, requires, or regulates possession, storage or administration of medical cannabis by school personnel, parents/guardians or students on school property. Initiative 437 protects a qualified patient's use/possession/acquisition generally (§ 71-24,105) but contains no school provision, no school-policy mandate, and no exception for school grounds.
  • What schools CAN still do on the texts opened: general school drug policies, the Clean Indoor Air Act's indoor-smoking ban (§12.5), and federal drug-free-schools funding conditions all continue to apply; a school is not required by Initiative 437 to permit on-campus use, store cannabis, or administer it. The dead LB 677 draft contained a detailed school/facility provision (no smoking, no vapour except aerosol inhaler, consumption only in a place specified by the school, no unreasonable limitation unless federal funding/licence at risk) — that was a proposal in a dead bill, NOT law, and is noted here only so the compiler does not mistake it for current law — source (LB 677 introduced text): https://legiscan.com/NE/text/LB677/id/3072020/Nebraska-2025-LB677-Introduced.pdf — accessed 2026-10-30. The dead LB 474 (2021) committee language was similar — also not law.
  • Minors as patients: a minor can be a qualified patient with a practitioner recommendation + parent/guardian written permission (§ 71-24,104, Run 1), and a parent/guardian or designated facility/agency can be a caregiver — but that caregiver status does not, on any text located, create a right to administer cannabis at school or require a school to accommodate it. Verify against any individual school district policy for a specific student; district policies were not swept in this run.
  • Health education (context, not a cannabis-use rule): Neb. Rev. Stat. § 79-712 requires comprehensive health education including drug use/misuse/abuse instruction, with special emphasis on commonly abused drugs including marijuana — source (secondary reproduction): https://www.lawserver.com/law/state/nebraska/ne-statutes/nebraska_statutes_79-712 — accessed 2026-10-30. Verify against the official statute page.

RESEARCH NOTES — Combined, Runs 1 and 2 (research date 30 October 2026)

N1. What this combined file is

Run 1 (Parts 0–6) and Run 2 (Parts 7–12) are combined here in part order, with no part dropped and no Run 1 figure silently overwritten. Where Run 2 resolved a Run 1 Verify flag, the resolution is stated below and the Run 1 section itself is left as written for auditability, except that the cover reconciled counts (above) carry the resolved figures. The 3 November 2026 election had not yet been held on 30 October 2026, so no 2026 result appears anywhere in this file. The federal hemp dates 12 November and 11 December 2026, and the USDA DEA-lab date 31 December 2026, are future dates at the research date, not current law in Nebraska.

N2. Items Run 2 RESOLVED (Run 1 Verify flags closed)

  • Certified 2024 ballot counts — RESOLVED (Part 9, Secretary of State official canvass book, pp. 76–77, opened directly): Initiative 437: For 637,126 / Against 259,643 / Total 896,769 = 71.05%. Initiative 438: For 600,481 / Against 291,867 / Total 892,348 = 67.29%. Run 1's rounded ~71% / ~67% figures are confirmed.
  • Cultivator plant cap — RESOLVED for draft text (Part 11, Title 238 §014.06): 1,250 flowering plants at one time. Final published Title 238 text still Verify.
  • Dispensary sale cap — RESOLVED for draft text (Part 11, §013.07): 30-day supply, 5 g Total THC per 90 days, no sale within 30 days of filling an order. Run 1's "30 vs 90 days" variant is resolved in favour of 90 days for the draft text; final text Verify.
  • Residency majority — RESOLVED for draft text (Part 11): "more than 50%" Nebraska-resident citizens (4 years). Run 1's 50% vs 51% conflict is resolved to "more than 50%" for the draft text; final text Verify.
  • Hemp plan status — CONFIRMED (Parts 5, 7): Nebraska is NOT a USDA-approved state-plan state today. USDA AMS list (17 Feb 2026) lists Nebraska as "USDA Hemp Producer License". Nebraska had an approved plan (Jan 2020); LB 262 (2024) transferred cultivation licensing to USDA from 1 Jan 2025 and repealed the NDA licensing sections.
  • Cultivator count 2 vs 4 — RESOLVED as timing (Runs 1 and 2): 2 offered 7 Oct 2025, 4th offered 16 Mar 2026; all four confirmed in existence by 11 May 2026. Cap 4 is full.
  • LB 316 status — RESOLVED (Run 1, Legislature history opened directly): indefinitely postponed 17 Apr 2026. It is not law. Claims that LB 316 was enacted are rejected.
  • 2020 ballot ruling — CONFIRMED (Part 9): State ex rel. Wagner v. Evnen, 307 Neb. 142, 948 N.W.2d 244, No. S-20-623, filed 10 Sept 2020 (5–2, single-subject / logrolling, withheld from the 3 Nov 2020 ballot).
  • Nebraska v. Colorado — CONFIRMED in outline (Part 10): motion for leave filed Dec 2014; denied without explanation 21 Mar 2016 (Thomas/Alito dissenting); denial is not a holding that Colorado's scheme is lawful. Docket number Verify.

CONFLICTS WITH RUN 1 — NONE ADOPTED WITHOUT FLAG

  • Run 1: "no out-of-state reciprocity stated in the Act" — matched: no reciprocity provision located in this run either; the § 71-24,104 practitioner definition (licensed in any state) is a practitioner-licensing point, not a patient-card reciprocity rule, and is flagged Verify rather than presented as reciprocity.
  • Run 1: "0 dispensaries, 0 manufacturers" (state-licensed) — matched: Omaha tribal activity is tribal-law activity, not state licensing; the tribal store is in any event not confirmed open by 30 Oct 2026.
  • Run 1: AG hemp suits "15 in AG release / 16 later secondary" — not re-litigated here; no new AG tribal/interstate suit located.
  • Run 1: hemp state programme ended 31 Dec 2024 / USDA from 1 Jan 2025 — matched and extended: tribal USDA plans (Winnebago, Santee Sioux, Omaha, Iowa Tribe) are separate from the former state programme and remain Approved Plans on the 17 Feb 2026 USDA list.
  • One secondary source in this run (cannabisnebraska.org) describes the medical programme as "blocked" and states no physician has publicly registered — that characterisation is not adopted: Run 1's position is that the programme is in law but not operational for sales, with no registry existing to register in. The compiler should prefer Run 1's framing.
